TARIFF REFORM AND

EFFICIENCY. EXPORTS OF BOOTS AND SHOES.

The progress of the international race to the right to the title of the largest i exporting country of boots and shoes, s shown in the figures below. Britain and America are far to the front; all other countries are out of it and may be neglected. Our own country had the start, but the progress was slow. In the early years of our record America overhauled us hand over hand, and in 1907 was ahead to the extent of £389,000. The next year we were neck and neck, during the last three years we have again drawn to the front, and in 1911 led by £491,000. Relative progress principally depends upon efficiency in manufacture, and in this machinery plays a large part. The improved machinery introduced into .boot manufacturing has been mostly of American origin. Naturally its effect was first experi- enced in that country,and American progress has been mainly due to this factor. The makers and inventors of any improved machinery abroad, once their home market is supplied, turned their attention to the export market. The Free Trade—i.e the English—market, is naturally the first to be exploited. We get the first offer of the products of the world's brains. It is only after the English demand has been satisfied, or as in this case the American machines are made in England, that exporters turn their attention to countries where the boot manufacturer has to pay the cost plus the tariff. It took a few years before our boot factories were fully equipped, and adapted themselves to the new conditions. This done the result became apparent in increased exports, and diminished imports. For a time we went forward abreast, and other countries, such as France and Germany, were left farther in the rear. During the last few years we have taken a definite lead, leaving to America second place. This can fairly be attributed to the difference of the fiscal policy We have no handicap, we can buy our raw materials and our machines at the lowest price the world can produce, we have the -widest free selection. Roughly speaking half of our boot manufacturers' raw material is drawn from abroad, and no tax however high on imported boots would compensate for a tax, however low, on leather. The propor- tion of imported boots to the number we manufacture is about 2 per cent, while, as we have said, the proportion of leather is 50 per cent. We have heard a proposal to put 71/2 per cent. duty on leather, and 15 per cent. on boots. As far as the boot manufac- turer is concerned he would be far worse off under such an arrangement than at present. Since the introduction of the machinery named, the imports of boots have been steadily diminishing. What we still import are mainly specialities which the British manufacturer does not care to touch. When we get facts of this sort we realise how wide of the mark are the ideas taught and implied in Tariff Reform Exhibitions, where samples of imported boots are shown to teach how the workers are deprived of labour which would gain them a livelihood. At these Exhibitions the main idea seems to be that we should work more. The main cry is to "make work," as though work were an end in itself. If the workers think their chief good is to be found in doing more work they ought to go for taxing imports; but if their aim be to do less work, and at the same time have more welfare, they should stand by free imports. If the difference between making a special class of boot in this country compared with the cost of the imported article were 10 per cent. in labour cost, then a 10 per cent. duty might keep the imported boots out. If the cost to consumer is not to go up lOper cent., then the wolikers could solve the difficulty by working eleven hours for the same pay instead of the ten they work on the boots for exports which effect the exchange. The object of the Tariff Reformers would then be achieved. More work would be called for, but there would be no increase in the value of the product. If on the other hand the extra cost is handed on to the buyer then he must either go without the boots (thereby stopping the demand) or he must go without something he at present buys, equally diminishing the labour demand. The Tariff Reformers display a strange want of appreciation of the difference between work and its result, the product. The two things are treated as synonymous and con- sidered (if they are considered at all) as if they had a fixed relationship. Their pro- posal is by means of a duty to bribe men into making for themselves at a greater cost, or —what is the same thing—by a greater expenditure of labour, the things they are in the habit of obtaining at less cost by a process of exchange. In brief PROTECTION IS A DEVICE FOR CREATING WASTEFUL LABOUR. By this plan we doubly cripple our exports, first, by raising our cost, and second by blocking the process of exchange, stopping the inward and thereby stopping the outward. But it should never be lost sight of that the fiscal problem does not cover the whole ground. It is only one of tho factors. And the future of the boot manufacturing, andl every other trade, depends largely upon the I enterprise, adaptability, skill, and energy of those engaged in such trade. These will go I far to determine whether we are able in the future to hold, and to increase, our lead in the international race of boot manufacturing. Freedom of trade does not take the place of these, but it makes every other factor more effective in producing general welfare. EXPORTS OF BOOTS AND SHOES.

SUNDAY MUSKETRY. The Committee of the Lord's-day Observance Society have received the following from the Secretary of State for War in reply to a protest:—" With regard to the use of the rifle range at Crowden I am to explain that the Council, after full consideration of the question of Sunday rifle shooting in all its aspects, have decided that Musketry Practices may be carried out on Sundays' provided that all attendance at such practices is purely voluntary, and that the amenities of the locality in which the practice takes place are inot interfered with. As it does not appear that the rifle shooting which took place at Crow den infringed these conditions, the Council are unable to take any action in the matter." The Society replied expressing their great regret that the Army Council should have resolved to takes step which in the past history of our country had had auch disastrous results for both Church and State.

BANKERS AND CONSOLS.

NATIONAL PROVINCIAL BANK OF ENGLAND, _LIMITED. The annual general meeting was held on Thursday' 25th inst. at 15, Bishopegate, London, E.C. Mr. M. tr• Vitzgerald, who presided, said that when they met year ego they were looking forward to a further development of the promising commercial conditiors which then prevailed. The tide had begun to flow, and it was anticipated that, in the absence of untoward events, the flow might continue with gathering force. These anticipatiens had not been disappointed. In spite of a very grave political struggle at home and of strikes and labour disputes unusual in number and importance—in the face of wars and rumours of wars abroad, the tide had gone on steadily rising, till the volume of trade in 1911 bad been larger than over before recorded. Agriculture alone, perhap,, had had something of a set-back generally, owing to the great drought of the past summer, and it was to be feared that its troubles might not 3 et be over, should we have a prolonged cold spell. On the other hand, manufac- turing industries of all kinds had been more or less prosperous, as was evidenced by the fact that the per- centage of unemployment during the year had been the lowest 30t recorded. Coming to their oen business, the directors hoped that the shareholders would con- sider the figures presented as satisfactory. It was true that the totals on both sides were lees than for the previous year, but it might be explained that in December 1910, they held large temporary deposits, and if these were deducted their normal business again showed a substantial increase, the total deposits on 31st December, 1911, being £3,349,974 end the advances £2,728,808 more than at 31st December 1909. The principal changes in the figures shown in the balance sheet as compared with a year ago were a reduction of £2,251,234 in the total deposits, of £2,314,384 in the bills discounted and loans, and of £1,199,968 in investments in Government and other gilt-edged securities ; whilst the cash and money with brokers at call and short notice showed an increase of £1,211,260. The year 1911 Lad, however, not been a particularly good one for banking proflte, for, though there had been throughout a constant damand for money, rates generally had been lower than in 1910. The disappointing feature was the continued deprecia- tion in their securities, which swallowed up £116,000. Various plans had been discussed in the Press and else- where for" rehabilitating Consols." as the phrase %mat. Some demand might be created by the encouragement of small investors through the pcst office, and by simplifying transfer,but the total dealt with in this way would hardly be large enough to influence prices. The only solid plan would perhaps be the restoration of the new sinking fund, and this was, it was to be feared, Utopian, having regard to the way in which in recent years the fund had been raided by both political partiea Certainly there were at present no signs of any reduc- ticn in the national expenditure, unless we were to take as such the acceptance by the Government of the proposed new committee on the estimates. After all, this was only machinery, and machinery that would be useless without the driving power behind it of a strong public opiniou. Meanwhile, huge additions were being made to the expenditure, and made too to the permanent obligations of the State, which had to be met in years good and bad. A yearly budget of £200,000,000 was within sight, and it was not too much to say that sober- minded men of all political opinions looked with the gravest concern and anxiety to the financial future of the country. The report was unanimoudy adopted.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LONDON CITY AND MIDLAND BANK, LTD.

The annual general meeting of shareholders of the Landon City and Midland Bank, Ltd., was held on Friday last at the Cannon-street Hotel. London, E.C., Sir Edward Holden, Bart., (chairman) presiaing. He said that the year 1911 had been the roost difficult, and he thought he might say had caused the greatest anxiety of all the years of the last decade. There had been troubles in Mexico' Persia, Tripoli, Morocco and had Chins, and they had had financial and increasing labour troubles at home. Shareholders were aware that, the Bank tad written off this year from their reserve funds £200,C00 to meet depreciation in securities. The depreciation was less than this sum, but having regard to the continued fall in securities they considered it a wise policy to make an additional provision. Their surplus profits for the year had been very satisfactory indeed and quite sufficient to more than write off the depreciatiou in investments. The depreciation of Consols was engaging so much attention at present that it was worth while to devote a little time to the consideration of that subject. Speaking metaphorically, a very interesting struggle to attract investors was always taking place between Consols on the one hand and Bills of Exchange (the representatives of the money market) on the other. The Bill of Exchange was becoming so popular that even securities were being turned into short bills. Prcceeding, Sir Edward detailed factors which had made Consols less attractive, , the late Lord Goschen's reduction of the interest from 3 per cent to 22 per cent., the further reduction to 23 per cent. in 1903, the deduction of 3d. in the £ for income tax in later years instead of 6d. in the £ as in former years, and the passing of the *este° Act in 1193. When Consols, he went on to say, had given an appreciable tatter rate than Bills then Consols had goer) up. When Bills had given an appreciably higher rate, then Consols had gone down, and having confirmed this:statement with detailed figures he asked what were the conclusions they were to draw from the facts he had set forth ? It Ras quite evident that if Consols returned appreciably less than Bills, the inducement would be for the financier to come out of Consols and go into Bills—then Consols would go dowu. It was equally evident that if Consols returned appreciably more than Bills, the inducement would be for the financier to Come out of Bills and go into Consols; then Consols would go up. The State was making large profits in consequence of the legislation of the late Lord Goad:len. Sir William Harcourt and Mr. Chamberlain in relation to Consols, and holders of Consols were making large losses. The State was further making large profits in continuing the income tax at, id. in the £, and holders were making corresponding losses. While the money market as represented by bills of exchange, was relatively so dear, the creation of sinking funds would not materially assist the price, because if more buyers, or one additional large buyer, came into the market, and this bad the desired effect of raising the price the result would be to still further reduce the return to the investor. This would bring in fresh sellers and the price would go back. This had been exemplified by the price of Consols during the year. The redemption had been very large, amounting it was said, to about 10 millions, yet Consols had , gone down. While he approved of the increase of Sinking Fund, yet, in his opiaion, the only effectual course that could be adopted for putting up the price of Consols against the Bill, and bringing in the large financial buyers, was to increase the value of Consols as an investment. Artificial measures such at issuing small bonds would only affect the position in the same way that an increase of the Sinking Fund would affect it—that is to say, the price might be raised temporarily, hut only to fall back on the reduced yield. He agreed with the statement that had been made " that it was a delusion to suppose you can raise the price of Consols beyond the level of their worth as an investment." He did not think any of the schemes proposed would be successful in raising and maintaining the price of Consols, except the one %hich advocated that the income tax should be taken off Consols. This would raise the value of Consols as an investmen,, because it would have the effect of increasing the return to the purchaser at 773 by 3s. 10d. per cent,, and to the pu•chaser at 100 by 213.11 d. per cent. The question then arose why should Consols he exempted from income tax any more than any other security? The answer I was that Consols had a claim before all other securi ties, because the State in its dealings with the holders of Consols had made an annual profit of about £3 millions I sterling since the rate was reduced to 23 per cent., and it was making an additional profit of 23 per cent. Finally the Chairman surveyed exhaustively the finances of the principal countries, observing that they must came to the conclusion that every country was I expanding and would require more credit. The exten- sion of credit meant increased prices. The increase of credit should bear a certain proportion to the increase of that portion of gold which was used as a basis of credit. If credit increased for any length of time in disregard to gold then a collapse must take place and prices would go down. There was nothing in his opinion to warrant them in believing that in the coming year they would have cheap money, having analysed the financial results of the Bank's business during the period under review, he said that their business was steadily on the increase, and he could give them the assurance that the Bank bad never been in a sounder or more progressive position than it was to-day. He moved the adoption of the report, which was unanimously carried. •

DISEASE SPREAD TO WORKHOUSE FROM CATS.

The cats in the Bolton workhouse were drowned by order of the Board of Guardians after a meeting on Wednesday. For some time ringworm bits been very prevalent among the children in the cottage homes at the workhouse, and an extra staff has had to be appointed to nurse the suffers. To enable the resident medical officer to diagnose the disease properly an X-rays apparatus was bought, and with It he found that the ringworm was of animal origin. Suspicion fastened upon the workhouse cats, and their destruction was accordingly ordered.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

" The Babes In the Wood."

There are few people to whom a good pantomime does not appeal but even those who consider they have no taste for this form of entertainment cannot resist the excellent fare provided by Mr. Harry Elliston and Company in "The Babes in the Wood," which has attracted large audiences to the Hippodrome during the week. There is an abundance of good song and humour and indeed the whole production is one of the best that has visited the Hippodrome for acme time. The staging is perfect and the dresses are magnificent, One is taken back to the atenice of childhood by the exploits of Robin Hood and his cheerful band cf outlaws. Everyone knows the E tory of how the baron plots with the two rnffitits to do away with the babes. The incident in the abduction of the babes from the schoolroom, and their transference to the wilds of Sherwood Fore,t, causes mars of] laughter. In the forest there is the mock combat between the ruffians, the simplicity of which, one could not fail to appre- ciate. The house is kept in a continual roar of laughter. The schoolroom scene and the jokes cracked by the" scholars " at the expense of the dame and the antics of the artistes proved quite a new and pleasing feature. 'the best scene of all is undoubtedly the last, where all the company assemble in the electric palace. The effect f the btillimat dresses and scenery is enchanting. , Miss Maude Osborne is a capital Robin Hood.' and she carried the part with much distinction. As Maid Marion, Miss Irene May is a great favourite with the audiences. Mr. Robb Wilton as the Baron proves ta be a comedian of high standard. In one part especially be was most conspicuous with his "dry " humour. Full of witty sayings and a pleasing style he brings down the house on many cccasions. August and September, the ever popular comedy couple, take the parts of the ruffians and as usual are most comical and entertaining. Where would a pantomime be without a good "dame?' This difficult but highly amusing pal t is silo ted to Mr. Elliston who displayed a born adaptability to anything humoreus. Misses Marie May and Clarice Handel also share in the honour. Other contributors to the success and the production are : The Four whose harmonising items are a treat. Miss Nance Grocutt, a singer with an exceptionally sweet and rich voice, and Miss Maud St. John as Lallie. The Sisters Gaye give a clever exhibition of dancing also, do the Five Yorkshire Lasses.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE BARBARITY OF STRIKES."

President of Chamber of Commerce and Picketing.

Indust?lel unrest was the chief topic, referred to by Mr R T Fairclough in his Presidential address at the annual meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Com- merce on Monday. Amongst those present at the meeting were :—Mr F Aylmer Frost, Alderman Smethurst, Mr J C Coppock, Mr H Kingdou, Mr II Grosvenor, Mr Segir Owert, Mr 0 Lofthouse, Mr H G Roberts, Mr .3 Richardson jun., Mr J Brandreth, and Alderman Bennett (secretary). Mr. FAincLouou pointed out that trade for 1911 had been good, as was shown by the exports and imports, the railway returns, and the statistics of unem- ployment, the proportion of members of trade unions unemployed being 3 per cent against 4.7 per cent the previous year. Those figures would have beea still bet:er but for the strikes. Oaeaspect of the industrial disputes which had not affected previous trade disputes was that the members of the trade unions had

REFUSED TO FOLLOW TIIE ADVICE or be bound by the agreements made by their leaders, and that would Seriously interfere a ith arbitration pro- ceedings and must be detrimental to the trades unions. (Hear, hear.) There was a short strike of railway workers' which hampered all trades, and which was dest ned to injure our future trade, but it had awakeued public opinion to the methods of trade unionism, the barbarity of strikes

THE GENERAL TYRANNY OF PICKETING, and the losses iuflicted on the general trade of the country. Those who were responsible disregarded the interests of the people by the stoppage of food supplies and precluded works from obtaining and delivering materials essential to the maintenance of their industries. Despite this the Labour leaders in the House of Commons actually condemned the Government for interlering with the strike proceed- ings and for employing troops to safeguard the nation. The Government promised to bring forth a Bill provid- ing that an increase of the cost of labour on the railways should justify them in increasing their charges and that would mean an extra burden upon trade. Those charges might be met by those who did a home trade by

RAISING PRICES ALL ROUND, but that could not be done with our foreign trade with- out loosing a large portion, as it would be very seriously handicapped, and a trade once lost was most difficult to regain. That would have a tendency to make more unemplosed labour in this country thin ever The increased charges upon foreign trade would be : increase of wages in the works ; increase of municipal rates principally through education; increase of cost in the raw materials ' • increase of cost in fuel through wages, and the extra cost through the Eight Hours Act ; increase of railway charges and increase through the Insurance Act. A loss of their foreign trade would react upon their home trade. Evidence had lately accumulated showing the difficulty experienced in raising capital for our home industries and that must influence the prosperity of the working classrs, for without surplus capital there could not be adequate expansion for manufacturers. A computation taken from the " Engineers' Journal " of December 10th, 1911, stated upon reliable data that investments sent abroad amounted at the end of the previous year to £3,722,000,000 sterling, and that the inci ease over a period of nurteen years was at the rate of £91,500,000 per annum ; and at the present time no less than £178,000,b00 was received in this country without British labour in any way participating in its creation. Such facts should be carefully studied and ought to be understood by those who represented capital and labour- Unfortunately the disputes In the tabour market were not ended, especially in the coal and cotton trades. With regard t the coming sear, it was not safe to prophesy, but he believed they might expect a good steady trade provided they had no more setbacks in the way of industrial warfare. The 35th annual report stated that the Alliance Box Co Ltd, the Castle Rubber Co Ltd, and the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co Ltd had joined the Chamber during the year. Mr. Fairclough having intimated his intention of retiring Mr F A Frost was elected president for the year. CIVILISATION AND DIET.

We smile at the accounts given of the gormandising powers of the natives of Arcfc regions and of various savage tribes, but tome of our own habits in eating and drinking are scarcely less preposterous. Look at a modern dinner, beginning with soup, followed by fish, meat, poultry, gime, sweets and dessert, to say nothing of numerous liquors consumed by the luxurious diner. All of which is a civilised form of gluttony accepted by custom. The rich who enjoy such meals pay the penalty with such horrors as digestive troubles, gout, and other forms of acute suffering. Now, the poor and middle-classes are, in reality, ranch better off in so far as they can enjoy a sufficiency of plain natural food, with the nuttiment and savouriness which Nature provides at a low cost. Instead of weird combinations on the table, it is much better from every point of view to enjoy sound wholesome fare such as Currants, Why Currants? says the reader. Simply because Vie little Greek Currant is a perfect food. it is ths work of Na.ure, and her special product for the feed- ing of mankind. Of late years leading medical men have urged the freer use of Currants in the daily diet. Currants are not the food for occasions, they are meant for constant use. There is no other food which can be compared with the Currant for pure nutritive force and economy combined. Just think of it—nearly 80 per cent, pure nourishment, and every particle of it easily diges'eo ; then it is likewise delightfully savoury. Si the Currant is unique, for notwithstand- ing its surptssing excellence, it is always cheap.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
i _ IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR FRI-SH READERS. -'WARD CARSON AND

SIR ED

TURNCOATS. It is difficult to recall a case_ during recent years in which a prominent politician has ,been so conclusively convicted of misstate- ments as has been Sir Edward Carson in regard to his account of the circumstances under which he resigned his membership of the National Liberal Club. It will be remembered that when addressing a meet- ing in the Free Trade Hall, Manchester, quite recently, he referred to Mr. Winston Churchill and Mr. T. W. Russell as turncoats, whereupon Mr. Hamar Greenwood, M.P., wrote to the " Times " suggesting that the charge did not lie well in the mouth of a Tory politician who had once been a Liberal and a member of the National Liberal Club. To this the rebel in full bottomed wig replied that while it was true that he had been a Liberal in former times, he had telegraphed his resignation from the Club on the day the first Home Rule Bill was introduced. Take careful note of the day. No doubt in his craftiness the disloyal loyalist thought that ended the matter. But an exami- dation of the Club records has been made and what is the result? The club records show this very interesting fact that so far from resigning on the day the first Home Rule Bill was introduced, Sir Edward Carson was not a member at all at that time, but was elected for the first time nearly two months later than Mr. Gladstone's introduction of the Home Rule Bill, that is on June 2nd, 1886. His membership lasted nearly a year and a half, resignation taking place, as proved by the records, on October 21st, 1887. As older Nationalists will remember this latter date has a special significance of its own, for it was just about that time that Mr. Carson, as he then was, began to find remunerative employment as prosecuting counsel under the Coercion Act. In these circumstances perhaps it is scarcely necessary to offer any comment on the turning of the coat.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. BONAR LAW AND THE

ORANGE MEN.

Butthis is no more surprising to the average Nationalist or rightly informed Englishman than is the cheap "wind and blather" emanating from the Orange Camp to-day about its loyalty. Mr. Bonar Law, speaking according to instructions we should say, rather than from accurate historical know- ledge, said at the Albert Hall meeting last week that "the loyal minority, in good report and bad report,have stood by us in the past." Mr. Law is new to his task, but he must certainly be more careful, for no more amazing travesty of the history of the whole period from the Union down to our own day can be imagined. The Orange faction endeavoured to alter the succession of the Throne by setting aside Queen Vicoria and placing the crown on the head of her uncle Ernest, Kin°. of Hanover, a man of the worst character, but who was if you please Grand Master of the Orange Lodge, and whose portrait, an ironic relic of Ascendancy days, is still preserved in the Mansion House, Dublin. The plot to change the succession to the 'I hrone in the interests of an Orange Grand Master is only in keeping with the loyalty of the loyal minority, who, so far from being, in Mr. Law's inflated panegyric,"men who have stood by us in good report and bad report," have been men who have been ready to threaten "us, " to keep to Mr. Law's word, with civil war whenever any of the privileges of a common citizenship was extended to the mass of their fellow countrymen. These " loyalists " as they style themselves, threatened civil war in 1829 because the freedom conferred on Catholics of admission to Parliament was a fatal attack on "the Constitution of 1688 " ! The rhymes that were on the lips of the " loyalists " of that day would make the hair stand on the head of any rational subject of the Crown to-day. The civil war threats were renewed on the introduction of the Church Temporal- ities Bill of 1833, on the occasion of the Municipal Reform Act, and, as is perhaps more widely known, during the Irish Protestant Church Disestablishment agita- tion. One " loyalist " orator during that agitation declared at Ballybay " We shall reject the ironhoofs of England and get rid of the Papacy at the same time—driving two serpents out of the island at once." That is indeed a curious way to stand by England. At a later period, the Ballot Act was denounced as "a Popish measure." King Edward even, was publicly charged by the " loyalists" with being "in league with the Jesuits" at the passing of the Irish University Act. Any Unionist knows Mr. Balfour sympathises with the demand which the Irish people put forward for a University, his letter to an East Manchester constituent made that abundantly clear, but nevertheless we find the King of these realms charged with complicity of the vilest kind on the realisation of Mr. Balfour's own hopes. The same King was warned by his " loyal " subjects in the Belfast Orange Lodge that they would "make his Throne rock "—a hoaryshout of treason—if he allowed his niece to marry the King of Spain. A still more up-to-date " loyalist " orator who !need not be named declared a few weeks ago that if a Home Rule Bill framed by the elected repre- sentatives of the people of Great Britain passed into law the " loyalists " would start a movement for the immediate establishment of an Irish Republic. As we ventured to suggest, 'the new leader of the Unionists has a heavy task on hand just now, but really we think he should talk to the gentleman on whose information he re- lied in declaring that " the loyal minority in good report and in bad report have stood by us in the past."

SUICIDE AFTEIJA GUN MISHAP.

At the Exeter Inn, Barnstaple, on Wednesday even- ing, two men ware found shot. It is stated that the innkeeper, named George Worth, aged 50, was showing a double-barrelled breech-loading gun to a customer named Fred Blake, a labourer, when the weapon was accidentally discharged, and Blake was instantly killed. Worth, horrified at the mishap, went into a motor girage at the rear of the inn, and later was found dead with a gunshot wound in the forehead and his gun lying a few yards away. Mrs. Wcrth, who was a witness of the first shot, declares that the affair was a pure accident.

MUSTARD FOR romns. If mustard is a good thing for poultry, then now is the time to use it. Last sear I experimented with " Farrows' Poultry Mustard " with excellent results. Unlike nearly all other condiments mustard is not harmful to the fowls system, and I can testify to its very practical value as an aid to egg production. Another beneficial feature is its wonderful cheapness. One teaspoonful for every six birds is ample, so it is easy to estimate the cost, knowing that you can pur- chase poultty mustard for something like ls. per pound. And when bought in larger quantities this price is greatly reduced. Many of our leading present day writers and poultry breeders strongly recommend its use. They all say with one accord, that it not only increases the egg-yield but also keeps the fowls in wonderfully good condition, and further it does not affect the fertility of the eggs. As a matter of fact it aids in this direction. I can strongly relommend readers to add a little poultry mustard to the morn- 'ing's mash. When doing so mix the condiment into the poultry meal when dry. By so doing you get it evenly distributed. Care should be taken to use the mustard judiciously ; never getting beyond the tea- spoonful yer six lirds. You will be able to get it through our advertise: a.

SILVER GREY DORKINC;s.

This week's illustration eepicts a pair of silver grey dorkings. It is very difficult to conceive of more beautiful fowl than these, not only beautiful but extremely useful too. When off a good strain Dorking hens are good layers of large white-shelled eggs. And it is almost unnecessary for me to remind you of the culinary properties of the Dorking. There is no finer table fowl in the whole lit of pule bred poultry than the grand old English Dorking. I cannot understand why this good old breed should have diminished somewhat in popularity. History however, repeats itself even in the poultry world and I am confident that when the" whims " of the fancy have died down a little, we shall have breeders returnipg to the Dorking fowl. As far back as 1853 eggs from this breed were sold at from to £4 a dczen, whilst 2 2s. was almost a common price. In support of one's claim's of all that has been said in favour of the Dorking, let me quote you from Harrison Weirs' " Our Poultry, " wherein he gives a reprint of the following report taken from the " Derby Reporter," 1851." Those persons, and those only, who saw and studied Pen 160, at the Birmingham Show of 1854, can form an accurate idea of the size, quality, and beauty of a first-rate Dorking fowl. They were the birds of the exhibition, and before them the whole tribe of Spanish, Cochins,black, white,brown and buff, 'keled their ineffectual fires'. . . . Thirty-five pounds' weight of the most delicious meat under heaven were there enshrined in their beautiful bodies, and robed in plumage in which richness and grace struggled for ascendancy." It is gratifying to relate that we still have a few reliable breeders in whose hands the future of the Dorking fowl is safe IMr. A. C. Major, one of the largest breeders and exporters, says that as well as being good table fowls they are (with attention) good layers too. Pullets will frequently lay at 18 weeks old. The Dorking fowl is not a back-yarder. It is the ideal farmers' and cottagers' fowl, and when given a free and unfettered range they forage well.

OTHER COLOURS. Silvers ate not the only variety of Dorkings. We have the Dark or Coloured Dorkings, White, Red, Cuckoos and Rose-combed Dark. The latter are about the best layers of all. But I think the Silvers are the best table fowls of all. 1 have taken a fair amount of space in my discription of the Dorking fowl, and even yet not said all—by a long way that could be said in favour of this grand breed of fowl. They ought to take a far greater hold amongst farmers than they have hithet to done. To these in search of a good all-round utility fowl let me recommend the Dorking.

SUITABLE FIRST CROSSES. Last week I promised to give you a list of suitable first crosses both for egg-production at d table purposes. First cf all then let us deal with the "egg- machines." It goes without saying that these should be a first crces between two pure breeds less likely to spend much time in broodiness. The following crosses constitute all that is requisite in this direction. Houdan-Leghorn, Houdan-Minorca, Redcap-Croad Langshan, Ancona-Croad—Langshan, Houclan-Buff Orpington, and Boudan-White Orpington. These are all grand rnatings. for the production of layers. Many more could be quoted but none better. If you breed a succession of chicks from the two first named crosses in the month! of May June and July, you get layers for the early spring and summer seasons. If you breed a succession of chicks from the latter—which you will note are a first crosses between a heavy and light breed—in the months of February March and April, you rvitl get good winter laying stock. It should ba noted that in the latter crosses, the male bitd should be of the lighter bleed. I have always found that this method if mating tends more to fertility, than if you mate a heavy male bird to light hens.

TABLE CBCSSES. What is mcst needed in table fowls is size con- sistent with quali y, also fowls of a quick maturing tendarcy. You will find therefore that any of the following first crosses will answer to this purpose. The order in which they read are not neccessarily in order of merit, as so much. depends on convenience and accomodation of breeding operations. Bleeds vary according to conditions However I will name only some of the most useful and most hardy ;—Fa,verolles Dorking,Old English Game Buff and White Orping- ton,lndian Game Dorking, Indian Game —Faverolles, Indian Game--Brahma, Dorking—Langsban, Sussex. Dorking, Indian Game Sussex, and Old English Game Indian Game. Surely there is vatiety enough here for the most fastidious. It is also to be expected that my readers may well ask for just one cr two of the best to save bewilderment ; very well then supposing you only intend mating two pens. Then I should re- commend Indian Game,—Dorking and Indian Game —Faverolles. Yon may g on horn now on the lin< suggested last ueek breeding and rearing in earnest. Chickens from t!ese matings when reared and well fed fetch gcod prices in the early sprirg and summer markets.

HAIB WEITHEII HINTS

The hard weather .predicted in our first column has come to pass, and it is just as well to remind 3 ou of a few salient points as a consepuence. When you let off the broody hens for a meal cover the eggs with a piece of thick flannel or they will soon perish with the intense cold. Also collect all egos as soon as laid or they will soon deteriorate from similar causes. And when stored see that they are placed in a room free from ken draughts. If you have any chickens out shelter thEm in an out house if at all possible, but in any case keep them free from cold winds, only let them out when the days are something like fit. Empty all drinking vessels at night, refilling the following morning. Keep plenty of chaff in the scratching oheds, and the last thing at night scatter a few grains of corn therein, so that your stock are kept busy scratching and thus at exercise. Don't forget the grit for it is impossible to get it under natural conditions during frosty weather; likewise green food, therefore a turnip or mangold cut in halves is a good and nutritious substitute. This also is the weather when a few extra feeds of maize can be given with advan- tage to your fowls. Drop it as soon as the hard weather declines.

WARRINGTON FIELD CLUB.

LECTURE BY MR. E. E. LOWE.

" Recent Geological Research.-

A meeting of the Field Club was held on Eaturday evening, January 27th, in the Museum lecture room, Mr. A. J. Jolley, J.P., in the chair. Mr. E. E. Lowe, B.Sc. F.L.S., Curator of the Leicester Museum, gave a most interesting lecture on "Recent Geological Research." Dividing his lecture into two parts, (a) The history of Igneous Rocks, and (b) The evolution of flowering plants, Mr. Lowe first defined igneous rocks as those which had solidified from the molten condition, as contrasted with sedimentary rocks, which were produced by the consolidation of gravel, sand and mud, and with the metamorphio rooks, which wore se iimentary rocks, altered by heat and pressure, due to earth movements. The lecturer then took two common examples, granite and basalt, and showed that their obvious differences were of two kinds, (1) difference of texture, and (2) difference of constituents. The former was shown to be largely due to difference in the rate of cooling, and the latter, to differences in the underground magma-reservoirs, from which they had originated. The order in which the various mineral constituents of any rock crystalized out from the molten rook-magna, can be made out by examining a thin shoe of the rock under the micros- cope and the lecturer pointed out that this order was not that of infusibility. This somewhat unexpected phenomenon was shown to be inexplicable, on the , assumption talat the various constituents forming the rock-magma, were in a state of mutual solution. Mr. Lowe then turned to the fascinating problem of the evolution of the flowering plants, first describing a typical flower, and then contrasting there- with, the r eproduotive organs and processes seen in a common fern. lie thou proceeded proceeded to show how,by A series of discoveries,which might almost be described as romantic, among the plants of the coal measures and Jurassic periods, the various connecting links between the fern and the flowering plant, had been brought to light. The dis- covery that most of the se-called ferns ef the coat measures, were not ferns in the ordinary sense, but on the contrary, seed-bearing plants, and that many of the so called cycads of th 3 Jurassic age, were really flowering plants of a remarkable type, were shown to be the most important contributions wade by geology, to tho solution of the problem. In closing,"the lecturer cello/ attention to the more than extreme probability of tho evolution of the flower being closely correlated with the increase in abundance and variety of insect life. It was undoubtedly a case of adaptation to fertilisaticn try insects. At the close of the lecture, an interestiug discussion took place during which, on the proposition of Mr. Woodcock, seconded by Mr. F. Womerslsy, a very hearty vota of thanks was accorded to Mr. Lowe for his excellent lrcture. Added interest was given to the lecture from the fact that Mr. Lowe is a Warrington gentleman, hnd was at one time connected with both the Museum and the Field Club, so that his successful career is not only an honour to himself, but also matter of congratulation to the town and to the Club of which he was so active a member. The discussion on the lecture was ably carried on by Messrs. Jolley, Woodcock, F. Womersley, Thomas May, Scholefleld, Mouolleid, L. Geening, Dr. Manson, J. W. Jones, Dunlop and Charles Madeley. The services of Mr. J.J. Armstrong as lanternist were highly appreciate/.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ANGLING

ASSOCIATION (NORTHERN ANGLERS CENTRE), Angling last week end was a blank as far as the reports to hand go. Several anglers made the journey to the Gowey, but as was predicted in last week's issue, owing to the amount of snow water running into the streams, the day was at its very worst, members not having a stir all day. The Norton cards are now in Land and members wishing to give this stream a trial should call for their cards at the club house as there are sure to be special prizes given for this water, and old members recom- mend fishing with a small worm at this time of the year. Mr. Redford from head-quarters writes me that travelling cards will be sent down to the Warrington centre as early as possible. Will members who have a spare week end and are Norton way try our Acton Bridge length as we should like our reports to come from members, the information from members being more reliable than the reports from other sources.

With regard to the live baits question anglers may leave the matter in the bends of the Northern Anglers Officials who will do the beat for the Warrington centre

It is announced that it is the intention of the directors of the Isle of Men Steam Packet Company, Limited, to have built in readiness for 1913 a new steamer for the mail and prssenger service between the Isle of Man and the mainland.

The Examiner, Saturday, February, 3 1912.

THE ICE AT LYMM AND LATCHFO

firms that were almost unequalled in the land. As one who began a works in a humble way he was proud to see it developing in line with the other industries of the locality. He had watched the growth of their president's concern, and wherever wire was used, the name of ftylands stood for quality. (Applause.) Mr. J. FUME-SMITH responded. lie said \Veiling- ton's trade had been built upon such solid foundations that it had become a household word throughout the world. That pointed to the capability of the men at the head of affairs and also showed that the directors had adopted the best way of developing their industries by encouraging the commercial travellers. (Hear, hear). Referring to the number and diversity of the trades of Warrington, Mr. Fulton-Smith said the town was particularly well blessed in times of crisis which had such a serious effect in places which depended upon one staple industry. There was very little reason to com- plain of want of employment in Warrington. If a man was willing to work, there was no excuse for his being idle in a town like Warrington. Whatever might be his talents and qualifications he would have no difficulty in obtaining employment—and that of a permanent nature. (Applause). Mr. W. PETER RIM/OMS proposed "The U.K.C.T.A. the schools and the Benevolent Institution." Ile said be looked upon the association as one of the most com- plete and perfect organizations of its kind with which he was acquainted. At the magnificent schools at Pinner, it was making provision for the young genera- tion, to rear up a class of commercial travellers trained In the very best traditions of their craft. Then when they grew up they would be united by that spirit of Freemasonry, that common bond of friendship which it was the purpose of the association to cement. He understood that an effort was being made to affiliate their British association with similiar associationa in other countries. Ile attributed the highest political importance to a movement of that kind. Ile never heard of international intercourse of trades or of societies without a feeling of profound satisfaction. Anything veld& tended to craw the people together in a manner which enabled them to understand one another must help to knock off those edges and corners, those fruitful sources of misunder- standing which existed between nations. Ile was con- vinced that the constantly increasing intimacy In the relations of the business men of England, Germany and the United States must tend to create better feelings between the peoples. (Applause). Mr. FRED Covstr, General Secretary, responded. He said the association was making excellent progress. The membership was keeping up, the work they were doing was ever extending in usefulness and in benefit to the commercial traveller and the fact that in an association consisting of 10,000 or 12,000 individualists the members were willing to skis minor diffences and work enthusiastically for the general policy of the association, was a proof of the soundness, justice and rightness of the lines upon which they were pro- gressing. He was glad to say they were receiviog excellent support from publio men of every type and of every political party and creed in the movement for a federation of the commercial travellers of the world. Every-right-thinking and far-seeing man would support the contention that federetions of that kind must have a profound effect upon the political relationships of great countries. The relationship between England and Germany recently were very strained but they were glad the tension had passed away and that there was a prospect of a more complete understanding in the future. Mr. Coysh showed how the influence of the Commercial Travellers' Association was exercised on the side of peace and goodwill, a letter being sent to a vice-president of the commercial travellers in Berlin and printed in almost every newspaper in the German Empire. He had at his office a bundle of letters and Press extracts expressing in the most kindly and friendly terms the appreciation of the people in Germany who read the letter published on behalf of the association, expressing the hope that the difficulties between the two nations might be speedily adjusted. Without being egotistical, he claimed that that letter, having the full weight and authority of the association behind it, must have had a great effect amongst the commercial classes in Germany. (Hear, henr.) He believed an international federation of travellers would bring about good results on the relations of nations which meddling politicians would in many cases he totally unable to undo. The association teas never in a sounder or more effective position for doing good work for the commercial travellers than it occupied to-day. In every department of their work—whether legal, benevolent, or insurance—everything was going on smoothly. That was due to the fact that they were receiving from every branch the same loyalty and help that they had received In such full measure from the Warrington branch ever since they came into existence. Referring to the Com- mercial Travellers' Schools, to which the association contribu'ed largely, Mr. Coysh said there was no publio school in the country that turned out its scholars more efficiently within the scope which it ret out to deal with. There was no work in connection with their community in which they could engage with so much satisfaction, knowing that every penny they gave produced the most satisfectory results. It was a singular thing that commercial travelling was the occupation to which fewest of the boys who passed through the schools 'devoted their attention in after-life. Whether that was due to the" horrible example" set by their fathers he did not know. (Laughter). The annual cost of tbe schools was £14,000, the amount per child working out at £37. With regard to the benevolent fund, the amount expended every year on pensions to the widows of members and in grants to members who had been unfortunate, was praotically the same as the annual expenditure On the schools. It was impossible to measure the comfort, happiness and softening of the hardness of circumstances which such beneficent work represented. An interesting fact alluded to by Mr. Coysh was that the penny collection taken at the hotels by means of boxes realised £5,000 a year. Whilst that result was very gratifyiag he would like to see more annual subscribers to the funds from the rank and file of commercial travellers.

Other toasts honoured were : "The Warrington and District branch," proposed by Mr. J. Sumner (Wigan), acknowledged by Mr. W. G. Hayes (chairman) ; ' The President," proposed by Mr. W. H. Owen •, " The Visitors," proposed by Mr. 11. Mercer, responded to by Mr. G. Bo man; "The Artistes," proposed by Mr. W. L. Walker, acknowledged by Mr. J. Rabbitt. An admirable musicel programme was submitted. Mr J Rabbitt sang "The Trumpeter " and" Dear Heart "in his usual soulful and artistic manner. Mr J B Poole was highly successful in " Song of the bow " and" When bright eyes glance," in which his excellent voice found full scope. Humorous songs by Mr J Sumner and Mr J Ashley proved very popular. The various accompaniments were very tastefully played by Mr. Tom Taylor.

RD

COMMERCIAL TRAVELLERS.

ANNUAL DINNER AT WARRINGTON.

A Peace Movement.

The Warrington branch of the United -Kingdom Commercial Travellers' Association continues to make good headway and the sixth annual dinner, which was held at the Lion Hotel on Saturday evening, was a highly successful and enjoyable function. Members and friends assembled in good force under the chair- manship of Mr. W. Peter Rylands (president of the branch). At the cross-table were the Mayor (Coun- cillor Dr. Joseph), the Mayor of Widnes (Alderman F Neil), Councillor J T Locker, Mr J Fulton-Smith, Mr Fred Coysh, (general secretary), :Mr. W G Hayes (chairman of the branch), Mr II Mercer I (ex-chairman), Mr A J Allen (hon secretary), and Mr 1 W L Walker (hon assistant secretary). After dinner, I for which Mr. John Lee catered admirably, an interesting programme of speeches and music was gone 1 through. . . . .

It was announced that letters regretting inability to attend had been received from Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., Mr. A. H. Crosfield, Councillor T. S. Steel, Mr. F. Mountain and Mr. Martindale (Widnes). _

Mr. Crosfield wrote that he cherished very pleasant recollections of the delightful evening they spent together last year and he thanked the association for again offering him their kind hospitality. He hoped the now year would bring to all the members all good things. (Applause.) The loyal toasts were submitted from the chair and honoured with enthusiasm. Mr. P. o'l3anor proposed the health of " The Mayor and Corporation of Warrington and neighbouring boroughs." He paid a high tribute to the public services of the Mayor of Warrington, pointing out that his self- sacrificing efforts on behalf of the Infirmary led a large number of citizens to persuade him to take still more work upon himself by entering the Town Council. His municipal work early merited the confidence and good- will of the Council which were shown by his election to the Mayoralty. The fact that he had been prevailed upon to accept office for a second term was an eloquent indication of the respect in which he was held not only by the general body of citizeos but by those with whom he was more intimately associated in town's affairs. With regard to Widnes, he did not think England recog- nized the value of that town as it ought to do. It was the home of the chemical trade and, as such, had an Important bearing on many other industries. The MAYOR OF WARRINGTON, in reply, said the town had greatly improved of recent years and the charge was largely due to the efforts of the Town Council. The improvement of an ancient town like Wariington was a very expensive process. Compara- tively new towns, especially in view of the Town Planning Act, had advantages which the older places did not possess. A great deal was said in Warrington, as in other towns, to this effect : " Why don't you get such and such a class of men on the Town Council ? " Us thought that, as a rule, every town got the best men it could for municipal work and it could not do better than that. Looked at from that point of view, Warrington had the best men It could get in the Council and they were the men who were boat adapted for the work. Those people who cavilled about the members of the Town Council not being of the proper calibre should come forward and offer their services. In his early days he probably thought the same as those critics, but his experience had made him more broad-minded and he found that, however members of the Council might differ on various points, they were all united in working hard and to the best of their ability for the good of the town. (Applause). The MAYOR OF WIDNES also responded. Ile said Widnes was described some years ego as "the black spot on the Mersey," but,like Warrington, the town had seen great changes and improvements. Some people were inclined to grumble because the rates had risen from 4s. 10d. (at the time of the incorporation of the borough), to 6s. 10d. In the £. The great question, how- ever, was "Can you show value for the money spent?" and he thought that in Widnes they could. Sir Gilbert Greenall once questioned whether trees would grow in Widnes—(laughter)—but they were growing some and hoped to grow more. With the aid of the teachers they had formed an association for the cultivation of window plants, and next they intended to plant trees in the streees. Whatever might be said about the vegetation, he believed that in Widnes they grew fairly decent people. (Laughter and applause.) His Worship referred to,the successful co-operation of Warrington and Widnes in ()posing the St. Helens Corporation Gaa Hll, end said joint action could be helpful in many directions.

Councillor J. T. LOOKER proposed "The town and trade of Warrington." He said the inhabitants of Warrington were proud to think they belonged to an ancient and historic town but there were some legacies of the past which presented difficulties for the present generation to fight against. The great progress of the borough and the growth of its many industries had brought such immense numbers of people into the neighbourhood that the streets in the business part of the town were very congested. No-one outside the Council had any conception of the obstacles which had to be encountered in carrying out the schemes of Improvement. It was, he supposed, natural for those people whose interests were affected to try to get as much as possible from the Corporation. Some members of the Council, however, were strong enough to say they would not be bled and they were prepared to let things wait if unreasonable demands were made. Athough progress had been made, much arduous work had still to be taken in hand. There was, however, no reason to be ashamed of the town. (Hear, hear). When the bridge and other important under- takings had matured, they would have every reason to be proud of Warrington. Few towns could compare with Warrington in the number and renown of the products of its industries. They could hardly go into any part of the world without finding Warrington commercially represented. Its products penetrated to every corner of the globe and it was gratifying, on travelling abroad, to find the name of Warrington so well-known. A striking feature of Warrington's great concerns was that not one of them was of mushroom growth. There was something solid about them and they had risen, like the English oak, from small beginnings to the commanding position of

On their return to the meeting the minutes of the School Management Committee, including the proceed- ings on the supplemental agenda were confirmed. The DIRECTOR OF EDUCATION said that much bad been said about the leaving oft of a iminute from the agenda. It had always been a practice to avoid printing anything in the minutes which in any way reflected on any person in the service of the committee, and for that reason the minute was not printed. The practice had been followed consistently. If the committee was prepared to take the responsibility of any minute reflecting on persons being published and would give an expression to that effect, he would see that it was carried out. Until then the practice which had been followed would b 3 adhered to. The members could always be informed of the character of any paragraph which was missing from the minutes. THE FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN. - . . .

IThe School Attendance Committee resolved th-a-t the scheme which was adopted by the Education Committee at their meeting on the 20th July, 1908, be amended so that, in future, when children are reported to the com- mittee, either by the head teachers or other persons, as being apparently unable, by reason of lack of food, to take full advantage of the education provided for them, the procedure shall be as follows, viz. :—(a) the chil- dren shall be examined by the school medical officer as , soon as possible with a view to his furnishing the com- mittee with his opinion as to whether or not the children are insufficiently fed ; (b) the. parents of all children reported by the school medical officer to be insufficiently fed shall be immediately informed therof, and the children at once admitted to the children's dining rooms ; and (c) inquiries shall then be made Into the financial circumstances of the parents of these children with it view to the committee determining as to.whether the meals shall be provided free or whether a charge shall be made upon the parents In respect of the cost of the food supplied, and if so, the amount thereof, each CPSB being dealt with on its merits. Mr. PLINSTON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said that when the question was before the committee some months ago a number of teachers reported cases of children whose parents were in receipt of parish relief being insufficiently fed. The doctor reported upon all these cases and arrived at the conclusion that no case was being improperly fed, They had that in their mind in coming to the recommendation submitted for approval. The scale was to be practically abolished and all cases reported by the teachers were to be medically examined. If in the opinion of the doctor these children were not sufficiently fed so as to benefit by the education received they were to be fed and then If the parents were in a position to pay they were to be compelled to pay for the feeding given. (Hear, hear.) Uuder the new scheme no child was to go without food. A medical report on each case had to be given, and that would be considered by the sub-committee in dealing with the matter.

Mr. WALKER seconded the approval of the minutee. Mr. llPsos said it was wonderful how calm the atmosphere had gone now that they had come to the real business of the committee and had to deal with poor hungry children who could not pull the strings. Although he had the highest confidence in the doctor he was rather afraid that perhaps some little hardships might be inflicted by the fact that the doctor was only to report as to when a child was insufficiently fel. There must be a considerable amount of suffering before insufficiency of food was manifested. He took it the committee would continue to follow the usual form of procedure, namelyz that in addition to the doctor's'report they would investigate the cases,vo that if any information was supplied that the income of the home had suddenly vanished or diminished the child would be relieved where necessary. Malnutrition was at times difficult to detect and to throw the whole responsibility on the shoulders of the doctor was too much, lie took it that it would be the intention of the committee not to wait for the report of the doctor if they had evidence of the need of the home. The CHAIRMAN : I think that is the spirit in which these recommendations are made. Mr. PLINsToN : That is so. - - _

Councillor PEMBERTON said it wag possible for a child of good phy•ique to undergo intense suffering, through under-feeding far several days and not to show it. He trusted that every care would be taken to investigate the case. The minutes were then confirmed.

"My back plagued me, on and off, for nearly four years," says Mr. J. McGough, of 17, Stanhope-street, St. Helens. " Every time I caught a bit of a chill the pains were very severe, and after stooping at work it was no easy matter to get up again. The urinary system was not in a healthy state, so I put it down that my kidneys were causing the trouble. I used to feel drowsy, and dizzy in the day time. Although I tried several different remedies, I can honestly say that I obtained no relief until I started with Dean's backache kidney pills. Six boxes of these completely cured me, and I am very pleased to say a good word for the medicine. I can recommend no better remedy than Dean's pills to those who suffer with a bad back. They did me the world of good. (Signed) John MeGough." 2/9 a box, C boxes, 13/9 ; of all dealers. Ask for DOAN'S Backache Kidney Pills, like Mr. McGough had.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

THE FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN.

In addition to the important discussion on the pro- posal to restrict competition for the headmastership of Evelyn-street Council School to Warrington teachers, there were several points of Interest at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee on Monday evening. EVELYN-STREEr SCHOOLS. It was decided by the School Management Committtee to appoint Mr. Bertha Pugh, and Miss Dorothy Bee, headmistresses of the infants' and girls' departments respectively of the Evelyn-street Council school at their existing salaries. The engagements of nine teachers connected with the Sankey Bridges (Infants') Council school and the infants' department of the Trinity school were ordered to be terminated in order that they might be transferred to the Evelyn-street school at their existing salaries. Messrs C E Bromley, Dale, W H Gorse, W Miles, J E Richardson, J Rose, and A Tilling, at present acting as managers of the new Sankey Bridges Council School, were invited to serve as managers of the new Evelyn-street school. At a libsequent committee meeting it was reported that all these gentlemen with the exception of Mr Gorse had intimated their willingness to accept the appointment as managers of the school, and it was resolved, that the above named gentlemen, together with Councillor Bellian and Mr C E Parker, be appointed managers of the school. These minutes were confirmed. TEACHERS' SAL kRIES. •

At a meeting- Or the School Management Sub-com- mittee on January 11th, It Wal resolved that the salaries of the teachers in public elementary schools in the borough be Increased in acoordance with the committee's scale as from April let. Alderman 13aNNsrrr said he should be glad of some information as to the actual net increase which the revision of the scale of salaries represented for the year. The DIRECTOR OF EDUCATION said the amount of the increase was £l4O less than last year. That, however, did not mean the actual increase, which could only be ascertained when the estimates were made. Last year the actual increase was £5OO, and probably this year it would be less than that.

At a later meeting it was recommended that the scale of salaries be further revised so that the salaries of those teachers serving in classes C and F be altered to provide for the rate of increases to be £5 per annum, and after nine increases £lO per annum, instead of £5 per annum throughout as at present. Alderman BOLTON moved that this minute be referred back. lie considered the teachers in Warrington were very liberally paid, yet the scale was being revised without a word of explanation. They seemed to be playing ducks and drakes with the finances of the borough. Mr. YAIRCLOUGH formally seconded the amendment remarking that he would lime to hear the reasons why the recommendation was made. Canon STEVENSON said the matter was a very small one. It was merely to meet the case of one or two teachers who belonged to a class which they should not perpetuate. They were, however, very worthy people and if their salaries were increased at the present at small increments it would be a long time before they reached any reasonable salary at all. There would be no further cases, and he hoped the committee would pass the recommendation. _ _ .

Alderman BOLTON : Yes ; but it is a permanent affair afterwards. _

The CHAURNIAN: No. This cle-ss of teacher will go out. Councillor PEMBERToN said be knew why the recommendation was made, but be had taken up a position in public life, and should maintain it, that every member of the committee had a right to know what all the other sub-committees were doing. The whole of the business should be made quite clear and for that reason he should vote for the amendment. A vote was then taken, and the amendment was carried by 12 votes to eight. The minutes with the alteration were then approved. Canon STSVXNSON explained that in consequence of the amendment, the salaries in two cases only must be reduced back from £125 to £l2O.

COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON BLAMES HIMSELF. Councillor Pr:4l3mm; called atention to the fact that a certain minute had been omitted from the minutes. He was not wishful to publicly discuss the matter but he thought the omission was very improper. The CHAIRMAN explained that minutes involving a personal question were not as a rule printed. Councillor PEMBERTON : It is a serious matter reflect- ing en the management of a school. He added tint he did not want to discuss the merits of the case, as he would stand to be shot at. The resolution was a subterfuge. It was suggested that the matter should be discussed in committee._ _ _ .

Councillor PEMBMITOS : It is a thing that the public ought to know. This is the first time in my public, career that I have blamed myself for not making a thing public. I deferred to the wish of the committee and thought the matter was at an end but now they want to re-open it again. I shall not shrink from my duty, painful as it may be, or from the confession I have to make that I did something I thought I ought not to have done. . . . . .

After further die:melon it Was decided to go into committee and the Press represeutatives retired.

Do you fully realise that all this quickened motion is making a tremendous demand upon your powers of endurance? As Bulmer finished reading the long state- ment, he let the paper fall on the table With a heavy sigh and sat staring at it in infinite. distress and grief. In the pause which followed, Mr. Steven- son, who knew now of the relations between 11:.s two companions, rose and went out of the room. Presently /Lamer looked up At Helen and found that, with the tears standing thick in her eyes, she was • writing, and a moment afterwards she handed him what she had written. It was a telegram addressed to Sir Gre- gory. forgive you with all my heart. - -Helen. " "That is my answer, Bulmer. Now do as you wish in everything." "It is worthy of you, dearest. Worthy of my Helen ~" and he took her in his arms and held her to his heart, pressing his kisses on her lips. Thee drew ans.rt on hearing some commo• motion in the next room, and Mr. Stevenson entered, followed by Mr. Garwood. "Mr. Garwood can explain that London business," said Mr. Stevenson. "Mrs. Caul- field wired him to come down for the. pia- ps2." "The explanation will have to be short," said Bulmer, "for we are going to the Chase."

Helen started and turned to protest, hut Bulmer laid his hand on hers, 'You eaid it should all be as I wished," he whispered with a smile.

Mr. Garwood', explanation was soon made. Mrs. Caulfield had sent him a long telegram in regard to Bostock's real charac- ter and the part he had taken, and how Helen's friend, Mary Pescott, had given the information which had enabled him to Ellic. coed for the time. _ _

And almeet before the lawyer had had time to express his regret at having been the dupe of Bostock's canning, Buttner hurried Helen away to get such things as she needed from her lodgings until her trunks could be Bent after her to the Chase. Mr. Stevenson was at the station to see them start. He was very depressed at losing Helen, but very delighted that happiness was to be hers at last. "I don't know whether I'm sorry or glad," be said as he shook hands with Bulmer. "At one moment I feel I should like to put a bullet into you for rob- bing me in this way; and at another to write you a cheque for a thousand pounds for making her happy. And if you don't do it, it shall be the bullet," sod he laughed in the effort to hide how deep his feeling really was. "It was your own letter that did it all, re- member, Mr. Stevenson," said Bulmer. "I shall have that little comfort in my lone- liness. I shall be terribly, terribly lonely, too," he said to Helen. "I'm losing the best. friend in the world." _ . _

..... "Losing the typist, but not the friend," replied llelen, pressing his hand. "No. Thank God for that," he agreed earnestly. Ile held her hand a while, look- ing steadily into her eyes. Then he pressed his lips to it, and after releasing it paused n moment, sighed, and said very abruptly. "l'in only an old fool. I won't wait," and hurried away. Bulmer looked after him in surprise and laughed. "Don't laugh, Bulmer," said Helen earnestly. "You don't know ;" and he saw that her eyes were dim with tears as she en- tered the carriage. On the journey Bulmer explained to her the plan which he had formed in the night— that they should affect to have just dis- covered the fact of her being the rightful owner of the Chase; but she urged that no- thing at all should ever be said until after Sir Gregory's death. And to this he agreed, if the matter could be so arranged. Lady Trevelloek was to know as little as possible; and when they reached the Chase Bulmer went at once to tell her of Helen's arrival. He found his mother in great dis- tress at Mrs. Caulfield's departure, and in some bewilderment as to the cause.

"Beth told me there was to be no marriage after all, Bulmer, and that she wile going away, for good. What does it mean? " "I have found Helen, mother; and Mrs. Caulneld had kept us apart." "But Helen is married, Bulmer!"

"No, mother. That was all wrong. And there is to be a marriage. Helen's and mine. She has been deeply wronged by Mrs. Caul- field, and we all owe her reparation for what she has been made to suffer."

"Where is she?" "Here at the Chase. She has come with me. I thought it best to see you first." "God bless the boy. You didn't think I needed to be prepared to take her to my old heart. Why I love the child for her own sweet sake as mush as for yours." He brought lichen to the room and let her enter alone. And when he followed soon afterwards he found them locked in each other's arms, with tears of happiness in their eyes. He was drawing back to leave them together, when his mother called him.

'Come in, Bulmer." lie croesed to her couch and she took his hand.

"This has made me very happy," she said gently, with a smile thro tgh her tears. "I ,have longed all my life for such a daughter as Helen." "Thank you for that, mother, from my heart," he replied, bending down to kiss her. She kissed him in return, and then looked at Helen.

"And you, my dear child? " Helen paused, too deeply moved for the instant to find words. Then she threw her arms round Lady Trevellock's neck, and al! her heart found vent in one passionak, almost sobbing cry, "Mother! "

THE END.
