Slit GREGORY'S SILENCE

ARTHUR W. MARCHMONT, Author of "The Man Who Was Dead." "By Right of Sword," &e.

CHAPTER XXVL

===== 0002601_19120203_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
THE END.

"My dear Bulmer,— I send the enclosed statement to you under cover to Mr. Steven- son. in order that you may read it before we meet again and know the full truth. Yoi. are at liberty to read as much of it as you will to the young lady you know as Helen Paxton, and also to Mr. Stevenson. I will have no more concealment, let the cost be what it may; and what the cost will be must rest in their hands. Let it be what it may, it cannot cause me more suffering than my own wrong-doing has caused me during the past terrible mouths. lam willing to give up the Chase at once. —Your affectionate father."

The following was the enclosure: "My dear Son,—For many years the world has known me as Sir Gregory Trevellock. I took the name when, as Gregory Powell, I succeeded the late Sir John Trevellock. But I have no right to either name; and how I came to be so known this statement will ex- plain. "When we were young men I, George Powell, and my cousin, Gregory Powell, were both sent to America; he because of the wild dissipated life ho had lived in this country, I because I wao poor and had no prospects. We met in Montana, where each had gone in the hope of finding an easy road to wealth at the mines. I, did net at first 'know that he was in that district, and only learnt it when I heard of his wild doings and generally evil life. The knowledge came to me under very painful circumstances.

"I was at the time under the influence of Alice Lacosta, and wishful to marry her. My cousin Gregory was her lover secretly, how- ever ; and one day he came to me quite un- expectedly, and, with many coarse jeers and taunts, flouted me about her, and declared that he had ruined her under a pretence of marriage, he himself being already married. A violent quarrel followed. which ended in his attempting my life. He then fled, and hearing of him, I wrote that if we met again I would shoot him.

"While I was still in this hot mood, Alice Lacoste came to beg me to help her. She did not believe Gregory's story of the previous marriage, and wished to find him, and force the truth from him. I agreed, and together we went in pursuit of him. We tracked him to a place called Wallace, a rough, lawless place close to some silver mines, where we were all three unknown. We met in a lonely shanty. All three were mad with rage. Gre- gory had my threatening letters upon him, and taunted me about them. I had drawn my revolver, really more to protect myself from his violence than intending to kill him; and when he refused to say a word about his previous marriage, and insulted Alice Lacosta in terms which even now, after the lapse of all the years, make my blood boil, she snatched the weapon out of my hand and shot him through the heart. "I could not blame her for the net. Ile had merited his fate; and no jury in that district woui(l have thought of punishing her for it. _ _ .

"But the moment we had ascertained that Gregory was dead, she showed how she in- tended to use the thing against me. With the weapon levelled at my head, she declared that unless I agreed to personate Gregory and make her my wife, for the sake of her nnborn child, she would denounce me as his mur- derer. My threatening letters were in his pocket; it was my weapon with which he had been killed and she vowed she, would swear that in jealousy I had followed them and murdered her husband in cold bloci. The apparent proofs were damning. I knew my life would be taken; and unfortu- nately I yielded. I ceased from that moment to be myself, George Powell, and became Gregory Powell. The story she had declared she would tell against me was told to explain Gregory's death ; and immediately after- wards we left Wallace, and I married Alice Lacoste. Her child was born—the girl whom you knew as your sister, Lotta. "I sought work, and lived an honest life; but, owing to a chain of circumstances, having once declared myself to be Gregory Powell, I found it impossible to again take my own name. In less than a year Lotta's mother died; and soon afterwards I married again—the purest woman who ever drew the breath of life, your mother. "She never knew me as other than Gre- gory Powell, and I had hoped that all that black, distressing past would have been kept from her until the secrets of all hearts are unsealed after death. Fate has, however, de- cided r)therwise, and I can struggle against it no longer. "When you were four years old I heard that, as Gregory Powell, I was entitled to the Chase and the name of Trevellock. As Gregory had died without leaving any chil- dren—and I believed that from my heart, and had made every effort to discover the truth—l was by right entitled to the succes- sion; and the one question I had to decide was whether I should take the inheritance in my own name, or as Gregory. For reasons you aill understand, after reading this state- ment, I decided to come to England as Gre- gory, and became Sir Gregory Trevellock. "Of the life at the Chase I need not speak, until, like a thunderclap, Helen came to me and put into my hands the proof of her parentage. She was Gregory's child by the first marriage, of which he had spoken. "I was as unstable as water in face of the temptation to keep silence. I could not avow the truth and face the exposure of that black leaf in my life. I fell. God forgive me, for I know now how deep was the fall; and my sin has been with me every hour from that terrible ordeal. I told her I had destroyed tha m•rtnf• she gave me. I lied. and foun% afterwards that she knew 1 had lied. 1 dui not destroy them. I could not. I still meant to avow everything, but found pretext sfter pretext to put off the avowal. .1 fear I should never have made it, but have left it to be discovered after my death. How I loathe my despicable cowardice I cannot tell in words. _ _ _

"Then Jasper Dostock came to the Chase, knowing of some of the evil deeds which Gregory had committed and thinking that I was the man who had done them. Oh, the punishment of all that! I bribed him to hold his tongue, and deceived you all, even my dear, patient, loving wife. All the rest of my miserable hypocrisy you know, and I need' not dwell on it. "The letter from Mr. Stevenson, part of which you read when I was all but stricken unconscious by the blow it dealt, told me that now discovery was inevitable; and when you came to me afterwards I was going to try and tell you everything. I called you back, but it was too late. I heard that you had started on your journey, and knew that you would hear everything from him. "So I have written this confession—thiit Helen and you may know the full truth. "I am in her hands. It is for her to do what she will. For myself, I ask no mercy. I deserve none. It is but right that I should suffer for the wrong I have done; indeed, I declare solemnly that it would be a relief to me to bear some other than the self-infliated punishment which has racked and tortured me to the verge of suicide. But for my wife, I would have you plead with. Helen. She has not many years to live, and if those years can be spared from the shame and suffering and grief inseparable from a knowledge of my sin, in the name of mercy let it be done. She is innocent of even a thought of all this; end the knowledge that it would prol,ably

HOCKEY.

LATCHFORD.

The First Team were defeated at Wiunington Park last week by 3-1. The visitors were the first to score, Travis netting after a nice movement among the for- wards. The Winnington inside-left however equalised with a fast shot before the intervc,l. The home team re-arranged their forward line for the second half with marked success as they got four more goals and won easily. Ridley in goal for Latchford played well and could not be blamed for any of the shots that scored. Knight and Fletcher in the back division were also very prominent. Just after half time two of Latch- ford's forwards got damaged accidentally and were of little use for the remainder of the game andlthis placed a great handicap on the team generally. The Second Teams met at Latchford and when the home eleden were leading by three clear goals ten minutes from time a comfortable win seemed assured. W'innington however male an astonishing rally and actually got three goals before the whistle blew and made a most creditable draw of 3-3 amid some excite- ment. 11T Gibbs (who hails from a Nottingham Club) made a very successful debut at right-half for Latchford and Isherwood and Banks also played soundly, with the result that the visiting forwards were well held nearly 'dl the game. Among the forwards Laver, Calton, and Minards did many good things. Tbe goals were scored by Colton (2), Minards (1). For to-day the first team had no fixture but a match has been arranged with the officers hockey eleven trona Orford Barracks. The death took place on Monday at his residence of Mr. Edmund Range, the assistant overseer for the townships of Whiston, Knowsley,Ecoleston, and Windlo of the Prescot Union. Mr. Range, who was in his sixty-second year, leaves a widow, four daughters, and two sons to mourn his loss.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

A TRADES DISPUTES BILL.

The Liberal Government in the Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society addressed itself to the legislative programme of the second part of the session on Tuesday when a Trades Disputes Bill was introduced by the Home Secretary (Mr. Mark Howarth). The attendance, though an improvement on some recent meetings, was not as large as the importance of the subject warranted. The Bill establishes compulsory arbitration in trades disputes in England, Scotland and Wales. Its provisions, as laid on the table of the House,read : Clause I. That the Country be divided up for the purpose of the Act into six districts, tour districts being in England, one in Scotland and one in Wales.

Clause 11. That this Act shall apply in the first instance to the following trades only :—Textile, Engineering, Shipbuilding and Metal Trades, Building, Mining, Railways, other transport trades, Printing and allied trades, Corporation Employees. Other trades shall be brought within the scope of the Act as is found necessary. Clause 111. That in every district separate Arbitration Boards shall be formed for each trade, these Boards to consist of ten representatives of the Employers and ten representatives of the Employees, presided over by a Chairman, agreed on by both parties. Such Chairman to be selected from a panel issued by the Board of Trade. Clause IV. That a Central Arbitration Board be established consisting of a Chairman appointed by the Government two repre- sentatives appointed by the Masters Fed.3rations and two representatives appointed by the Trades Unions. Clause V. That it shall be incumbent on both parties in case of dispute to give thirty days notice to their local Arbitration Board, before either the Masters shall lock out their Employers, or the Employers declare a strike. Clause VI. On receiving notice the local Arbitration Board shall meet within ten days and endeavour to arrive at an agreement._

- Clause VII. Should no agreement be arrived at by the local Arbitration Board within twenty-one days of receiving notice, the matter in dispute shall be referred to the Central Arbitration Board who shall endeavour to effect a compromise between the two parties, failing which they shall publish a statement of the terms of settle- ment suggested by them. Clause VIII, An agreement once arrived at shall be binding on both parties for a period of twelve months and any employer or ' employee who shall break any portion of the agreement arrived at shall be liable to a fine not exceeding £250 and ,£5 respectively. It was announced that Clause IV. had been altered to provide that the members of the Central Arbitration Board should include two representatives "appointed by the masters" and two "elected by the workers throughout the country." The Home SECRETARY, in introducing the Bill, said he did not think it embodied any principle that was directly opposed to any of the tenets of any political party. During the recent uarest and upheavals great industries had been " held up," almost at a moment's notice, and the transport trade had perhaps suffered more than any other. Th result had bean in many cases that the very necessities of life, the foodstuffs of the people, had risen almost to famine prices. In a speech during the recent railway troubles Mr. W. P. Rylends described tSe recourse to strikes and lockouts as a barbarous system, and advocated the eettlement of disputes in the transport trades by means of an Arbitratiou Board which should inspire the confidence of every section of the community. In IMO there were 3l strikes in this country representing a loss of about £2,000,000 in wages. The one redeeming feature I was that in practically every care masters and men had shown a willingness, even an eagerness, to meet together and endeavour to arrivo at a solution. Speaking generally, he did not think the fault lay entirely with the m4sters or the men or with both, but that it was chiefly due to a want of the necessary machinery which would enable grievances to be ventilated and a com- promise effected before such a drastic step as a strike or lock-out was resorted to. The present Bill, he claimed, provided that me.chinery and would do much to end the evils which were fraught with such disaster to our trade. It was a measure upon which both sides of the /louse could work, with the object of producing the most perfect instrument of legislation possible. The new scheme would not apply to Ireland which had already received Home Rule from that House and, they believed, would receive it from the Imperial House next session. (Mr. E. England : "It does not look like it"). Trades disputes were a domestic question which might well be left to the Irish Parliament with- out outside interference. The trades included within the scope of the Bill were those which the experience of past years had shown to be most likely to suffer from disputes. If the local Arbitration Boards did not arrive at an agreement, it was felt that with the assistance of the Central Board a successful compromise would be effected. If not, the Board would publish the condi- tions which they suggested for a settlement, and the Government would be prepared to leave the rest to the force of public opinion. The scheme would mean the recognition of trade unions and he did not think it was necessary to make any apology for that. If there should be any persons with such antedeluvian ideas as to object to the recognition of the union., he could only say that it was useless for them to kick against the pricks. The trade unions had now become an essential part of the machine of industry, and they contributed very largely to its power and smooth running, The representatives of the Cantral Arbitration Board would, however, be elected irrespec- tive of membership of trade unions. The Government did not say that the Bill was an ideal method of dealing with the subject, but they claimed that it was an honest and sincere attempt. Mr. J. MCDERMID (C., Paisley), in moving the customary Opposition amendment, protested against the way in which the House had been treated in being called upon to discuss at sight a measive of such importance. That fact might to a large extent account for the meagre attendance. The Hom a SECRETARY regretted that the Bill did not appear in the local papers but said the Opposition were to blame for occupying so much space with their dumping exhibition. (Laughter). Mr. laloDmimick : Our friends seem to have been somewhat in the dumps last week, and it is plain they have not altogether recovered from the effects. (Laughter). Mr. WATSON (Socialist, E. Birmingham) : That exhibition would give anyone the dumps. (Laughter.) Mr. E. ENGLAND (Li Warrington) : It 'ought to do. Mr. MCDERMID I admit a section of our friends have been upset over it. No doubt they will recover in due time. (Laughter.) Continuing, he said, that in all sections of the House there was a consensus of opinion as to the necessity of some such measure as had been placed before them that night. (Socialist "No, no's.") They were all agreed as to the disastrous effects which industrial disputes had on the community and they had for a long time felt the need for something more up-to- date than the barbarous method of the strike or lock- out. Those who suffered most from the effeots of that method were those who were lowest in the social soale. They were the first to feel the distress and they felt it longest and most acutely. He' contended that the present Bill had been hurriedly prepared. If there were any benefits to be derived from it by the industrial popu- lation,he protested against Ireland being left outside Its scope on the visionary and somewhat uns.lbstantial prospect that they might have, in a short time, some measure of self-government which would enable them to deal with such matters themselves. The prospect of any such development became more remote every day they lived. If the Bill provided the necessary machinery for settling disputes, why limit it to certain trades ? They ought to have a measure that would be equally applicable to the lowest trades in the country as well as to the better organized workers. It was a significant fact that, while the Rome Secretary professed to give recognition to trade unions, he struck out the only reference to trade unions in the measure. Trade unions had in the past been highly beneficial to the workers and it was to be regretted that the moderm develop- ments of the movement had been of such a character as to destroy very largely the benefits formerly derived from it. It was a great pity that the unions had in some oases practically abdicated the functions for which they were originally intended and had become political institutions. Representative men on certain trades councils had been compelled to retire in favour of extremists who had advocated impracticable schemes and had been largely responsible for many trade disputes. The trade of the country had suffered very disastrously from the effects of industrial welfare. The Opposition were twitted when they said there was another cause of dwindling trade, but they bad on all occasions pointed out that there were many contributory causes, and that unemployment was not I solely due to any one of them.

Mr. L. WARBURTON (C., W. Bristol) seconded the amendment. He said the Bill was ri mixture of what bad been done in Australia, New Zaaland and Canada, but he questioned whether it would be satisfactory to this country. He believed that before they took a Ray from a roan the power t. strike strike they must offer him some equivalent and else establish a minimum cage. The only power the workman possessed was the power to strike. Under the now Bill he would have to accept the decision of the Central Arbitration Board. Wes that just ? (Socialist " No's.") How were the workers to elect two represent itives on the Arbitration Board ? They were no: suleci sntly co nolileted. It was proposed to inflict a £5 penalty on a workman eh() brcke an agreement,but the majority of the workers had not £5. Bow could they fine a man who had no money? Mr. E. ENGLAND (1., Warriegton) characterised the Bill as a pill to cure an earthquake. It was oaly a slight palliative, whereas remedies and cures were required. Rarely in the history of the country had there been so much labour unrest as at present. La t year the nation was brought to the verge of revolution or civil war. The fact was tint the people had been bitterly disappointed at the non-fulfilment of the political promises which had been made t ) them. They were still waiting for the refreshing fruit which bad been dangled so often before their eye, under the disguise of Liberalism. Under this Govern- ment Liberalism had become almost a dead letter. (A voice : " Nonsense.") Some people went so far as to say that the legislation of the Government was Socialism. (Sosialists " No, no.") The Socialists denied that it was Secialism aid he said that the legis- lation of to-day was bastard Soo'a'ism. The trade returns of this country undoubtedly showed an increase; yet during the last ten 3ears the stets of the workers was worse than before that period. ( ' Question.") No wonder that the people were tired of their allegiance to the Liberal party. They felt that the Govern- ment bad played them false or, at least, bad not come up to expectations. They were so bitterly disappointed that they felt they must take matters into their own hands, and they were doing so. The Trades Disputes Bill would do very little to alleviate the situation. It was no better than the machinery already in force—in fact he preferred the present machinery. If employers and workers failed to agree they could invoke Board of Trade intervention. Fancy Liberals gaing in for compulsion ! They seemed to be losing all idea of what Liberalism really meant. True Liberalism said that capital and labour must be free to work out their own salvation. rbe pelioy of Mr. Lloyd George was crippling industry, while the policy of Mr. Boner Law sought to assist and build up our trade. The recent eleetione showed that the tide was turning ageinst the so-called Liberalism of to-day and if an appeal were made to the country the Government would be defeated. It only held office by the aid of the Welsh and Nationalist parties. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN (Soc., Gorton) said the Bill was inefficient, impracticable and utterly imp -ssible as a solution of the question. Never in the history of the House had they been presented with such a fallacy. The Socialists were the only party with a remedy which would bring about a final settlement in the industrial world. Whilst they realized the destitution caused by strikes, they were unwilling to advise the workers to forego the right to strike. The railway servants had had an experience of conciliation. The scheme had been interpreted to the liking of the masters, leaving the men in a worse position than they occupied before its introduction. The railway strike of 1911 was settled by arbitration and the men were tricked Ince to their work. The same discontent still prevailed amongst them. By allowing non-unionists to be elected on the Central Arbitration Board, the Ham Secretary had killed the idea of recognition. Before an agreement was con- cluded, would it be submitted to every worker ? How would it be possible, in the event of a coal strike, to fine every miner in the kingdom £5 ? The only means of effecting a final settlerneet wis to sooialise the means of production and thus establish tee equality of interest on the part of every worker and citizen. The CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER (Councillor J. Ha vthorn) said the members of the Government were not in the dumps. Any Free Trader who visited the dumping exhibition And saw the quality of the goods exhibited most have come away rejoicing in the supremacy of the British manufacturer. The Opposition attitude with regard to the Trades Disputes Bill was like their policy on the Insurance Bill. It was a " Yes-No " policy. They were in favour of a Trades Disputes Bill, but because it had been intro • duced by a Liberal Government they opposed it. The Tories were supposed to believe in the Colonies, yet the Colonies had led in the matter of trades disputes and had organizations such as the Bill suggested. He was surprised that the Socialist members objected, because in She Colonies the Labour members had been foremost in wive:eating compulso-y arbitration. The , fines proposed in the Bill were less than those already in force in the Colonies. (Mr. ENGLAND: "They. must be better off.") He did not thine anyone in the House could assert that the trade of this country was a dwindling quantity at the present time. The member for Warrington seemed to have a bee in his bonnet. (Laughter,) He was more concerned abcut the supposed injustices imposed by Mr. Lloyd George than about anything in the present Bill. The new scheme did not prevent worke-s from striking, but what it insisted upon was that workers and employers who had a dispute should come round the Arbitration Board before they struck, instead of afterwards This was no inter- ference with trade unions—in fact the Bill gave them a standing in being able to propound the dispute as far as the workers wore concerned. Mr. C. DUKES (SOC., West Ham) opposed the Bill root and branch, because he opposed the principle on %Mob it was based. The only line of demarcation between the wage slave of to-day and the old chattel slave was that the former had the right to withdraw his labour at any time. No power should be brought in to deprive him of that right end compel him to submit it to any arbitration that might be bought or sold by those who happened to control the money bags of the country. Industrial conflicts were net the result of agitation but of economic developments. Society was divided into sections —the exploiters and the] exploited—and between them there existed an antagonism which neither conciliation nor agitation could destroy. By virtue of his ownership of the means of production, an employer oould precipitate a look-out in order to dispose of surplus stock. The Bill would undermine the sympathetic strike and he believed it had been drafted for that purpose. The debate was adjourned until next Tueeday evening.

BURNING FATALITY.

Warrington Child's Sad End.

An inquest was conducted by Mr. Brighouse at the Warrington Police Station, on Monday morning, on the body of Bertram Philip Riley, aged two years and eight months, son of Joseph Riley, ,Pinewood Avenue, who was burned to death on Saturday morning. Joseph Riley, deceased's father, said his wife had informed him that she brought the deceased downstairs and put him in a cradle. She made a fire and at 9.30 she went out on an errand, leaving the child In the cradle near the flPe. When she returned she saw the cradle was in flames and she thereupon shouted for help, and a neighbour, Mrs. Eokersley, went in the house and helped to extioguish the flames. In answer to questions by the Coroner, witness said the child could walk and it would be in the cradle about two feet away from the fire. The cradle was made of wicker and the child would be covered by a counterpane. Mrs. Eckersley, 5, Pinewood-street said that about 9.45 on Saturday morning she heard Mrs. Riley scream" Oh I my child." On entering the kitchen she saw the cradle in flames. She got a rug and threw it over the cradle and afterwards put water on and she got hold of the child and took it into a neighbour's house. The child expired before she got it to the house. She thought the fire miglA have been caused by a spark dropping on to the counterpane. The mother of the child was not present as she was suffering from shock.

The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."

SUCCESSFUL WtIIST DRIVE AND DANCE.

The second annual whist drive and dance in aid of the Infirmary Million Penny Fund, bok place in the Empire Skating Rink on Monday. After the success which attended the first venture of the kind, a similar, if not better, affair was anticipated, and over five hundred and forty persons avembled. Their expecta- tions were not belied, the function being a highly enjoyable one. The first portion of the evening was devoted to whist, and for this popular game one hundred and twenty tables wers occupied by enthusiasts. Twenty games were played, the duties of M.C. being ably carried out by Mr H Collin, assisted by an energetic band of stewards, which included Messrs C H Wells, F Short, H Hope, F Garnett J H Smethurst, H Rogers, J L Davenp ad, J Bailey, A C Binks and W Kilduff. The prizes for the whist, which were given by Lsdy Greenall, wore presented by Mrs. W Peter Itylands.Mr. J. 11. Smethurst presided and the following were the winners: Ladies : 1, Mrs H B Houghton; 2, Mrs T Gumboil; 3, Miss Perdue; 4, Miss M Todd. Gentlemen : 1, Councillor W J Wilson; 2, Mr F J Mynekyme ; 3, Mrs F A Frost (playing as a gentle- man); 4, Mr H Baldwin.

_ A vote of thanks was accorded Mrs. Rylands for her services. About eleven o'clock the company assembled for dancing. An excellent programme bad beeen arranged and the party indulged in this popular pastime with great zest, to the strains of the Rink band. A feature of the evening was the shadow dances, for which lincelights had been fitted at each end of the spacious hall. The M C was Mr C II Wells, and he was assisted by Messrs F Short, B Cooper, H Maddock, J L Davenport, and Dr Burt as stewards. For those who were not disposed to dance a bridge drive was conducted, the prizes for which had been given by Lady Greensll and Messrs. Eustace and Ce. Ltd. The stewards were Messrs H Hope and 1 Bowes, and after a series of interesting games had been played the following winners were announced : —Ladies : 1, Mrs. Bops. Gentletren, 1, Mr. Baldwin. At intervals refreshments were provided. The management of this department was well cairried out by the Ladies' Committee of which Mrs. F. Short is the bon. sec-etary. The refreshment bar was in the hands of Messrs. C. Garton Stone and J. Holland, the licence having been obtained by Councillor Rogers. At an eaily hour on Tuesday morning the party broke up and the prevailing opinion was that the affair had been an immense success, one that reflected much credit on the General Committee of the fund with Mr. J. H. Sn.ethurst as the chairman and Mr. 11. Collin as hon secretary.

NTIDNES WOMEN'S STRANGE DRINKS, WHISKY NOT STRONG ENOUGH. A Pole named Anthony Xing, living in Waterloo road, Widnes, was summoned at the Liverpool Police Court, before Dr. Whitford and other magistrates, on Tuesday, for having unlawfully had in his possesivon, whilst on the premises of the London and North- Western Railway Company, without permission in writing, a glass bottle containing 41be. of inflammable spirit—to wit, ether drops. Evidence was given to the effect that on November 28th, a boy in the company of the defendant vreat into Lime-street Station, Liverpool, carrying a bottle con- taming the spirit. While the defendant was purchasing his ticket the boy dropped the bottle, and the contents burst into flame, the incident creating no little excite- ment. The defendant pleaded ignorance of any offence. 110 said he was taking the ether to Widnes where it w' used by women for putting in their A we. 'used gin, or coffee. He had bought it for them twice previously when visiting Liverpool. The Chairman remarked that ether was a most inflammable spirit. He had witnessed an operating theatre blown to atoms through a quantity of sunb spirit catching fire. A flue of 408. and 275. Gd. costs was imposed.

thised with the wish to get the best man. As a bumble educationist he had sympathy with that view, and he should be extremely sorry if the resolution were to form even a precedent for future action. The only justitiltion of it that he could see was that it might be advisable for the present moment to do something which m;ght be an encouragement to the teachers of the town, but if it were intended to be the future policy of the committee, he wouldifeel bound to vote against it. There seemed, however, to be some need to put some form of encouragement before the assistsnt teachers in the employ of the committee. There was some- thing to be said for that point of view, but there was nothing to he raid for making it a precedent for such a policy in the future. Councillor Dr. McKEE supported the amendment. He said there was a very important principle at stake and the resolution would undoubtedly be taken as a precedent. He failed to see how the committee could do injustice to the local teachers by appointing the best man. He was sure the members of the committee would pay rarticular attention to the Warrington man and, if his qualifications were equal to those of the out- sider, let him have the preference. If they had six good men in Warrington and they appointed one, would there not be more jealousy among the remaining five than if they brought in a man from outside? Education Committee Decides Against

Restricted Competition.

' driven to the absurd conclusion that Warrington lads must gain their experience only in their own town. That would not be a good thing. He was willing to ' give local applicants every possible chance and even to lean to thorn if it could be done without any unf Arness. Mr. Ursort Laid that ideally the thing to aim at would be to throw open every appointment to a headship to every certificated qualified man in the whole kingdom. That was the ideal, tut what did they find ?—t hat the schools of every county borough, almost without excep- tion, were closed to the teachers who were in the employ of that committee. The teacher whose name had been mentioned that night had the power to apply for hundreds of headships from which every teacher employed by that authority was excluded. (A voice: "How ?") Liverpool restricted all its headships to tenchers in its own employ. Manchester, London, and Birmingham did the same—in fact all the authorities, almost without exception, restricted their headships to teachers in their own employ. The

ONLY SCHOOLS TIIAT WERE OPEN TO COMPETITION were very small country schools where the salaries were not equal to the maximum which Warrington paid to its class teachers. On the face of it, the case Councillor Wilkinson had quoted looked as if there were a little hardship. A man, Warrington born and bred, went to Liverpool where he got probably a better salary than the Warrington authority was paying and where he had open to him hundreds of headships. In Warrington they had about fourteen headships. The men who were occupying those positions would, under normal condi- tions, hold them for the next fifteen or twenty years. The Warrington Committee had nearly 100 men in their employ who were qualified by their attainments to be headmasters, and they had to wait that time because all those other openings were closed to them. Ho had on his own staff men from Carlisle, llaslingden, Crewe and Maccles- field. All of them were natives of those places and desired strongly to get back to them, but they were excluded by the practice of those authorities from applying for any headships in those boroughs. It Warrington teachers were to find that the headships to which they might legitimately aspire were to be thrown open to the whole country, the committee could easily see that their enthusiasm in their work would be to some extent chilled. If there was nothing to be gained by devotion to duty, by strenuous toil, by doing work over and beyond—as many of them did—what the committee required, if there was to be no prospect of reward, the whole tendency would be to lessen their services and do the minimum that was required. The whole service of that committee would suffer through that one fact. He strongly urged upon the committee, in the interests of the work in which they were all engaged, to find among the 90 or 100 men in their own employ—all of whom were competent end qualified by having passed the necessary Government examinations to take the position, men with long years of experience behind them, men who bad been highly praised in that comnittee for the splendid iwork they had done—he said—" Give them the opportunity." Outside teachers had all the places under their own authorities omen to them—in fact where there was one opening in Warrington there would be at least ten in the other places he had mentioned. The MAYOR said he did not know until that evening that there was a SYSTEM OF "PROTECTION" _ _ _ . .

existing in the schools of Liverpool, Manchester and Birmingham. It seemed to him very clear how evident were the evils of" Protection "in that respect. If the results of " Protection " were as it was claimed from the political point of view,t hey would have no difficulty in voting on the suLject. There was no doubt that the larger the area of choice, the better candidates they obtained, the more likely were they to get a good man for the post. If other authorities were Darrow-minded and bigoted end confined appointments to their own areas, that was no reason why Warrington should not eet a better example. Let Warrington be more broad- minded than Manchester, Birmingham and other places. Even if they failed in widening the area to an unlimited extent, he certainly thought that in such a. case as Mr. Greene's it was hard for a native of Warrington to be excluded from competi- tion and possiLly an alien who happened to be in tho service of the Warrington authority at the present time, allowed to compete. Further, Mr. Greene was actually in the service of the Education Committee,as he taught in the evening classes at the Technical School. Councillor JENKINS : At Ileathaide.

The MAYOR said it was a HARD AND ARBITRARY RULE that such a teacher should be excluded. The broadening of the resolution would not do any injustice to local candidates, because if the teacher from outside was not the best man they would having nothing to fear from him. The resolution embodied a very artitrary and narrow-minded policy and he should vote against it. Councillor PiaIBERTON said he felt sorry that the champions of the teachers in Warrington thought it was necessary that they shculd be protected or that they could not hold their own with any outside teachers. One would have thought tbat Warrington was capable of producing head teachers equal to any to be found in any borough in England. Why should they fear compe- tition ? He did not know. If they did fear it, it seemed to be at once admitted that there were better men outside for head teachersbips, and that they in Warrington had not got as good men as were to be found outside. He for one did not believe that and he thought it would be a great pity, in the interests of the teachers themselves, to ask for that protection. It was belittling them and it wculd be a great mistake, from their own point of view, to limit the competition. Mr. JOLLEY said he would be very sorry indeed if the amendment were carried. What it really meant was that some members were very

DESIROUS THAT AN OUTSIDER SHOULD DE BROUGHT IN to fill that post. (" No, no.") He did not say "All " he said " Somo.', Let them be frank about it and look at the facts fairly: Mr.Upson had laid the case clearly before them. If his arguments were patiently followed they ought to influence the minds of several gentlemen who had announced their intention of supporting the amendment. That was only the second occasion on which it had been. possible for that authority to have the chance of appointing the headmaster of a boys' school. The Beamont school was the first and this was the second. They must not forget that whilst there were 13 or 14 headships for which teachers were eligible in ,he county borough area, most of thsm were in nen- provided schools and the committee had no say what- ever in the matter with regard to them. That was only the second occasion on which they bad had the oppor- tunity of doing justice to a number of able, faithful, experienced and loyal servants. Now it was proposed that that justice should not be done. That was really what it amounted to. (" Ni, no.") " That is my opinion " added Mr. Jolley. "It is no use Councillor Pemberton laughing like that. It is not argument." Councillor PEMBERTON : Mr. Jolley is saying some- thing as a fact that it is only En opinion: That is what I object to. lie has a right :to his opinions, but he I must not glee them as facts. It is totally wrong. Mr. JOLLEY : I think I know what is the difference between a fact and an opinion. Councillor PEMBERTON : I think you do. That makes it all the worse.

Mr. JOLLEY, -continuing, said that ON A FORMER OCCASION AN INJUSTICE WAS INFLICTED on a number of men who had given years of faithful service to tl e town in the cause of education and from whom a suitable headmaster could have been selected. He did not want to say a word against the gentleman who was appointed ; he was undoubtedly an able and capable man. But he said that there were men in the committee's employment who were quite capable and competent to fill the position. There were such men

irt their employment to-day. A number of authorities iestricted headships to the men who had served them. Mr. F. R. ROBERTS: May I ask if they restrict them in practic3 or by resolution? I think there is a grave difference there. We have peered a resolution that we will only appoint local men. Have they ? Mr. JoLLEY : No, we orly recommend it in this one care. Mr. F. R. ROBERTS: I understood there is a stand- ing resolution of 3 ours that you will only appoint local men. . .

Mr. JOLLEY: No it is only for this one particular case. Continuing, he said he did not know whether it was with the intention of stirring up party feeling and exciting prejudice that the question of Protection had been brought in. That was not the way to look at the question. There was a very real, practical and important side which it would he very silly to label Protection or Free Trade. They were all agreed as to the necessity of appointing the best man? Was it the man uho had two or three letters behind his name who was ntcessarily the best? When they invited written applications from all over the country, the people who selected candidates were naturally swayed by the paper qualifications. But were sucu qualifications all they needed for a headmaster? A headmaster had a grave responsi- bility placed upon his shoulders. CIIARACTER AND TEMPERAMENT , counted for a great deal more than academic attain- ments in a headmaster. He could name, off-hand, half- a-dozen men in Warrington mhose character and temperament were well-knoun to most of the members of the committee and eho in that respect, as well as by their experience and faithful service, were admirably qualified for headships. If they rejected the amendment it meant in prantice that those men were to be thrown over and discouraged in their work. Was it not silly to drag in the question of Protection ? Surely they were better able to judge of people of whose worth they had practical experience than of people a long distance away of vihom they knew nothinp, except by paper qualifications. The MAYOR : I alluded to it, humorously. Mr. JOLLEY : Yes, but other people diknot. Councillor WILKINSON Oh yes, we did. Mr. JOLLEY : Then I say it is not a question where any humour is necessary. These are points of vital importance to the future welfare of the educational work hf the town. I say quite deliberately that the rejection of this amendment will inflict serious injury, and deal a serious blow ut the educational work of the town. _ .

Councillor JENKINS said be came to the meeting with the determination to vote for the resolution, but he hoped he possessed a mind that was open to reason. The CHAIRMAN : I hope you won't decide till you have heard what I have to say. Councillor JENKINS: Having heard the explanation of Mr. Upson that all our teachers are barred from competition in other large towns— The CHAIRMAN : It is not accurate. . .

I .'o"t-tri-cillo-r diNR.INS : If they are never to obtain outside appointments, it is a strong argument in favour of confining the chance of obtaining a headmasterthip in Warrington to our own assistants, that they may gradually secure the positions of importance which they deserve. I will wait until the Chairman has spoken, but at present my inclination is to support Mr. Upson's views. The CITAIRMAN Eaid he would make his remarks at that stage for fear of any further possibility of a change of opinion. He felt quite as strongly the other way as Mr. Jolley did from his point of view. A great deal bad been said about. justice to teachers. There was something in front of that, and that was justice to the schools themselves and to the cause of education in Warrington. Their primary duty—and he laid it down as a solemn fact,apart from the saving grace of humour introduced by the Mayor and which he rather rejoiced at bearing because he agreed with a great deal of what his worshio had said—was to consider how to get the very best man they could for the school. (Hear, hear.) That was admitted, so what was there to fear?

WHAT COULD THERE POSSIBLY BE TO FEAR from competition by Warrington teachers with terchers from outside ? If they were agreed upon the first principle of getting the best man, they had either got the best man in Warrington or they had not. If they had got him in Warrington, by all means appoint him ; the committee would be absolutely unanimous on that question. He asked them, however, not to allow sentiment to be strained to the extent that it was pro- posed to strain it—in the interests of a very valuable section of their official staff, he admitted, but whose interests, after all, were secondary to those of the general question of the education of the children of this town. He was not going to suggest that they had not got the best man in Warrington. He believed it was quite possible that they had. But what was the best that had been said that night in favour of the Warrington teachers? He was rather surprised that more had not been said. The best that had been said was that they had competent and qualified men in War- rington. They all admitted that. But

WHAT WAS THE STANDARD GOING TO BE by which they would judge the qualifications of the teacher? They could not fix a definite standard. Their standard woe the best that the market had to give them, not the best that they had got locally. That was the standard they must act upon if they were going to make the appointment honestly and according to their consciences. They were all sufficiently locally patriotic to be prepared to give the preference, on equal terms, to any local teacher as against an outsider but they were not prepare A—at any rate, he was not—to do what was called justice to a teacher in Warrington at the price of the chance of having to refuse a better man. A great deal had been said about the practice in other towns. Tura practice was partial ;it was not complete. It applied to the provided schools in certain towns but he did rot think it applied to the non- provided schocls. What were they proposing to do in Warrington by that resolution? They had two provided schools and the Evel3n-streot school was the third. , They were proposing to restrict the competition for the headmastership of that school to anumber of gentlemen who had ALREADY RECEIVED PREFERENTIAL TREATMENT in regard to their present appointments, because of the fact Hitt these appointments were denominational appointments and were necessarily restricted very largely to the people of the denominations to which the schools belonged. (" No, no.") He did not want to comment upon it; it was an ugly fact which they had to deal with and their resolution proposed to give further preferential treatment to those gentlemen, to the exclusion of a man born and bred in Warrington, who had been out of Warrington for experience on the advice of their own official iepresontative (Hr. Murray) and who could not compete for a post in his own town. after having gained experience in another town. Councillor WILKINSON : It is scandalous. Mr. Lirso's : It is good canvassing. The CHAIRMAN : I sincerely hope that in the interests of education, not of any class or sectional interest, you will throw this appointment open to a much wider competition than is proposed in this resolution.

Mr. PLINSTON, in supporting the resolution, said paper qualifications often had too great weight in making appointments. One point in favour of the local teachers was that the committee had the oppor- tunity of judging of their teaching capacity, their power to control a class, end their kindness towards the children. The CHAIRMAN pointed out that the School Manage- ment Committee vc as not by any means unanimous on the question. The resolution was only carried by a majority of one. Canon STEVENSON said the feeling of the committee had no reference to any individual. They all lump:

===== 0002601_19120203_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTES BY " OVALIST,"

Was it Mclntyre's fault or Creevey's that Warrington did not beat Widnes? That is the question of the moment in local football circles. Personally I have never had the slightest doubt on the matter. The man who was responsible for giving Widnes the five points which robbed Warrington of victory and made the result a draw, was Creevey. He stood in front of the full-back preparing to take the fateful catch, and it was only when the ball and the on-coming forwards were nearly on him that he stepped aside and signalled to Mclntyre. The latter made the best of a desperate situation and dived for the ball, but was too late. In plain language Creevey funked the catch and the tackle, and gave the game away. Such an offence —and it is not the first—can not be readily forgiven.

Warrington had rather the better of the game, and gained their five points in workmanlike fashion. Defence was superior to attack on both sides, and Widnes would never have scored but for the incident above described. The wrath of he home partisans was extreme, and a good deal of it wrongly directed against Mclntyre. Last season, there was the same result to the corresponding match, and even then the equalising point was gained by the visitors in very lucky fashion. I am willing to admit that Widnes have had a better team than the Wirepullers for a couple of years past, but they should never have taken any points at Wilderspool.

The features of Saturday's game were the fine for- ward play on both sides, and the brilliant effort by which Renwick scored for Warrington. The ex-Wiganer "cut in" in the approved Colonial fashion and left the Widnes backs standing. I hope be will perfect the art, for it is absolutely essential to sound centre play and rarely fails to bring points. Gilbert (Australia) and Wagstaff° (Huddersfield) are past- masters in the art. The borne centres seem to have dropped into orthodox methods which are easily checkmated nowadays. Dickenson has not the pace for the " cut-in," so that much depends on Renwick for effective threejuarter play. He is mcre than justifying expectations.

The forward duel was keenly waged, and honours were about even. Those three 3oung scrummagers in the Warrington ranks—Fearnley, Tranter and Skelliorne—were untiring la their following-up and tackling. What they need is practice in combination. Forwards of their type need to model their play ou the clever loose methods of the Australm-ians.

Where Warrington were let down was at half back. Creevey did little on the attack—he seems to have forgotten the side-step and " dummy "—while his defence was a negligible quantity. Brooks doubiless suffered through the weaknrss of his partner, but he seems out of condition and made little headway from the pack. Lalley and O'Garra were, on the contrary, always breaking through and giving the threequarters chauces that were not made the best use of. Young Laws was the one Widnes threequarter to do himself justice. He was vxcellent whether attacking or defending, and completely bottled up Bradshaw. The latter did not run with anything like his usual pace, or he would have got the winning point in the second half when Renwick made a perfect opening.

Mclntyre is not playing as well as he did a year or so back—he seems slower and does not punt as far— but he played a sound game on Saturday. The best part of big work was his tackling, which twice saved the situation when Widnes were almost through. Probably, all that "Mac "requires is more training. He ought to make a full back second to none, if he will take the game seriously.

THE FRAWLEY CASE.

Frawley committed another breach of his agree- ment with Warrington, by turning out for the Colonials in the extra match against England on Wednesday. For the firet time he failed to score. The Warrington Club have referred the Frawley case to the Union, but there is little prospect of the Wirepullers obmining any satisf.iction.

LOCAL RIVALS ENGAGED.

Warrington Draw with Widnes.

After a fortnight's cessation from football owing to the snow, Warrington enthusiasts turned out in large numbers for the match with Widnes at Wilderspool on Saturday. The previous meeting between the teams had resulted in the defeat of the Wirepullere, largely owing to an accident to Ike Taylor, and there were confident hopes that the side would be avenged. Both teams were at full strength, and with the weather favourable, and the ground in fair order a capital game was exrected. The crowd numbered about 6,000, several hundreds coming from Widnes, and the teams were as follows:

Warrington.—Mclntyre, back ; Bradshaw, Renwick, Dickenson and Woodburn, threequarter-backs ; Brookes and Creevey, half-barks ; Thomas, Shugars, Cheaters, Tranter, Fearnley and Skelhorne' forwards. Widnes.—Jones, back • Reid, Taylor, Laws and Swinton, threequarter-hacks ; Lally and O'Garra, half- backs ; G Aspey, S Aspey, Johnson, Lloyd, Cosgrove and Higgins, forwards. Referee, Mr F Renton, Hunslet ; touch-judges, Messrs W Mainwaring (Salford), and %V T Chapman (Salford). Warrington opened in dashing style, and smart following by Fearnley, Tranter, and Skelhorn carried the ball to the Widnes quarter. Here Brooks passed out, and Renwick served Bradshaw, but the wing man was finely grassed by Laws. O'Garra relieved smartly, and clean passing by the Widnes backs saw the home defence tested. Mclntyre cleared finely, however, and went down to a rush by the Widne3 forwards in plucky fashion. Widnes were very troublesome at this stage, and Renwick earned hearty cheers for checking a dangerous move by Lally and Laws, and dribbling to the centre. Then came Warrington's turn, the forwards gaining ground with smart play. Brooks and Creevey I did their best to get the rear line going, and I Renwick again fed Bradshaw nicely, but the I wing man failed to beat J., nes at the corner flag. Still, the locals kept up the attack in persistent fashion, young Fearnley nearly going over after a rush. Widnes tackled splendidly, but were outwitted when Renwick took a pass from Creevey and swerved through the backs to score brilliantly near the posts. Thomas added a goal, and the cheering by the local supporters was loud and long. At this stage Widnes were decidedly overplayed, and Warrington would only have got their due if they had added to the score. Renwick and Bradshaw both made gallant efforts which succumbed to keen tackling, and Shugers burst over from a " scrum "on the line only to find the referee's verdict against him. Half-time: Warrington 5 prints, Widnes Early in the second half the ball was passed out to Bradshaw, who preferred to kick to the open, where Jones was in attendance, and a safety mark was the result. The kick availed Widnes little, for Brookes's response was accurate, and a subsequent pass from the half back almost sent %Voodburn over at the corner, the combined tackling of Jones and Reid avoiding disaster for the "Chemicals." Danger was cleared by Lally and another return to the attack by Creevey, who was now working the pack, gave Woodburn a second opportunity, but numbers again told. A forward rush relieved the . . _ _ _

line somewhai., whilst Widr.es claimed a Tut:ill-cr.-tidy-Li-- tage when they were awarded a matk for obstruction. Mclntyre tackled well under pressure end speedy following up gave Jones a warm time of it, but the visiting custodian was not found wanting. Warrington forced matters and Renwick gave Bradshaw another chance, but the wing rnr.n was slow in starting and missed the try by the yard. Two penalties in quick succes- sion gave the visitors a fine opportunity to attack, and from the second Widnes scored a lucky try. George A:quay punted high in the air and Creevey misled Mclntyre by going for the catch and then stepping aside. The full back made a plucky dive for the ball, but it was too late and Widnes forwards rwhed it over th 3 line near the posts, Jones equalising with an easy goal. The keenest play followed,Warrington trying hard to regain the lead, but there was no beating the Widnes defence. Luck seemed against the Wirepullers for several promising attacks were rendered ineffective by penalties. Near the close Widnes made a great bid for victory, Taylor narrowly failing at a penalty goal. Then O'Garra broke clean away from midfield, and only a grand tackle by Mclntyre saved the hornesters. Final : Warrington 1 goal, I try (5 points) ; Widnes, 1 goal 1 try (5 points). ST. HELENS WIN AT ROCHDALE.

There were 5,000 spectators on the Rochdale Athletic Grounds when St. Helens gained a capital victory over th 3 Hornets. St. Helens soon opened the scoring, Greenwood crossing from a cross-kick by Molyneux. Belshaw kicked a grand goal. From a scrummage near the Hornets' line Schofield gathered and kicked straight into Flanagan's hands, for the latter to romp over with a try. Turtill failed at goal. Paddon made a brilliant run down the field and passed inside to Woods, who re-passed, and enabled Paddon to score. The same player failed to land a goal. Half-time—St. Helens, 8 points ; Rochdale Hornets, 3 points. In the second half Belshaw kicked a penalty goal for St. Helens. Result :—St. Helens, 2 goals and 2 tries (10 points) ; Rochale Hornets, 1 try (3 points).

RUNCORN'S MISFORTUNES.

Ton minutes before the kick-off there were twenty people on Irwell-lane ground for this match, and the crowd at any time did not total more than 500. Five minutes later Padbury scored at the corner for Richardson to fail. Then the Rangers attacked, and after a nice bout of passing, Wilde got over near the posts, Davidson adding the goal points. A weak spot was found in the Runcorn defence a little later, which enabled Barnes to get over, Davichon again improving. Result :—Broughton Rangers 2 goals 2 tiles (10 points) I Runcorn 1 goal 2 tries (8 points).

A SCHOOL FOR DOMESTIC SERVANTS. Following a proposal to crganise a training shoor for waiters, the London Couaty Council has under con- sideration a &Theme for increasing the supply of efficient domestic servants. In the secondary schools directly controlled by the Council aid in others which are aided there are rattly bright girls bolding scholar- ships with no prospect of a congenial future. To meet their case and to supply an urgent need it is intended to open a large establishment which will be at the same time a boarding-honse for business and pro- fessional women and a training school for domestio servants. The purpose of Mr. Norman IS WAR Angell's " The Great Illusion," GOOD a book which Mr. J. M. BUSINESS? Robertson, M.P., who is an

author and a critic as well as a politician, describes as the greatest work of persuasion ever issued in connection with any propaganda, is to prove that in war the loss of the conqueror is as great as that of the conquered, and that, therefore, it can never be carried on at a profit. This is exactly contrary to a very widespread im- pression. Many even of those who dislike and condemn war believe quite sincerely that wars are good for us from the trade point of view. This can be said with some certainty, of course, where we are not involved in them. It is then possible to play the part of "the honest broker," to use Lord Palmerston's famous phrase, and to do so at such a profit as could not be made under other conditions. But there is even ground for doubting whether a prosperity thus acquired through the misfortunes of others is really profitable in the long run— to the community, that is, for individuals who are "in the swim" do unquestionably clear out with great fortunes. During the Franco-German War we did more or less well out of both combatants, and the con- clusion of that great conflict brought such a prosperity to this country as had not pre- viously been. Those good years in the early seventies were followed by a deep and long depression in trade. If the losses of the lean years were set against the gains of the fat years, Mr. Norman Angell might find good ground to base an argument to the effect that the apparent profit made out of war by neutral countries is also a great illusion.

There was a discussion On Mr. Norman Angell's theories at the National Liberal Club the other night; and it was by no means one-sided. Mr. G. H. Levy; the veteran political economist, questioned whether the theories of Mr. Angell contain the whole truth. War, he said, makes capital scarce, and the scarcity of capital makes interest higher. This is another way of putting what we have just said about the possibility of making fortunes out of other people's quarrels. But is it to the general interest

that capital should make large profits? That A a question. that even Mr. Levy himself answers in the negative. , The people who are injOrech he says, are the working classes; who have to pay in taxation, interest on loans/ and then to pay off the lean itself. Then, too, wages fall as a consequence of . the rate of interest. As the working classes are the bulk of the community, all wars . must be bad business for them. We are ' brought back to John Bright's simile of the , I stone sent rolling downstairs, and beunding from one step to another until it rests at the bottom. That is how taxation, inap,artially bumping against one class after another; eventually settles on the working man, who, whether he has temporarily profited from war or not, has by one roundabout way or another to " foot the bill."

Lord Welby, who is now becoming a very old man, reminded the gathering which Mr. Norman Angell addressed that ,he has seen five or six wars. lie means, of course, wars of historical importance. Each of them had excited anticipations of good times, and in each instance the anticipation had been dis- appointed. Yet it will be a long time, we fear, before the belief that war is good for trade, is finally extinguished. Practical reftt- tations of this idea there have been in plenty. Lord Welby mentions the latest, the case of South Africa. The Boer War was to be fol- lowed immediately by such a boom in trade as had not been experienced since the seventies. But the boom did not come. The effects of what proved to be a great finan- cial catastrophe had to be lived down before a revival of trade became possible, and we are only now experiencing what the enthusi- asts for war made certain of as an event of a dozen years ago. War, it hardly needs to be pointed out, is to countries which engage in it a heavy tax not only on present re- sources but on posterity. William Pitt is said to have died with a lament on his, lips—" My country; how do I leave my country? " There is still a dispute as to whether these were the exact words; but certainly Pitt had good reason for using them. I-le left his country with a debt of £700,000,000, the result of his contest with Napoleon. That liability still forms the bulk of our National Debt, and the taxpayer of to-day is still paying, and paying heavily, for the warlike statesmanship of more than a century past.

Lady Greenall has been Comm PAPERS AND complimenting us. We Comm POLITICIANS. are "W ar ringt o n's Comic Paper," and the distinction has been earned by no greater effort than was involved in poking a little fun at the Union Jack aprons and caps of lady helpers at "The Dumperies." They looked as if they had been on tour, with the rest of the goods, but Her Ladyship assures us that they "came down, brand new, from London." Then the journey must have dis- agreed with them, for they were nearly ag dingy as some of the exhibits "from abroad." Her Ladyship also charges us with harbour- ing the green-eyed mon ster,and being jealous of the success of the Exhibition. We hasten with a denial. Comic we may be, for "laugh and the world laughs with you," but jealous, no! a thousand times no! The Dump Exhibition was certainly very successful— even remarkably' so—for Free Traders are as pleased with the wholesome lessons it drove home,as the Tariff Reformers profess to be. Dumping lost its terrors, even to the unthinking voter, when he saw for himself what rubbish the foreigner spent his time in making and "dumping I " But we are not the only comic paper, unfortunately. Our newly-acquired title is already in danger of being filched. Last Saturday, the " Observer" tlevoted a lot of space to reporting the sayings of Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., and in one column it credits him with an expression of opinion which certainly requires explanation, unless we are to accept it as merely comic. Did the Member for the Borough really tell the Parr Hall audience (and Sir George Doughty, too) that "He

WOULD BE GLAD IF THE BATTLE COuLD BE BOUGHT TO-MORROW ?"

The " Observer " says so (in capitals as above) and being the official organ of the local Tory party it should know. We should like to put the question to Mr. Smith that he is so fond of putting to speakers on th Liberal side : " Was he correctly reported? We have heard of elections being" bought ' before, and he was certainly referring to the Warrington election. If the report is correct then we can understand why Mr. Smith would be glad if the battle could be "bought to-morrow." He is on the side of the big battalions with the long and deep purses. The prospect of "buying " such a commodity as an election in Warrington would present no difficulty to a party with such unfathomable resources. Why, the other side would hardly be able to raise a bid In imagination we can hear the fate- ful tap of the hammer and the unemotional voice of the auctioneer saying: "Going; GOING, GONE—to Mr. Harold Smith! ' Perhaps he will tell us if he said something and meant something quite different!

In passing a resolution in favour of curtail- ing the hours of London public-houses the London branch of the United Kingdonl Alliance, has based its argument chieflY upon the experiences of Liverpool during the strike and the subsequent report of the Liver- pool justices. The conclusions arrived at iP the Liverpool report are, of course, that opportunity makes the "drunk," and that he is reduced sensibly in numbers when his facilities for obtaining drinlc- are curtailed. The hours of opening in London public-houses are 151 more Per week than in Liverpool, where there are more taverns to the population, and it is argued that the temptations are there- fore more constantly present to a greater number of persons. This supposition (sal° ' the London correspondent of the "Manchester Guardian ") is strengthened by the fact the the convictions for drunkenness per thousand and the death-rate among public-house employees are higher in London than in an) other cities. Scotland Yard does not keel) , account of the number of arrests made froin hour to hour, but Mr. Chancellor, member for an East End constituency, who took the principal part in the discussion, received s letter from the Police Commissioner stating that the larger proportion of the charges ill London are evening charges. These temper- ance reformers ask that the public-houses on week-days shall not open before severl and shall close not later than eleven. To this end they demand either a special Act, or the inclusion of a clause in the promised Licensing Bill.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Assessment Committee was held yesterday morning at the Work- house, Mr A Brooke presiding Others present were: —Messrs A J Jolley, G Mather, W C Brooks, J Billings, W Houghton, A Bottomley (clerk), and E Gunson (valuer). In accordance with instruct lens the valuer submitted a list of the re-valuations of the off- licences in the borough. The list comprises 64 houses whose total gross valuation under the old valuation was £1,097, and the rateable value £1,659. The valuer's report shows the gross valuation at £2.193, and the rateable value at £1,861 10a., which is an increase of £196 on the rpross and £172 on the rate- able. On the proposition of Mr A J Jolley, seconded Iby Mr G Mather, the report was adopted, it being understood that the committee would hear appeals at a later date,

A. large slumber of parents and friends along with the members of the" John Dimond Juvenile Tent," assembled in Fiiars' Green schoolroom, on Monday evening for the annual meeting and the distribution of prizes. Bro. William Lowe (tent superintendent) presided. A short entertainment was given, the following members taking part: Sister Elsie Hardman rendered a song in a very pleasing manner and recitations were given by Sisters Jennie Warhrick, Amy Litdewood and Doris Thompson. Councillor Bro. Rober:. Henshall then addressed the members in a very interesting manner and afterwards distributed the prizes for regular attendance. Wright, was also presented with a Past Chief Ruler's sash for having passed through the whole of the tent offices. At the close of the meeting each person received a bag of fruit. Warrington Photographic Society

"Rambles with a camera" was the title of Mr K. F. Bishop's lecture on Tuesday last, and if the extent of country covered by the lecturer be a justification for a title then this informal talk was rightly named, for it included a gocd many places between Aberdeen and Evesham, our own locality of course, furnishing the greatest share. The difficulty would be to select any section of his lecture for preference. The slides included slum and sea side snap shots, snow hoar frost and ice subjects, a first rate series of pictures of the Roman Wall from New Castle to Carlisle, and what was particularly appreciated a large number of views of Moreton Old Hall, Congleton This beautiful old mansion, now utilised in part as a firm, is free to visitors for three days a week, and is a marvel of preservation. The old moat is still full of water, the black cak beams and rafters, and carving are as perfect as they were three centuries ago, and every corner of the demesne seems to make a picture. The snow and frost section of the lecture was well illustrated by Mr Bishon and many valuable hints were given as to this difficult branch of the amateur photographer's art.

Funeral 01 Mr. William Owen Taylor.

The funeral took place at the cemetery on Monday of Mr. William Owen Taylor, of 20, Arpley-street, who died on Wednesday of last week. Mr. Taylor, who was thirty-seven years of age, occupied a Position in the offices of the Warrington Slate Com- pany and was greatly esteemed by his colleagues. The Rev H Williamson conducted the service. The principal mourners were the father, two brothers and two sisters of this late Mr Taylor, Mr Williams, Mr Poyser, Mr Hughes, Mr Worthington, Mr Jewkes and Mr Warren. The Slate Company were represented by Mr Gordon Roberts,Mr Hayes, Mr Jolley,Mr Bayley and Mr Perrin. The coffin was borne to the grave by members of the staff of the Slate Company. Wreaths were sent by the " Father, mother, brothers and sisters," Mr and Mrs ii Roberts, Mr and Mrs WT Williams, " Garnett, Jessie and Minnie ." " Frey and Alice, " "From Joe" Mr and Mrs Greenhalgh, Mr H Jewkes, Mr A Dutton, Mr W Worthington' the Directors of the Warrington Slate Company Ltd, and the Office staff of the Warrington Slate Company Ltd. The carriages were supplied by Mr Greenough.

CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETIES AND THE

SMALL TRADER.

"The History of Co-operation" formed the subject of a lectme given by Mrs. Philip Snowden in the Co-operative Hall on Thursday evening. It was a matter of regret that the audience was not a numerous one, as Mrs. Snowden is one of the ablest and most eloquent lady speakers of the present day. The chair was eccupied by Mr. J. -Jarman (secretary et the Warrington Co-operative Society), under the auspices of which the lecture was given. The CHAIRMAN said so far as they were concerned in Warrington, they had a very successful society, which was going forward with great strides. They expected to hear bona Mrs. Snowden something of the ideal side of the movement, and of some of the things they ought t) sand for and which very often they forgot. There was a tendency in these days to break away from the ranks f c eoperation and to joint the Labour Party and Socialist Party, b.cause it was stated there was no room in the ranks of co-operation. There was a tremendous amount to be done by the work-re in the matter of organization, and in the co-operative move- ment lay the hope for the future. If they could make all the workers co-partners f their organization there would bean end of labour struggles. Mrs. PHILIP SNOWDEN. who was warmly greeted,said many of the members thought of the movement as simply a shop-keeping movement. On the contrary it was a great national movement and more than that it WAS a gteat iitornational world-side movement, based upon eternal prineipl.s. Every meml'er shiuld be informed upon the grave questions of (COI2OMiCB, and should know us mothieg of business and the duties of citizenship, beeeuse the manner and control

Frain the's . ost MIIS PIIILIP SNOWDEN.

of the governmeet of our towns and even of our Country, was determined to a large extent by the character and prospetiry of tha judividual co-operative societies. In order to understand the birth of their movement they must go back to the days of 150 years ago, the days preceding the industrial revelation. The good old tines in many respects were bad old times, but in some respects they were superior to the present. There was a better relationship between the average m rater and the average workmon. There was a human touch between landlord and tenant which was lacking a metimes in these days. There was not abroad that hateful c mercialism which han destroyed and disfigured the ' life of the se esent day—except where the co-operative spirit had made it preseece fe t. Those were the daye when the worker owned hs own tools and instru- meuts,with which be worked. Afterwards there came the industrial revolution, the discovery of labrea,- savi. g machinery, the discovery of the power of • team in I the invsntion of the railway train. Those inventions created a revolution. It was a well-known economio truth that the way in which a man grt his living, did to a large extent deterrnins the form and character of their institution's, volantary and involun- tary. The industrial co-operative mevernent began in the early years of .the industrial revolution. It war largely helped by the &Torts of one greaegood man Mr Robert Owen rose,whofrone very poor circumstances to a business partner in a fir-n of cotton spinners in Manchester. He combined good business instincts with idealism and it was mainly due to his efforts that the ten hours work day was passed. It was in the year 1843 that the Rochdale pioneers started on the lines of the modern co-operative move:nent. Witi small capital of £2B they opened a little shop in that town. Since that time the co-operative movement had grown to immense pr portions. The present capital in the United Kingdom was fifty mil io: s, thrir annual turn over one hundred millions and a member- ship of three milioe people. They sympathised with the local traders who felt that their business was seriously damaged by the movement of co-operation. Unhappily the law of progress made it impossible to move along the lines of real pro- gress without somehow hurting someone. They wished it was otherwise. They would like to extend their society in order that they might give employ- ment to every local tradesman whose business had been seriously damaged and give him a preference in their employment so that he would trot suffer. They had to move on the lines of progress with as mach forbearance as they could summon. What was the real spiritual inwardness of the movement ? Was it dividend ! No, but she held very strongly that any member of the Society need not be ashamed of taking the dividend, provided the servants of the society were well paid, and a reserve fund pat by, to make it secure from a business point of view. They most not forget the larger purpose of the Co-operative naive- meet. The founders of the movement realised that . only could salvation come to the people as a whole under present industrial conditions, by the application in one form or another of the principle of co-operatioe. The solution of the problem of poverty was the main purpose of the movement and they muet make their society attrac- tive to the very poor. They as members of the society must see that the goods they purchased were produced under fair conditions. She knew of a town in Yorkshire where almost every person was a member of the co-operative society. Ia that town the employers of labour knowing that trade unionism was not wrong, and that co-operation brought to the members a certain amount of benefit, they dropped down the wages of the workers by just as many shillings as were paid in the pound when dividend day arrived. She was not concerned with politics but she was very much concerned with the capitalists who were not co-operators who got themselves into ,Parliament and used their politics to smash the co-operative organization. They were not safe even as a commercial organization until they could get hold of productive enterprises so that they could employ more thee they did employ of their own members. It was important that they should expand and hold together and move forward in the direction of co-operation to apply the principle all round and work together in the eternal cause of their common humanity. (Applause). Questions having been invited, Mr. B Flint It asked if Mrs. Snowden could give them some information with regard to the methods of reaching the poorer classes. He felt strongly that it was a point which they ought to aim at in Warrington. Mr. SgOWDEN said she only knew of one society who attempted the experiment of trying to reach the poorer people and they found it extremely difficult. The poor people were very shy cf leaving the traders to whom they owed credit. Another Questioner : Was it not right that the co-operative movement should be represented in Parliament?

_ Mrs. SNOWDEN : If you F end good co-operators 0 Parliament I think that would do just as well. Do you consider co-operation or monopoly? Mrs. SNOWDEN : In the case of some of monopolies the profits go to a limited number of people, whilst in the cage of the co-operative society the profits and dividend go to the people. On the rroposition of Mr. FERGUSON, seconded by Mr. G. T. IlustivoN a vote of thanks was accorded to Ifrs. Snowden.
