ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

MR. LOADER'S BENEFIT. Mr. A. 11. Loader, the arateemed general naaterger of the theatre, must have felt proud and gratified on Thursday evening. It was the cocasion of hie second annual complimentary benefit, and no more convincing mark of public appreciation of his successful r Tens to prosicle high-class entertainments at the Rylands- street house could have been desired, than the fine audience which assembled to do him honour. Every rt of the theatre was crowded, and many leading tewngpeople were present. A pleasing feature of the betrefitwas the co-operation so willingly extended by the managements of other places of amusement, and the apleudid way in which local artistes else rose to the occasion and gave of their beat. It was a rnonstre proeramme, with sufficient variety to appeal to all, and the show was kept going merrily, without a sign of satires' or weariness, for nearly fair Th • audience was remarkably enthusiastic in its approval, every item being warmly acclaimed. Mr. Tom floothroyd conducted a largely eugerented orchestra which acquitted itself with distinction in the ever- welrenne " Poet and Peasant "overture, the details of light and shade receiving adeqnate attention. A more ambitious item was the grand selec- tion from Wagner's operas " Taunhauser " and " Lehengrin " and, although ths performance was not without blemish, the intricacies of the various movements were neg)tiated with a col- siderable meaauce of 81.11008 i. A feature of tie even- ing in which an exceptional degree of interest centred was the dramatic episode. " The breken string" from the pen of the beneficiaie. The theme was not altogether new, but it was worked out on unorthodox lines and the denouement was powerful and nrexpected Briefly, the story is of a captain of it duatry —Sir Richard Vacce —who, by appropriating the invention f a poor w alcrnan, has amassed untold wealth. His only daughter Margaret,—a very cons& ent ion; young lady with a t uching belief in her father's genius—is to inherit the fortune and already she has begun to make herself a Lady Bountiful to the poor of the district. Then, like a ghoat from the past, the much-wronged inventor —old Ben Bowden —appears cn the Berne. Adveesity has dealt hardly with him and heia reduced to fiddling in the streets to earn the pittance nerea.ary to keep body and seul together. Unwittingly Margaret draws from him the story which convicts her father of a mean theft and, her confidence dr her idol thus rudely destroyed, she insists on his making restitivion by restoring the whole cf the ill-gotten gaina to Bowden. The old man, however, is unwiliing that such a sacrifice should be made arid will only accept on cendition that Margwet will cons-nt to be 1 his heiress. The episode has a tragic (doe-, for just when the wanderer is about to come into po session of the comforts of which be has been so long and cruelly deprived, the string of his beloved violin tinges ominously, and be immediately collapses and dies. The Geis c.le was very creditably presented. kir. Loader himself carrying off chief honours as Ben Hamden. He was the tottering old musician to the life—the pathos of his portrayal creating a profound impression, while his" make up " was a triumph. Mr. Charles D. Law gave further proof of his histrionic talents as Sir Richard Vane —a part which he t lek in a very natural way, the conflicting emotions of annoyance and remorse being well broneht out. Pleasurable nnticipat s had been arouse I by the announcement that Mr. and Mrs. B. S. Townroe would give a dnelegue " The Clown," written by Mr. Townroe. and the item Prole d one of the most delightful on the programme. Mr. Lodge-Perey's Company in Repertoire.

Mr. Lo.lge-Percy's Company has this week presented some choice selections from its admirable repertoire and the many playgoers who have taken the oppor- tunity of renewing their acquaintance with this talented comdination have been well-repoid by per- formances of superior merit. On Monday and Wed: esday the attraction was an adaptatien of Ouida's famous novel " 11)t1s " which sheds a lurid light on the bad side of society. Lord Jura is one of those fine character studies in which Mr. Lodge-Percy revels and he brought out the contrasting moods of easy- going no ,chalance and highly-strung emotionalism in a realistic st% le which betokened the true artiste. Miss Henrietta Schrier also added to her lam els with a delightful p3rtrayal of Fuschist Leach, the candid, vivaciouS American girl. THE MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Concert in the Parr Hall.

It is a pleasure to record that for once in a long reriod the Musical Society had,en Wednesday evening, an audience worthy of its work, its traditions and its objects. That Parr Hall should be well filled in every part on the occasion of this particular concert when others, musically much more deserving have languished for want of it, is difficult to explain, unless, indeed, the reappearance in our midst of Miss Marie Houghton is accountable for the fact, in which case the implied compliment to a young and rising local singer is as flattering as it is undoubtedly deserved. The items which choral members had to sing were neither great in bulk nor very exacting in character. Sir C. Hubert Parry's "Blest pair of Sirens" was the most important, and its difficulties are such as any choir cf average efficiency and sufficient numerical strength can surmount easily. The work, which is described ass choral ode,is probably the finest and most , monumental piece of contrapuntal writing that has, so Lir, been given us by an English composer, yet its total effect iu performance must be described as heavy and wanting in due relief. The society's vocal members sang the work in an appropriately massive manner, making the few crucial points of the middle portion most satisfactorily, and negotiating also in all sections if the voices the intricate involutions of the eight independent parts towards the close in a way which led up to a really effective climax. In regard to the two part-songs, " The World's Hay Queen "and " King Arthur," beard in succession in tne second part of the programme, it was noticeable that the first presented some points of weakness in performance and that it was the less pleasing of the two as a whole. Some uncertainties of intonation between the sopranos and altos, and some murkiness amongst. all the parts in the more involved and quickly moving passages, resulted in a lack of lucidity and a clouding of the part-song's meaning. " King Arthur " is of course in quite a different category. It was however, given in that whole-hearted way which shewt.d that members had taken firm hold of the music and its comical humour, with the result that the audience hugely enjoyed it. The Bridal Chorus, from Cowen's "Rosa Maiden, " sung in quite a finished and tasteful manner, completes the list of the choral items. Miss Marie Houghton was greeted with a vociferous euthurst of applause at the conclusion of each of her songs. Her first was the aria " Rejoice greatly, " an item which seemed curiously inappropriate to the occasion if not altogether out of place in the programme, , and one wondered whether the 'audience themselves, would not have preferred something quite different. That Miss Houghton has greatly advanced herself as a concert singer siuce she was last beard in Warrington is true enough. The ease with which she vocalised Handel's tripping roulades and followed in a facile way the best precedents in interpreting this number, denoted a finished technique and remarkable steadiness in trying circumstances, but one could not help being struck more by the mechanical correctness of the performance than by any degree of the purely personal and emotional element discernible in it. The group of three songs with pianoforte accompaniment, which Miss Houghton sang later in the evening— though none of them of absorbing interest—served to

emphasise some fine qualities in her voice, albeit_they also indicated its limitations. Their dui rendering however, made a call upon Miss Houghtoa's versatility which was met in a way that greatly pleased listeners most lavish in their tokens of appreciation. The playing of the solo violinist, Miss Helena. Kontorovitch was unquestionably the outstanding feature of the concert. Seldom have we heard a

_ matures style in so young a performer, or one who can so completely abandon herself to the task of the moment. In Mendelssohn'e violin concerto a splendid technique was, in the person of Miss Kontorovitch, associated with an appassionata so profound that here, as the " Airs Russes " (Wientawski), one listened to familiar themes as to a new reading.. Everything she did compelled wonder and aduairatices and drew from the audience applause the most enthusiastic. The society's orchestra, augmented as ssital in its various sections, had some very respossible and it was noticeable that in the concerto it was more than usually successful in subordinating itself, thus contributing to a most artistic interpretation of a work at once supremely graccfnl awl thoroughly characteristic of its composer. The overture to "William Tell" ani the Peer Gynt Suite were, on the whole, effectively and, in some respects, adequately performed, incidentally reflecting high credit upon Mr.. Crossley the scciety's conductor, under whose baton some arduous work in rehearsal must leave been done. Bet this, the second or third time of hearing these things performed by the society, makes clear the fact that, where the amateur element so largely pre- dominates amongst instrumentalists, there must always remain a residuum of the music which cannot be purged of faults of intonation and evidences of indefiniteness—thin places, as one may call them, which the momentum of professional leaders may be counted upon to carry us over with a minimum of discomfort. The well-known Barcarolle from •Offenbach's "Tales of Hoffman" proved pita a goal amongst the orchestral items. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mas. Bac , played the pianoforte accompaaiments to the violin solos excellently, and Mr. It. H. Pearson acted as leader of the orchestra is his customary efficient manner.

TUE STELLA CAROL CONCERT.

Our readers are reminded of the musical treat which Mr R. H. Pearson has arranged for Wednesday evening next, tThen Sells Carol, the marvellous young prima donna, and a strong concert party will appear at the Parr Hall. Stella Carol, who is only fifteen years of age, was discovered singing in the streets one Christmas Eve by Madame Amy Sherwin,thanks to whose discrimination and sympathy ' she has been put into the way of an artistic career. I Her voice is a high, clear soprano, and her chief per- formances lie in the demain of bravura singing. She has A charming personality and the technical attributes of a born smist. The other artistes will be :—Mr. Fraser Gauge, Scottish basso cantate ; Miss Dorothy Webster, contralto; Mr. Clement Harvey, a pianist of consider- able attainments; Miss Lilian Fonlis, an extremely clever young Scottish violinist; Mr. Henry Fransella,. the well-known solo flautist of the Royal Opera,, Covent Garden; and Miss Amy Watts, an elocutionist who has secured numerous high awards. Reserved seats may be booked at Mr. It. 11. Pearson's, Bridge- street.

WEDDING OF WARRINGTON'S MAYORESS.

A UNIQUE EVENT AT TRINITY CHURCH.

The old church of Holy Trinity, Warring- ton, was the scene of a picturesque wedding of unique interest on Tuesday afternoon, when Miss Phyllis Joseph, only daughter of Councillor G. W. Joseph, M.D., J.P., Mayor of Warrington, was married to Mr. Arnold Nield, second son of the late Councillor J. J. Nield and of Mrs. Nield, of Warrington. The occasion will be long and happily remembered and rightly so, for it was the first time in the history of the borough that the townspeople had had the opportunity of witnessing the marriage of their Mayoress during her term of office. The bride enjoys a well-deserved popularity with all classes of the community, the graceful and tactful manner in which she has discharged her civic duties having given complete and general satisfaction. It was, therefore, not surprising that all Warrington was interested in Tuesday's ceremony—in fact no local wedding of recent years has aroused such widespread attention. At all the municipal buildings flags were displayed in honour of the event, the public officials enjoyed a holiday, and the schools were closed at noon. For close upon an hour before the time fixed for the wedding, the approaches to tho church were thronged with spectators eager to obtain a glimpse of the bridal party. Within the church a great congregation had assembled. The nave was reserved for the invited guests, while the side aisles and gallery were crowded with the general public. Many members of the Town Council, officials and other prominent townspeople were present. The chancel and altar rails were tastefully decorated with white flowers, palms and plants, the general effect being one of striking beauty. Only the sun's rays were needed to complete the brilliance of the picture, but on a mid-winter afternoon it was sufficient cause for congratulation that the weather was fine and dry. During the assembly of the guests, Mr. W. B. Randall, organist of the church, played the following choice voluntaries with taste and skill : " Priere Offertoire " (Paul Devred); 'Wedding Procession" (H. Hofmann); 'Saint D'Amour " (Elgar); "Ad Herselt " (Lemaire),"Wedding " (Guilmant).

feathers to match. The dresses were made by Messrs. W. Hodgkinson, Ltd. Dr. Harry Nield, of Liverpool (brother of the bridegroom) discharged the duties of best man and Mr. Cecil Joseph (brother of the bride) acted as groomsman, The service, which was fully choral, was most imprPssive. The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton officiated throughout and after he had given the marriage blessing the hymn "0 perfect I love" was sung kneeling. At the close of I the service the choir gave an expressive rendering of the anthem "Teach me, 0 Lord" (Attwood). As the happy couple left the church, the strains of Mendelssohn's Wedding March pealed forth. The crowd of well- wishers assembled in Sankey-street showered confetti on the bride and bridegroom as they entered their carriage. Afterwards there was a reception at the Masonic Hall, which had been profusely decorated with scarlet tulips and greenery. The wedding presents—a splendid collection including silver, china, lace and paintings— were on view and were greatly admired. Conspicuous among them were the silver tea and coffee service, presented by members of

A few moments after two o'clock the bridal procession moved along the centre aisle. The Mayor's Chaplain (Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, M.A.), and the choir met the bride and her father at the church door and preceded them to the chancel, while the hymn "The Voice that breathed o'er Eden" was sung. Follow- ing were the two churchwardens, Mr. John Hesketh and Mr. Welsby, carrying the ancient staves with gilded "cups." Then came the Mayoress leaning on the arm of her father, by whom she was given away. She looked very charming in an exquisite dress of rich white liberty satin.A fichu of Limerick lace draped the bodice, and panels of the same beautiful lace on the skirt were fastened with clusters of orange blossom. The long train of lustrous satin had the initial "N " in one corner, worked in pearls and bridal flowers. The dress was finished at the waist with a brilliant and pearl rope girdle, the gift of a friend. A dainty cap of tulle and orange blossom crowned her hair, and a long Brussels veil completed the trousseau. Her ornaments included a diamond and emerald ring, the gift of the bridegroom, a link of pearls and diamond earrings. She carried a sheaf of Madonna ilies, presented to her by the bridegroom. The bride was attended by three brides- maids—Miss Beatrice Nield t(the little niece of the bridegroom),Miss Dorothy May Wright (daughter of Dr. and Mrs Wright, Spellow- lane, Liverpool), and Miss Beatrice Sankey (daughter of Councillor and Mrs. Sankey, Warrington). The junior bridesmaid was daintily dressed in white satin, with a rucked and gathered white satin hat, and carried a white satin cushion, upon which the bride knelt at the altar. Miss Wright and Miss Sankey were beautifully gowned in Saxe blue satin. Their over-dresses of filet net in the same soft colour were fringed with beads, and the yokes and sleeves were composed of filmy ivory-toned lace. On their hair they wore bands of Oriental embroidery with white ospreys. They wore gold and pearl brooches, and carried shower bouquets of La France roses, the bridegroom's gifts. Ttie .Examiner, Saturd y, February, 3, 1912. POPULAR SCIENCE.

MOON IND CLOUDS. The old notion that the lull moos clears away clouds is no longer held by meteoro- logists. The view of the present day is con- firmed by Mr. W. Ellis, F.R.S., from the Greenwich records, which show that a maxi- mum of cloudiness in the forenoon and a minimum in the evening is the general rule, so that moonlit evenings, instead of being clearer than others, have their freedom from cloud brought to notice by the moon's brightness.

THE LATEST A.zuorLims. Besides the usual hand-operated steering and balancing devices, Captain Lucas- Girardville, a French inventor, has designed special gyroscopic apparatus to operate smaller supplementary balancing rudders to prevent undue pitching or rolling of his unique biplane. He seeks also to eliminate the, upsetting gyroscopic action of his motors and propellers by using two separate motors revolving in opposite directions, one operating a pulling propeller and the other

AEROPLANE WHICH IS BALANCED BY GYROSCOPES.

a pushing propeller. The captain has ap- plied that property of the gyroscope which causes the instrument to gyrate at right angles to a disturbing force applied to its axis of rotation. To this end the inventor has mounted two gyroscopes, each driven by a flexible cable, and arranged so that it may turn with its axis of rotation moving in a plane vertical to the shaft on which it revolves. Each gyroscope operates its own biplane rudder through suitable , gearing, and the action of the rudder is to restore quickly and Cffectively the state of equili- brium which preceiled the action az a dis- turbing force.

AIR FOR FIREMEN.

A remarkable device for supplying air to firemen in smoke-filled rooms, the invention of James Halloran, a New York fireman, has emerged successfully from ite,- first test. It was used by the brigade in fighting a fire in the cellar of a tenement house against thick volumes of smoke. The invention is cha- racterised by the greatest simplicity. A common garden hose is used, one end hang- ing free in the outside air, the other end being attached to a small box on the nozzle of the firemen's water hose. Three rubber tubes lead from the box, with nose-pieces attached, and each is fixed over the faces of the firemen. The bottom of the box on the nozzle is perforated, and the force of the water rushing through the nozzle draws air by suction through the garden hose and feeds it to the three firemen holding the nozzle.

Fon STARTING AEROPT.ANEB.

The commencement of an aeroplane flight is at present an undertaking requiring the help of several attendants, including a man to start the engine by turning the pro- peller, and the men sometimes, to hold the machine back until the motor reaches the required speed. To dispense with these aids, a French airman has designed a simple apparatus consisting of a stout rope pro- vided with a spring-actuated clutch. One end of the rope is attached to the frame- work of the machine in such manner that it cannot get in the way of the mechanism, and the other end, with the clutch attached, is engaged with a short piece of rope made fast to any solid object on the ground. Ths, spring tension of the clutch is so calculated that the clutch opens when the propeller reaches its maximum speed, and the machine, thus released, sails off into the air.

BRAINS A r THEM FINGER-TIPS.

The wonderful sensibility possessed by the finger-tips of blind people has long been a matter of remark. The reason for this is thus explained by a doctor, and the illustra- tion supports his theory:—" If you examine the inside of your finger-tips with a magni- fying-glass of even moderate power you will find the skin to be in narrow ridges. Standing point up beneath all these ridges are the so-called ' corpuscles of Pacini.' Each Pacisian corpuscle contains a nerve. trunk, an artery and a vein. It is chiefly in these localities that it performs its func- tion of touch. Not long since I had the op- portunity of examining under a microscopa portions taken from the inner surface of th.. index and middle fingers of the right hand

I G. 1 , - I G. 2 , •

===== 0002601_19120203_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
NODMAL AND BLIND MAN'S FrNGEIL-TIPS.

of a blind man, whose sense of touch had been abnormally developed. On beholding the highly magnified Pacinian corpuscle ' I observed, instead of a single nerve-trunk, artery, and vein, as in Figure 1, an infinite number of dainty and minute nerve-twigs branching off from the main-stem, as in Figure 2 (arteries and veins being omitted for purposes' of clearness). Extreme and constant employment of this sense of touch in the blind man, and greater use of his finger-tips, cause blood to tend towards the latter in unusual quantities. Blood means growth. Hence in the blind there is an extraordinary development of the 'corpuscle of Paeini '--its nerve-trunk, artery, and vein—and this more perfect growth means more perfect performance of function."

UNTARNISHAELE SILVER WANTED. Silver is a metal that has its problems. The tarnishaylity of silver, particularly to people who live in the smoke-enveloped cities of the present day, is a time and muscle consuming annoyance. It is not sur- prising, then, that a prominent company manufacturing silverware should inquire as to the possibility of adding small quantities of other elements to silver, with the idea of lessening or eliminating its tendency to tarnish.

'S THEFT OF CIGARETTES.

- - - At tho Children's Court on Saturday, Stanley Lewis, aged 12, son of Ada Forster, 2, Buckley's-yard, was charged with stealing by false pretences from a shop 113, Sankey-street a box containing 50 packets of Woodbine cigarettes, value 4s. 2d., the property of Margaret Jane Allford, of 18, Heaton-street, Runcorn. The Chief Constable stated that on Sunday evening the boy went to the shop of Mrs. Allford and said he had been sent by Mrs. Lewis, an ice-cream dealer, for a box of Woodbine cigarettes. She handed him the cigarettes. Afterwards it was ascertained that the boy had not been sent by Mrs. Lewis, but that he had gone to the shop on his own account and had taken the cigarettes to his grandmother, who tried to sell the goods to various people. He bad told her that he bad won them from another boy through playing pitch. Prisoner was ordered to receive four strokes with the birch, and the magistrates told the grandmother to keep a stricter watch over him in the future.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, William Jones, 5, Six Houses, Dallam-lane, was charged with having committed an aggravated assault on his wife by striking her on the head nith a shoe and a pair of tongs. Complainant said prisoner went home on Saturd97, and gave her 3e. 10d. and she gave him ninepence back. He then went out and returned about 3 o'clock and sent for two pints of beer. About 9 o'clock he asked for tke the remainder of the money and snatched at her (witness's) pocket, which contained 4.5. 7d., and tore it away. He again went out and returned in about half an hour and commenced to threaten her. He kept on threatening her until about 11 o'clock. He then got hold of her and knocked her over a chair, and after- wards pushed her under the table and hit her with an old shoe. Ile also struck her with a pair of tongs. He had a knife in his band and said be would "finish her out and out." Ile tried to draw the knife across her throat but after some struggling she got hold of the knife and he then said he would strangle her. She screamed and sent her boy for a policeman. She was afterwards attended at the infirmary. Prisoner said all the bother was caused by complainant taking a table cloth off the line and going to pawn it for drink. Ile denied having brandished the knife. William Jones, son of the prisoner, said there was bother nearly every Saturday night. He corroborated the evidence of the last witness. P.C. Steel said that he wept to the house of prisoner about 12 o'clock on Saturday night. On the I way down to the Police Station it took him and another officer all their time to keep prisoner from his wife. Prisoner, who made his 15th appearance, was sent to prison for three months with hard labour.

At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, Albert Antrobus (16) and Percy Antrobus (IP, of 70, Oldham- road, were charged with stealing a quantity of cigarettes and sweets on November 2nd and 15th from two shops in Grove-street, and Albert was also charged with stealing sweets, cigarettes and cash, to the value of 13s. on January 17th, from a shop in linutsford-road. —Arthur Tomlinson (15), 4, Old-road, was charged with receiving a quantity of cigarettes and sweets knowing them to have been stolen.—All the three pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable stated that the police had made the charge, ono of simple larceny instead of shop-breaking. The earliest date on which the elder boy commenced that career was November 2nd, and there was no doubt that he had led the other two astray. They appeared to have been getting up early in the morning and going into the shops and going back to bed again. Two or three cases of shops being broken into had occurred in a week. The elder prisoner seemed to have done the major portion of breaking into the shops. Tile parents had no know- ledge of the thefts. The lads were placed under probation for twelve months.

SOCIAL SIDE-LIGHTS FROM PARISH REGISTERS.

Two words carried into the office the verdict of a suffering public: " D—n Napoleon." The same risk hangs over us as we resume upon Parish Registers. But the subject is Marriage, and we venture.

PUTTING UP THE BANNS.

If long usage can -ma-e- a thing familar "Putting up the Banns" is a familiar pro- cess in England. For seven centuries at least it has been the law of the Church that no marriage can be contracted "without banns thrice published in the Church." Such was the law as early as A.D. 1200. The word Banns is Anglo-Saxon for Proclamation, and a later rule decreed that the times of declaration should be on "Lord's day or festivals distant from each other."

===== 0002601_19120203_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
" CLOSE " SEASONS.

The " Askings " could be made at any time, but not so the marriage In earlier days the Church had its close seasons, if we may so use the word, and forbade marriages under pains and penalties.

The first forbidden season was, of course, Lent, fixed as early as A.D. 365 at the Council of Laodicea. Two other seasons were afterwards stated, but the Council of Trent deleted them, keeping only Advent and Lent. The Church in England seems curiously to have followed the older usage and held to three close seasons.

lhese will be best understood of the average reader if we state them in the rhyming language written by the Vicar of Everton in his register. Advent marriage doth deny But Hilary gives thee liberty. Septuagesima says thee nay, Eight days from Easter says you may. R,igation bids thee to contain But Trinity sets thee free again. And there is another quaint verse in the Beverley Register under date 1641 which also states the old rule : When Advent comes do thou refraine Till Hilary sett ye free againe ; Next Septuagesima saith tbe nay ; But when Low Sunday comes thou may, Yet at Rogation thou must tarrie Till Trinitie shall bid thee mary.

The singular thing is that England alone in Western Christendom adopted the period before Pentecost as a forbidden season.

Nor could marriage take place save during the forenoon, the hours between eight and twelve being duly decreed. This rule arose from the fact that the Nuptial Mass formed part of the ceremonial and Mass could only be said in the morning. Under this rule we hai to manage until the new law of 1888. Marriages are generally entered in the old Registers with great care. Whatever slovenliness there may be in the entries of baptisms and burials, the marriages are recorded with great regularity. We perhaps owe this to the fact that they would be written in the t presence of the contracting parties who,with future possibilities in mind, would see that a true record was made.

But strange to say, in the old registers the ages of the man and woman are very seldom given. This is the more curious seeing that the Church has always been careful to define the age at which marriage can take place. In the Middle Ages infancy was supposed -to end at seven years and children could then give consent for espousals, but espousal was not actual marriage and the contract could only be completed when the youth had reached 14 and the maiden the age of 12. This held until the reign of George 11. when the age was fixed at 21 for each sex, though with the consent of parents an earlier age was allowed.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
A WEDDING EXTRAORDINARY.

Child marriage and the reasons lying behind them have been mentioned too often in this column to afford novelty to our readers, and in our search for the unusual we may pass to records of another kind. Here is a long ently—which was surely justified under the circumstances—from a register at Leicester under date February 15th, 1576. Thomas Tibye and Ursula Russel were maryed ; and because the earl° Thomas was and is naturally deafe and also dumb°, so that the order of the form of marriage used usually among others, which can heare and speake,could not for his parte be observed. The said Thomas, for the expressing of his mind irts'ead cf words, of his own accord used these sigrs ; first he embraced her with his arms, ard took her by the band, putt a ring upon her finger, and laydo his hande upon his heart, and then upon her heartet and held up his handes toe ard heaven. And to I show his continuance to dwell with her to his lyves ende,he did it by closing of his eyes with his "'armies and digging out of the earth with his fcote and pulling° as though lie would ring a bell, with diverse other signs. Everyone will agree that " the said Thomas " explained himself very well. Another case in London is recorded a few years later and left the minister so nervous that he consulted the Lord Chief Justice as to its legality. He wrote in the register that the man took the Book of Common Prayer and his license in one band and his bride in the other . . . and made the best signs he could to show that he was willing to be married.

The rector wrote a long description and concluded "This marriage is set down at large because we never had the like before."

MARRIAGE CURIOSITIES.

Of what may be called, for convenience, wedding curiosities, the Registers of England show many excellent examples, the record being held in all probability by the old village of Dudbrook in Essex. Here a slab sets forth that . _

----Robert Hogan was the husband of seven wives successively. Merciful indeed to his memory to add that last word 1

But he was sufficiently besbed by Mary Blewitt of whom the parish register says when she was buried.

Mary Blewitt, ye wife of nine husbands successively, buried eight of yin, but last of all ye woman dy'd and was buried May 7th, 1681. Note. - This was her funeral text. Again that merciful word "successively.'' But did the parson forbear a smile when he read as text "Last of all the woman died also." -

For sheer curiosity this last quoted entry probably holds the first place, but in its power to amaze and stupify one from the register at Sheldon surely stands supreme. 6th January, 1753. The Man was 14_ years of ago. _ . _ _

Marry'd Cornelius_ White and Ellen Dale,

of Sheldon. The woman 70.

A Derby antiquarian was so sceptical con- cerning this record that he looked up the "Derby Mercury "of the period. He found

there an account of the wedding which showed the bride to be a widow and a gentle- woman. She was so incapable, from a variety of disorders, as to be unable to walk to church and was carried there in her chair. At a dance which followed the wedding feast she called for her crutches, commanded her husband to dance and shuffled herself as well as she could. But she was so infirm that death overtook her within a few days and the monthly news sheet which described the wedding described her funeral. The newspaper gives no hint of the family plot that surely must have been behind this amazing marriage. ROMANCE READY MADE.

Of the raw material of romance there is a certain amount in every register. We will copy a record just as it stands in the parish book of Bermondsey. 1604 August. The forme of a solemn vowe made betwixt a man and his wife; the man bavinge bene long absent, through which occasion tie woman being married to another man, took her again as followeth. The man's speech— Elizabeth, my beloved wife, I am right sorie that I have so long absented mysealfe from thee, whereby thou shouldst bo occasioned to take another man to thy husband. Therefore I do Pow vowe and promise in the sight of God and this companie, to take the again as my cune ; and will not onley forgive thee, but also dwell with thee, and doe all other duties unto thee as I promised at our marriage. The womat.'s speech—Ralph my beloved husband, I am right ecrie that I have in thy al,sence taken another man t be my husband ; but here, before God and this compauie, I do ren-unce and forsake him, and do promiee to keep mysealfe only usto thee during life, aid to per- form all duties which I first promised unto thee in marriage. A TWICE-MARRIED COUPLE.

Halifax has a much more remarkable story to tell and the register shows accounts of a woman being twice married to the same man. It is comparatively modern history and is surely without parallel. On the Ist of October 1827, Samuel Lumb was married to Mrs. Rachael Heap. She had been previously married to a soldier who was supposed to be dead. Mr. Lumb and his wife lived together in Halifax and had three children. But after a few years her first husband returned alive and well, and claimed his wife. It was a difficult situation, and Mr. Lumb met it by offering to buy his wife from her true husband The bargain was made and was ratified in public, the woman being placed in a halter and delivered from one man to the other at Halifax Cross. The death of Mr. Heap made it possible to make a legal mirriage, and twenty-five years after the first wedding, a second one was solemnized.

As though this silver wedding celebration were not sufficiently curious in itself it had the • added novelty that the bridegroom's grandson gave away the bride, for Mr. Lumb had been previously married and had a grandson old enough to give away his bride.

SMOCK AND SHIFT MARRIAGES. The most pitiful of all marriage customs and the one most likely to jar upon our modern instincts is that which allowed women to be married in a state of practical nakedness. A stupid and vulgar error which held the public mind for several centuries was to the effect that a man was not liable for a woman's debts provided ho married her in no clothing save a smock or shift. Such marriages were common and a description of one of the year 1774 at Saddle- worth will be the best illustration of an extraordinary custom. On Tuesday sen'night was married Abraham Brooks a widower, cf about 30 years of age to Mary Brodley a widow of near 70 but as the bride was a little in debt the Bridegroom obliged her to be married with her shift, and the weather being very severe ti-row her into such a violent fit of shaking as induced the compassionate minister to cover her with his coat whilst the marriage was solemni-ed. Samuel Sttaies, curate. One is grateful to Samuel Stones, but the marvel is that the clergy would tolerate any- thing so unseemly. Two entries of widely differing dates may be quoted as describing smock weddings. 1547, August 4. Here was wedded early in the morning Thomas Munslow, smith, and Alice Nycols, which wedded to him in her smock and bareheaded. 1714. John Bridmore and Anne Selwood were married October 17th. The aforesaid Anne Selwood was married in her smock and without any clothes or headgier on. "Headgier " certainly is not missing from modern weddings. But whence came the idea that the semi- nakedness of the bride saved the bridegroom from debt-claims? Our own reading has not supplied a clue.

SHOT DEAD AT Burrs. During rifle practice at the Curragh Camp rifle butts on Wednesday, Private Albert Percy Burton, of the sth Dragoon Guards, was shot dead. The men, it appears, were supposed to be firing with dummy cartridges at a disc held op by Burton, but in some way the live cartridges became mixed with the dummies, with the result that a bullet pierced the brain of the unfortunate

By Philip Snowden, M.P. THE LABOUR PARTY CONFERENCE. The Annual Conference of the Labour Party which was held in Birmingham last week was an eminently successful gathering. It was large in numbers and remarkable for its unanimity and good feeling. Those who were hoping that the differences which had shown themselves in the party during the last year particularly on the question of the Insurance Bill, would give rise to acrimoni- ous debates have been disappointed. Even on that very controversial matter there was practical unanimity, the Conference without a division 'condemning the Insurance Act as being in certain respects unjust in its burdens, unfair in its treatment of the most needy,and finally urging that the party ought to demand the abolition of contributions so as to make the measure a non-contributory one This resolution of the Labour Party Con- ference was a complete endorsement of the action of the minority of the Parliamentary party who opposed the Bill. THE FRANCHISE QUESTION. •

A very large number of questions engaged the attention of the Conference. A resolu- tion was passed declaring that no Franchise Bill would be acceptable to the Labour Party which did not include women. This means that the Labour Party in Parliament are instructed by the Conference to vote against any Government measure for giving more votes to men unless the sex disqualification now imposed by law upon women is removed This decision of the Labour Party should be a serious warning to the Government not to ignore the rights of women when drafting the Reform Bill which they have promised for the coming session. THE OSBORNE JUDGMENT.

The operation of the Osborne judgment which makes the use of trade union funds for political purposes illegal does not appear as yet to have had much effect in reducing either the income of the Labour Party or the enthusiasm of the movement for Labour Represent Won. Most of the unions against whom injunctions have been given have found some means of paying their contribu- tions to the Labour Party, and of finding the money to send their delegates to this Confer- ence. A certain amount of dissatisfaction was expressed that the Labour Party in ' Parliament had not shown more energy in demanding from the Government some legis- lation for the alteration of the present state of the law •in this matter. The determin- ation of the delegates not to sit quietly down under judge made law was shown in a resolution which was passed calling upon all trade unions affiliated to the Labour Party to defy the law until the Osborne judgment is repealed. It is not likely that the trade union officials will carry out that resolution to the extent of getting themselve§ put into prison, but it does indicate a strong determination on their part to have the law so altered that trade unions may collect and spend their funds freely on any legal object.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RECENT STRIKES.

The "industrial unrest," as it is called, naturally occupied a good deal of attention, and a number of resolutions were passed on questions arising out of it. The debate on the employment of the military in times of trade disputes showed how very deep is the ' feeling which has been aroused among the working classes by the action of the authori- ties in sending down troops into disaffected areas. Nobody could listen to the views and feelings which were expressed on this matter without being convinced that the employ- ment of troops during strikes and lockouts may do much more to excite riot than to maintain peace. The authorities would be well advised to take serious note of the temper of the working classes on this ques- tion. The resentment at the proposal to enroll special constables for service during disputes was hardly less emphatic than the protest against the use of troops. THE GENERAL STRIKE.

There was very little evidence in the Con- ference that the delegates were imbued with the ideas of the Syndicalists on the question of a general strike. It is true that there was a unanimous opinion that in no circumstances must the right of the workers to strike be taken away from them or in any way impaired. But there was an utter absence of that idea which is being promulgated in some quarters that the strike must be the great weapon of emancipation. It was the opinion rather that the strike is a regrettable necessity, but not a means to be either admired or desired. The faith of the Confer- ence was rather in the peaceful ways of political action. Only in one resolution did the Conference give even an indirect encouragement to the idea of a general strike, and that was in the very exceptional circum- stance of a war. It was resolved that the Executive of the Party should consider and report on whether and how tar a stoppage of work in countries about to engage in war would be effective in preventing the out- break of hostilities. This resolution met with considerable opposition, but it was ultimately passed on the understanding that it did not commit the party to more than an enquiry into the matter. THE DAILY PAPER.

The Conference gave its hearty and unani- mous support to the proposal to establish a daily paper in the interests of the Labour movement. This is the biggest business proposition over made by the working class movement in Great Britain. A company is to be formed with a nominal capital of £150,000. It was announced that a sum of about £60,000 has been promised already before the prospectus has been issued. There ought to be enough support in the country to run a daily Labour paper successfully, but if that should happen it will be the first time that a journal owned and run by a party lorganisation has been made a financial success.

A PRACTICAL QUESTION. There y Con- ference unanimous wwi ta hs ount e derebasoteluatinodn pbays as e b vote which might very well have received more 'detailed consideration. That was a resolution urging the Labour Party in and out of Parliament to press for the nationalisa- tion of th4l Railways, Canals, and Mines. It is very little use such resolutions being passed unless they are acted upon. There never was a time when an agitation for these reforms was so favourable as it is at present. The labour troubles in the coal trade and on the railways have convinced a good many people of all classes that it is desirable that these great public services should be under public control. It is to be hoped that the Labour Party resolution will not be forgot ton. There ought to be a strong agitation for these reforms now as an alternative to the use of the strike to get decent labour conditions. THE GOOD RELATIONS. Of course the usual hardy aunuals were passed, such as resolutions on the Right to , Work, Unemploymenr, Education, Labour

===== 0002601_19120203_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
"GOODALLS"

DECORATIONS CARPETS CURTAINS FURNITURE

KING STREET, MANCHESTER.

Exchanges, and the like, but the value and impressiveness of the Conference was in emphatic views it expressed on questions of immediate practical importance. The Con- ference, in my opinion, made a few Mistakes, particularly in its decision on the question of contributions, but on the whole it was the most hearty and harmonious Conference of the Labour Party yet held. The relations between the two sections of the party—the trade unionists and the Socialists—are friendly and sympathetic as was shown, to take one instance only, by the reception which was given by both sections to the Secretary of the German Social Democratic Party who was a fraternal visitor to the Conference. As a further instance of how things are moving it may be mentioned that Mr. Arthur Henderson, who is fond of des- cribing himself as an old fashioned trade unionist, was appointed Secretary of the British Section of the International Socialist Bureau. The trade unionist and the Socialist movements on the continent are practically one and the same thing, and the tendency towards a similar indentity is more marked in this country than seemed likely to come about a few years back.

THIS WEEK'S STORY.

When students sit for which is called their Arts Examination they are questioned with a view to ascertaining their general informa- tion. In this "general information "exami- nation some very curious questions are asked and the students are often very sorely tried. Lately the examiner put down the ques- tion "If Oliver Cromwell were living what would be his opinion of Tariff Reform 9" It was a poser. But a bright idea occurred to the student, and he wrote : "If Oliver Cromwell were living he would be much too old to take any interest in the subject."

THE DIFFERENCE.

Eight fingers, Ten toes, Two eyes, And one n. se, Baby said, When she Emelt the rcse, "Oh, what a pity I've only one nose." Twelve teeth In even rows Lots of dimples, And one nrse. Baby said, When she smelt the snuff, " Deane me ! One is enough." A BEAVER'S DAY'S WORK.

I suppose all my nephews and nieces have read something about the beaver and his splendid teeth. He can cut down big trees by eating through them, and can then divide them up and place them just where he wants them to make a home or to form a dam across a stream. Not long ago at the Zoological Gardens they allowed a young beaver to attack a tree growing in the Park and watched him to see how rapidly he could work. The tree trunk was 12 feetdong and ten inches thick. This is what happened. It was just 12 o'clock when the beaver was let loose and he began by taking off the bark from the bottom of the tree about a foot above ground. That done he attacked the wood. He worked hard, alternating his labor with dips in his bathing .pond. He bathed and labored alternately until 4 o'clock in the afternoon, when he ate his supper of bread and carrots and paddled about in his pond until half- past 5 o'clock.

Ten minutes later, when only one inch of the tree's diameter remained intact, he bore upon his work and the tree fell. Before it fell the beaver ran as men run when they have fired a blast. Then as the tree lay on the ground, he portioned it out mentally, and again began to gnaw. He worked at intervals all night, cut the log into three parts, rolled two of the portions into the water and reserved the other third for his permanent shelter. The work done he took a bath. He had cut down a tree and divided it into three short logs in much less than 25 hours. And all with his teeth.

BUSINESS OUTLOOK IN WESTERN CANADA.—The out outlook of general business in Cunada for 1912 is bright throughout the whole of the four Western Provinces. With the combined forces of Euch far-reaching adver- tising campaigns as those outlined by the Dominion Government, the big railway corporations, the provincial governments, cities, towns and districts all working for population, industry and capital, favour- able results are bound to ensue. A keen interact is being displayed for the control of the home market 137 home industries throughout tha land, and with this patriotic desire for the purchase of Canadian goods— tne product of Canadian labour and resources—Western Canada will receive a goodly share of the general development in 1912.

Already the evening papers held the brief report of their engagement. And soon all the world would know it, and envy him. When he arrived he was shown into the drawing-ruoia. His pulses were throbbing with happiness, his whole being wnis strung to a high pitch of gratified pride aid love.

"While the man who broke my sister's heart is still alive I can never give up the search," Jaspar said in a low voice. "Though it happened twenty years ago, when I was a child, I took the quest on from my father. The man who married Alys and deserted her and her child in London, must be proved to be dead before I throw up my search." "But what clue have you?" Lady Mary said. "If he married her in Australia it might have been under an assumed name, as he intended to desert her in London."

But her hard, handsome face flushed slightly as she said:—

"Thank you Miss Frant," he replied, then hie eyes wandered involuntarily to the door. A throb of envy shot through Isobel Frant. She, in all the superb radiance of the amber velvet gown she was wearing, might not have existed so far am the man before her was concerned. Yet she knew that the des• perate pain .of jealousy had made her surpass• herself that evening, and that her dark glow- ing beauty had never looked so perfect. When they were in the motor on their way to Lady Mary Stainer's' Clifford Hawke, glancing at the lovely profile of his fiancée, was sure of his power to kindle the flame of love in her. He laid his hand on hers, glad .of the few momenta' privacy the drive to Cadogan - gardens was giving them. Mr. Traine and Miss Frant were following them in another motor.

"That a man called Paul Steinway was a witness to the marriage in Australia," Jas- par said. "If I can find him I shall learn the name of the man who must one day answer to me for the heart of the woman he de- serted,"

"Sweetheart, are you pleased with your ring?" he said softly. .Stella turned smilingly to him. "Yes, of course I am, Clifford,' she said quietly. "It is quite perfect." Her voice was gentle and low, and yet the man's ears, straining for a note of tenderness, found it cold and unre- sponsive. He would have preferred a flush of happy shyness on the pale cheek, a gleam of love in the deep blue eyes. And Stella herself, as her hand rested in that of her future husband, felt that her heart lay dead and cold in her body. Grati- tude for his attention, queenly acceptance of his caresses, was all that she could give him. Even the touch of his strong hand roused no responsive thrill in her cool fingers. Then the motor pulled up suddenly before a house that seemed literally to blaze with light. Across the stretch of pavement be- tween the kerb and the doorway was a bright awning, and a small crowd of passers- by lined the way along which the guests would pass. The big red motor attracted a lot of at- tention, and as Hawke stepped on to the red baize and helped Stella out, a low murmur of admiration rose from the group of interested spectators, the vision in white chiffon and silver being lovely enough to stir the blood of the most sluggish Londoner. As Stella walked beside her fiancé there was a sudden movement from the crowd, and the next moment a girl in a black dress swayed suddenly forward and fell in a dead faint across the strip of red baize. Stella uttered a low cry of alarm and bent over the still form. _ .

She looked at him euriouely. "And what will you do? It is foolish to dish up an old scandal."

• Jaspar Tennant's eyes flashed like steel. "When we meet face to face I shall know how to deal with him, and as he showed no mercy to a helpless, loving woman, neither shall I show mercy to him.'' (To be Continued.)

_ "Clifford! help me to cary her in," sho said quickly, "she has fainted, and looks so delicate, poor girl." "Nonsense, clearest," he replied quickly, "Toll can't possibly take this poor girl into Lady Stainer s." "We can't leave her here," Stella said firmly. "I know Lady Stainer won't mind her being brought in, she looks so ill and miserable, poor girl. Please help me, Clifford." _ _

Hawke hesitated. It was the first request the woman he loved had made to him, and yet he could not obey it. The idea of lift- ing that slim, pathetic figure in his arms was absolutely repulsive to him. He beckoned to one of the footmen who stood ex- pectantly waiting some decisive action before taking any responsibility on their own shoul- ders. At Hawke's gesture they moved for- ward quickly. "Take this young person where she may be attended to," he began, when the words were cut short on his lips. A man in evening dress had dashed from the crowd, and pushing aside the footmen, had lifted the frail figure in his arms, then his eyes fell on Stella's pale, gleaming face. "Where shall I take her?" he said quietly. "Please bring her inside," she said quickly, her eyes for a moment resting upon the cool, clean-shaven face and clear, grey eyes that looked into her own so strangely. And as he intercepted the look that passed between the woman he loved and the man who stooped to do her bidding, Cliff. fd Hawke realised that he had made a fatal mistake in having failed to do what sho had asked of him.

CHAPTER 111. LOVE-AND VENDETTA.

While Lady Mary Stainer was congratulat- ing Stella and Cliftord Hawke upon their engagement a stranger joined the group. "lour stray guest is going away, Lady Mary. The poor girl had fainted from hunger, I. fancy." "Poor creature!" Lady Mary exclaimed feelingly, "how awful to be hungry !" Then she turned to Stella.

"Let me introduce-- Mr. Tennant to you, dear. He is a very old friend of mine, in fact I have been a sort of godmother to him as well as to you."! Jasi?ar Tennant laughed. "More a good fairy,' he he said, as his eyes rested on Stella's face.

Lady Mary drew Hawke on one side, and Stella after a time found herself talking to Jaspar Tennant as she could never talk to her fiancé. The sympathy that flies from youth to youth was theirs, and when she learnt that he was an artist and would try to help the poor girl in whom she had taken such an interest, her eyes shone with pleasure. "I am so glad, Mr. Tennant; I was won- dering how 1 could help her," she said softly. "Have you asked her for her name and address?" _

"Yes, her name is Lotty Bond, and her ad- dresg is 14, Vincent-buildings, Westminster," he answered. "I am calling there to-mor- row." Then almost as though he was afraid to ask the question that quivered on his lips, he went on, "May I call to tell you the re- sult of my inquiry, Miss Traine?" "I shall be very pleased to see you," she answered simply, her serious eyes resting for a brief moment on the young and ardently eager face that was in its masculine way al- most as fair as her own, only his skin was burnt to a deep tan, and the crisp hair was cropped closely to his head. Jasper Tennant was not a lady's man; he loved the beautiful in woman from the point of view of an artist, and yet had he been told that at Lady Mary Stainer's ball that night he would meet a woman who appealed to the man in him as well as the artist he would have laughed. Before he left he sought Lady Mary, who was stealing a few minutes' rest from her guests. "I want to ask you a favour," he began smilingly. ger kind aristomtic face dimpled with ,srausement.

"Well," she said, "what is this wonderful favour, Jasper? Do you want me to buy a picture? because I simply can't! I don't know how to make both ends meet as it is." He laughed amusedly. "Nothing :•so un- reasonable as that! I only want you to come to my litudio on Sunday, and—" he hesi- tated slightly, "will you bring Miss Trains with you? 1 think she takes an interest in art." •

Laity Mary laid her plump, well-kept band on his arm. "Jaspar, don't be foolish," she said softly. "You know that to lift your eyes to Stella Traine is to desire a star beyond your grasp. She is engaged to Clifford Hawke, the dark 'Bill Sikes' looking man who was hero with her."

'Ate nand that lay in nis ow-n neitrier tered nor tried to withdraw itself, but lay like a snowflake, cool and soft in his own burning fingers. And so it was with the heart that beat gently against the silk mus- lin gown she was wearing. Not a pulse of it stirred the faster, not a tremor quickened the blood that throbbed with each breath, macl yet §tella Traino liked and respected her lathers triena more than any man she knew. But his words of love left her unres- ponsive and still. Was this calm quiet what women felt towards the men who loved them? she wondered, or was she only sur- prised and frightened by the depth of the passion she had roused?

Stella, I am waiting, dear, don't send me away miserable," Hawke said huskily. "I feel that if I lose you I shall lose my hope of salvation; you are everything to me, dear, the sum total of all that makes life heaven or hell." A faint colour stained her cheeks. She was moved by his devotion, as an older, wiser woman would have been. The red wine of his passion began to tint the cool water of her crystal-like calm. Surely such love must be able to win love? she wondered.

"Mr. Hawke," she began slowly, "I don't know if I care in the way that I should like to care for my—my husband. I respect and honour you, but I feel that love—" Ile laughed happily and drew her to him. "Dearest, I would rather teach you how to love than win the love that had been brought into life by another man. Only let me know that you will be mine and love will follow— with a woman like you it must, dearest, for such devotion as I have for you cannot go unrequited! You must—you shall love me in return!"

"And you are content with what I feel for you now?" she asked slowly, "the respect and honour that is yours already?" "More than content," he answered her ten- derly, "because from them I shall watch the birth of love, and when you are my wife, dearest—" He paused a moment, and she looked up at him in surprise. His face had grown cold and strangely pale, almost as though he had forgotten what he had been going to say. "Are you ill?" she asked quickly. "Ill!" He broke into a sudden laugh that somehow jarred upon her already highly strung nerves. It was the laugh of a man who had thrust an unpleasant memory be- hind him. "No, dearest, I am never ill," he went on quietly; "how could I be when I know that I have won the most beautiful woman in the world for my wife?" and he drew her into his arms and kissed her hair, her eyes, her cool cheeks, and lastly the red lips that lay against his own hot ones like the petals of a rose the rain- had lately washed.

• "My darling,' he murmured brokenly, "you will not keep me waiting long for my happiness? You will marry me soon?" When you wish," she answered, "only, please, Clifford," with a sudden swift glance into his face, "don't hurry me too much! You know I want to get accustomed to—to— everything, and besides," she added in a dif- ferent tone, "I must study father a little." Her lover's brow contracted slightly. "Your father will be glad. I have told him of my feelings long ago." "Yes," she murmured, "it will please him.v

Before he left her he raised her slim, white hands in his and kissed them reverently. "You will never regret this day, darling. All my life I will devote to your happiness, and each joy I bring you may perhaps wipe out part of my debts in the Recording Angel's book." _

"Is it a very big debt?" she smiled. "It is a debt that I shall be glad to see wiped off," he replied gravely. "Only when I loved you did I realise what it was. I have not been a saint," he went on, "but when I met you I knew for the first time what it was to wish to place a clean record before a woman."

She looked at him with interested eyes. She did not love the man she had just pro- mised to mary, but she admired him, and liked him for not treating her like a doll. She had never been shown the other side of a man's heart, and her sympathy was roused by his confession. "Was there ever another woman, Clif- fold?" she asked softly. His dark eyes contracted suddenly. "Once —long ago,' he answered, "but she is dead —the brief flame of a boyish passion burnt itself out, and yet," he added tenderly, "I would give my life to be able to say there had been no one; I resent even the shadow of another woman having cast a reflection on my soul." "And yet that other woman once loved you!" Stella mused. "Why did you part?" She was surprised that she felt no jealousy concerning this other woman in her lover's past, but only a warm interest. "We parted as all sensible people part who find that they are making a mistake," he an- swered curtly, "but don't let us mar our first hour alone together, darling, by unveiling what has been," he added tenderly. "I am jealous of even a thought that is not mine."

CHAPTER I. THE ACCEPTANCE.

Clifford Hawke's chambers were in a block of fashionable flats and chambers built at the end of a long thoroughfare, and just a stone's throw from Piccadilly-circus. It was a late afternoon in summer, one of those delight- fully balmy days in June when the atmos- phere seems to quiver with a keen, throbbing desire for life, and even the very street noises take on a joyous note. The sinking sun was casting long golden shafts of light into the room, where the open window revealed great bowls of roses that filled the room with the scent of exquisite longings, their petals weary with the weight of their own loveli- ness. The golden light and the roses, by far the most beautiful sight in a room full of beauti- ful things, stirred even the sluggish pulses of the man who sat in the recess by the open window, listening lazily to the muffled voice of the great human tide below and about him. Clifford Hawke loved the voice of the ceaseless roar, for it sang to him now the mellow notes of success, and more than suc- cess' as it had in the early days of young manhood sung to him of the constant need of grit and pluck. It had egged him on in those days to pit his roar against that of the crowds who were surging round him and he had won—yes, on looking back on the slim, pale-faced, indomit- able-eyed stripling, with no influence and less capital, to the man he had made himself, rich, successful, feared in the City and courted in Mayfair—Clifford Hawke could now hear the note of triumph in the song that floated in through the open window. tt Yet that same evening was to put the crown on Clifford Hakwe's success and social glory, for at six o'clock he would be in his 40-horse power Panhard, on his way to propose to the woman upon whom he had set the seal of his ambitious love. To be the husband of the only daughter of the managing director of the Amalgamated Copper Company, of which he was the secretary, at a salary of fifteen thou- sand a year, to say nothing of other outside speculations, would, to Clifford Hawke's am- bition, be the crowning stone in the mosaic of his life.

For twenty years he had denied himself the dalliance that other men have with love. Ile had sternly held aloof from women, forging his way to the goal in view without so much as a backward glance at the temptations of the fleshpots of Egypt. And now his reward was at hand. All those years of hard self. repression was to be rewarded by a rich har- vest that the good reaper might justly ex-

pect. Presently he rose and gazed at a photo- graph in a silver frame on the mantel. It was the latest that a fashionable lady photo- grapher had taken of Stella Traine. He had cut it out of a Society paper, and it was the only woman's face in the room. As he looked at it his deep-set eyes flashed with pride. "The loveliest face in London," he muttered, "and the whitest soul in the world! With you as my wife, Stella, my guiding star, every dark memory will be washed in the waters of Lethe." He bit his lip for a moment, then added firmly, "Yes, every dark memory that belongs to the past." Half an hour later he was stepping into his dark red and white car that was shrieking and throbbing in the courtyard. As he paused to give an instruction to the chauffeur, who stood by the open door, the ball porter came hurriedly down the steps, a letter in his hand. "A man left this some time ago, sir," he said apologetically. " I was not on duty then, but the other porter thought you were out and did not send it up." Hawke looked at it carelessly. The writing was unfamiliar to him, and he thrust it into his pocket with a nod. He had something more urgent to think about than what was probably a begging letter, since the paper was poor and the writing shaky. In ten minutes' time the car stopped out- side a white house with a red awning over the balcony that was hung with flaming scarlet

geraniums. As he entered the white and gold drawing- room, Clifford Hawke realised for the first time the extreme nervous tension that had gripped him during the short journey from Piccaailly. He again felt as he did the day when years ago he had made his first big at- tempt to bluff fortune into playing him a good turn. He had won then by sheer force of personality. Would that same force stand by him now? he wondered. At that moment the door opened and a girl entered the room. A girl who looked, with her exquisitely lovely face and corn- coloured, simply dressed hair, the embodi- ment of an old world picture, yet whose tall, slender figure and graceful, self-possession showed that she had been brought up in an atmosphere of modernity. As she went-forward to greet him a slight flush atained her clear skin. The look in Clifford Hawke's dark eyes as he clasped her hand told her what she had begun to sus- pect was the reason of his desire to see her. Stella Train° was not vain. Indeed, her nature was too sweet and natural for her to ever think much about the admiration that was poured at her feet. Men had made love to her and always been met by the same frank, smiling, friendliness that had disarmed pas- sion, and sent them away baffled yet hoping. But now for the first time in her twenty-one years the girl shrank from the words she saw trembling on the lips of the man who was her father's friend and right hand in all his undertakings. Ever since she had known him she had always looked upon the big man with the dark strenuous face as belonging to her father's time of life, and the sudden glimpse of the lover in his eyes startled her. "I have only just returned from Lady St. Barbe's bazaar," she said lightly; "it was an awful crush, and 60 hot; you were wise to keep away." "I could go nowhere except to such places where work would keep me from thinking too it something else," he replied. "1 City all day nearly, and got back in :hie your note that told me and see you." a into a nervous little laugh. at can be no very rare event in You see me so often, Mr. Hawke." CHAPTER 11.

I BEGGAR ON HORSEBACK.

It was late when Clifford llawke left the house, and as his car, with a great deal of hooting and snorting began to glide into Park-lane, a hansom pulled up outside the millionaire's house, and a woman, dark and imperious looking, sprang out and ran up the steps and entered the house as one who had the free run of it. "Any callers, Mason?" she asked the but- ler in the hall.

"Only Mr. Hawke, miss," he replied. A rosy glow flushed the girl's dark face. "Was anyone in?" "Yes, miss, Miss Traine was, and Mr. Hawke went to her boudoir, and has just left," came the reply. A hard look stole into Isobel Frant's black eyes. She was a niece of the millionaire, J. Carter Traine, and lived with him, sharing her cousin's life as a sister might. "He came to see Stella," she muttered, bit- ing her lip with the sharp white teeth of which she was so proud. "I wonder what about? Has he been nice to me only because he loved her! Oh, I hate being a poor rela- tion," she thought, as she entered the beauti- ful suite of rooms she occupied, "I am only here on sufferance; if Stella chose she could make Uncle James give me an allowance to live abroad. Oh," she cried passionately, as she glanced at her dark, gipsy like face in the mirror, "if Clifford Hawke would only ask me to marry him. I love him more than Stella ever could, and as his wife I could have all London at my feet. But there's plenty of time yet," she smiled presently, "perhaps after all it is I whom Clifford Hawke will marry." In the meantime Clifford Hawke had ar- rived at his chambers in the Albany. As he dresed for dinner h suddenly re- membered the letter that he had thrust into his pocket as he was leaving the Albany earlier in the evening., He drew it out and opened it carelessly. 'Now for my begging letter!" he mused. "Anyway, whatever the request is I can't refuse anything reasonable to-night!" He drew the electric reading lamp nearer to him and then bent over the ill-written scrawl. _

"Paul Steinway in London!" he muttered, "in London, and with my address at bis fn. ger-tips. What does it mean except that ho imagines that he is going to make me dance to any tune he likes to play.," A grim smilr flitted across his mouth. 'Paul Steinway does not know me, or else he would remembet that Clifford Hawke was never one to bare his hand forced."

The next evening Hawke hurried home from business and dressed with impatient haste. He was going with his fiancée and her cousin and father to a ball that night at the house of Lady Mary Stainer, who was a well-known society leader, and Stella's godmother. WOMEN'S CHAT.

Thanksghings at St. Paul's.—St. Paul's Cathedral has been the scene of many historical thanksgivings, and, all being well, the King and Queen will attend a 'special thanksgiving service there on February 6th, on their return from India. Queen Anne attended a service in the Cathedral before the building was com- pleted, to render thanks for the victories of Marl- borough, and George 111, was present at a similar service in 1797 on behalf of the victories of the British , Navy over the French and Spanish forces. Within recent times, the most solemn of these occasions was the thanksgiving sal vice on February 27th, 1872,f0r the restoration to health of King Edward then Prince of Wales. Queen Victoria went to tho Cathedral On the occasion of the Diamond Jubilee in 1897, and the 'thanksgiving service on February 6th will follow very much on the lines of that which their Majesties attended thero after the Coronation. The Word "Obey."—The question of omitting the word "obey" from the briae's vow in the Church marriage service, is ones more the subject of frivolous discussion, but these controversies always appear a little futile because no woman need say the word if she does not wish to. It is more often than not omitted from the marriage service of the Free Churches, and there is always the civil marriage open to the woman who objects to the promise to "obey." Such a promise in the marriage service, in some or other form goes back to very ancient times, but it was more suited to the social conditions of the period when St. Paul wrote the context in Ephesians than to the relationship of the sexes in these altered times. It bus in fact become little more than an abstract form of words, more honoured in the breach than the observance, just the same as is the bridegroom's promise to "worship and endow his bride with all his worldly goods." In the majority of cases a woman likes to think of her husband as a superior being, but most people if they have any opinion at all on the subject would say that the promise to " obey " is more likely than not to engender unhappiness in marriage, and if that is so, it would be better omitted from the revised Prayer Book. Marriage Experiments.—lt is sometimes said that nowadays all marriages are trial marriages, and in America, where divorce is easy, there are not infrequently novel experiments in this direction. It used not to be uncommon in Scotland for a married woman to partially retain the use of her maiden name, and, carrying this idea further, a Professor of Columbia University recently married under a compact that he and his wife would retain their own identity. So they had their names separably inscribed on the door plate and on their visiting cards, in equal-sized letters. Such strange ideas may commend themselves to people with a love for notoriety, tut despite the modern craze for "faddism," the conventialities are for the good and con- venience of the whole cc mmunity and without them we should speedily relapse into semi-barbarism. The Independent Bachelor.—The London County Council is opening a new cookery school for boys, possibly with some idea of making them independent of the help of the other sex. The average girl, on leaving the elementary schools to take up a business career, has little practical knowledge of domestic s ffaire. She may hive attended one of the local cookery schools, but home tuition in such matters is more or less becoming a thing of the past. We may possibly arrive eventually at corporation schools for bachelors. Something of the kind will be seen at the Simple Life and Healthy Food Exhibitio' n which is to be held at the Caxton Hall, Westminster, in March. Here bachelorism will be encouraged, and its votaries imitated into the mysteries of sewing on buttons, the darning and mend- ing of clothes, also the arts of cookery and washing up, the laying and lighting of fires, and so on. Man, in short will be taught how easy it is to live alone and perhaps wish he had never been born. The Tunic.—lt was after the days of the first Empire in France, that the tunic first appeared in English dress. It was also at that period that dress experts turned their attention to Eastern countries and introduced the flimsy muslins and gorgeous embroideries of the Orient both of which modern experts have so successfully exploited in connection with this charming accessory as it has hitherto been regarded. This season it will become the drs ss itself rather than a part of it, and has thus been promoted to a more important position in the dress scheme of the immediate future. It has changed places with the underskirt to judge by the New Year modes, but the colouring, cut, trimming and material of the latter will require consideration because it must be still regarded as part and parcel of the whole. Both tunics and some severe tailor-made coats are cut away in front in swallow-tail fashion, but in the case of the coats there is rather an individual experiment than a decree of Madame la Mode. The draping of the tunic must necessarily affect the width of the skirt at the foot, but it will not lead to any appreciable widening at the hips lor knees. The rage for fringe on dresses, if not in millinery, is said to be declining, and its place is taken in Paris by scallops which are seen on bodices, hems, revere lingerie, on the tops of shoes and boots, and of long ;loves, on everythiug in fact that has an edge to be cut out.

The Sloping Shoulder.—A new development and a very charming one is the fashion of wearing diaphanous scarves with evening dresses and frequently they are added to the dinner dress also. Although theatre hoods are made in the most bewitching styles, women show a decided preference for the plain scarf as a protection for the head when going to the theatre, to judge at least from the number to be seen of the former, and the few to be noted of the latter. The scarves for evening wear are reminiscent of the beauties painted by Reynolds and Romney, but it is doubtful if the robust- athletic woman of to-day will wind the scarf as grace fully round her well-developed figure as did her forbears in the days of wasp waists and sloping shoulders. And this is a reminder that, in conformity with many Victorian revivals,A is quite possible that this summer will see a vogue for sloping shoulders. At any rate, some of 'the Paris houses are debating !this point, and encouragirg this feature by exhibiting models displaying them. As a matter cf fact not a few dresses for the Riviera have been made and are now being worn, that distinctly elope,where for so long they have been square and high. It remains to be proved whether the idea will " take " with our own designers.

EXCITING STATION SCENE.

At Crewe Station, on Monday, two horses were being conveyed by rail from Stafford to Warrington, and as the train was approaching the station the lamp of the horse box was seen to shoot from the roof of the carriage Into the air. The train was stopped, and it was found tbat one of the animals bad plunged through the window, and was resting on the edge of the window with its head and forefeet banging over. The horse struggled violently, and appeared in great pain. It was impossible to extricate it without cutting a portion of the carriage away. Saws, axes, and chisels were requisitioned, and about "thirty men began to break Op the carriage. In its mad endeavour to re- lease itself the horse had become more tightly wedged, and its bead was covered with blood caused by the broken glass. After working nearly an hoot sufficient of the compartment was cut away to enable the animal to get a little relief. An improvised platform was erected to rest one part of its body till the whole could be releaeed,
