JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

MRS PHILIP SNOWDEN,

who on Thursday lectlured here on Co-operation, shares the versatile gifts of her talented husband. She is an effective temperance speaker, and is booked to address the annual meeting of at least one big Band of Hope Union this vety month. As I coed at the time, I recently heard her from the platform of the non-militant section of women suffiagists. She has a calm, easy, argumentative style, tree horn froth and rant, and her addresses, packed as they generally are with well ordered facts,carry f,rre and weight and are altogether admirably calculated to inform and convince. SCOTIA

The recurrence of the Burns anniversary, and the in- clusion of haggis in the menu at the Warrington celebration of the event, suggests a passing allusion to the " Sub Rosa " column which has long been a conspicuous feature of the "Morning Leader" and since the advent of the pres-nt year has regularly appeared also in the "Daily News. " Even if the cynical author of that unique production were not thoroughly well tinown to readers, none would ever dream of accusing him of having a "wee droppie " of Scottish blood in his veins. Ho loses no oppor- tunity of poking fun at Highlanders and Lowlanders alike; the speech that every aihere bewrayeth them, the national costume, food, music, and little idiosyncracies, are by this caustic writer satirised and held up to ridicule in a manner which loyal Caledoniarsjcan hardly be expected to appreciate. ith other Sassenachs, I can myself enjoy all this ; as a matter of fact " S.L.H." is at his best when handling such subjects; but, unless I greatly mistake him, my excellent friend " Scottie," who is naturally quick to resent amusement at the expense of his cherished aspirations and sentiments, will not be too well pleased with " Sub-R-sa," its flouts and jeers and frivolities. TOASTS.

More agreeable reflections are induced by contempla- tion of the toast list at the Lion Hotel on the occasion ju-t referred to. I commend to those who there fore- gathered a toast which I heard in East Lancashire : "Here's to them that love 119, and as for those who bate us, may the Lord change their hearts." There are mors selfish examples, and those who prefer such may be interested in these : "Here's to those who love us, Not to those whom we love ! For those whom we love May not love us." " Here's to yen two and us two ! If you two like us two As we two like you two, Then here's to us four ! But if you two don't like us two As we two like you two, Then here's to us two, and no more ! " What is one to say of such toasts as—"llere's to our sweethearts and wives; may they never meet ! " Or, again : " here's to women who are tender, Here's to women who are slender, Here's to women who are large and fat and red ; Here's to women who have married, Here's to women who nave tarried, Here's to women who are epeeebless —but they're dead ! "

When the of Charles IL, rose to honour the toast " Here's a health unto His Majesty," it was their pleasant habit to make this characteristic addition

" And he who will not pledge this health, We'll wish him neither health nor wealth, Nor yet a rope to hang himself. So here is to our glorious King, And he who will not join and sing, To him I will apply my toe, That with full speed from us he go To eat sour crabs in Jericho." Perhaps ene of the best known, and undoubtedly among the smartest of all the old toasts that have ccmo down to us, is— " God bless our King the Faith's defender, . .

God bless (no harm in blessing) the Pretender; But which is Pretender, and which is King. God bless us all, that's quite another thing." "RACE IS EVERYTHING."

Hatton.

BILLIARDS.—The handicap given in connection with the Hatton Institute resulted in a win for J. Houghton, W. W. Leach being second, and W. Matthews third. This Is the second occasion that J. Houghton has secured first prize, %V. Leach being second to him each time.—At a meeting held at Daresbury January 24th, it was again decided to play for the Sloane Challenge Cup, the Moore, Daresbury and Hatton Rooms to compete home and home matches. The first match is at Dares- bury, February sth. Moore are the visitors. Moore. WESLEY G UILD.—On Wednesday evening Miss Dutton, the talented literary vice-president, gave a very delightful paper entitled " Literary Tit-bits." Mr. Jackson presided over a very bir attendance. At the outset Miss Dutton said that some of the best tit-bits were really scraps, and she did not intend to speak upon any of the really well known poets, but upon the works of four lesser known poets chosen from four different nationalities, Sir Thomas Brown, English ; Thomas Moore, Irish ; Alexander Dumas, French ; and Heinrich Heine, German. She gave a few biographical notes about each and spoke of Dumas' gnat admiration for Shakespeare. The remainder of the lecture consisted of quotations and recitations of some choice selections from the works of these four poets; Victor Hugo,another French poet was also quoted. In Conclusion the lecturer said that perhaps something which had teen said would inspire somecne to a closer acquaintance with these poets who although lesser known than others were worthy of consideration. Miss Castle proposed a vote of thanks to Miss Dutton and this was seconded by Mr Lawton, and carried with applause.

Great Sankey.

TEA AND ENTERTAINMENT.—The annual parochial tat and entertainment took place on Friday of last week, in the St. Mary's Sunday School. About 250 persons sat down to an excellent tea and were served by a band of willing workers. Trays were given by ladies and gentlemen connected with St. Mary's Church. After tea dancing and entertainment took place, when the gathering was considerably increased there being over 300 present. The large room was nicely decorated for the dancing. Mr. F. T. Newbold (vicar's warden) and Mr. E. Dumbill (people's warden), acted as M.C.'s and as usual the Bros. W. and J. Ray, were efficient as accompanist. In another room (for those not interested in dancing), a concert was kindly arranced by Mr 11 It Griffiths and Mr W W Whittle. The Vicar (Rev J 10 Jones) presided, and the various items were very much enjoyed. The following gave their services, pianoforte solo, Miss Foster ; songs, Misses H Hall, A P Jones and Gilbert ; Messrs W H Ireland and D 10 Griffiths, and Mr W W Whittle was the humorist ; Miss Murray very creditably recited ; Miss Standish was the accompanist. The Chairman expressed thanks to all those who had helped to make the evening a successful one. Needless to say this was carried with great applause.

Lymm.

MISSIONARY SEnvicEs.—On Sunday two excellent missionary sermons were preached in the Primitive Methodist Church, morning and evening, by the Rev. 3. W. Price, lately returned from Manazela, Central Africa. Mr Price also addressed a juvenile missionary meeting in the afternoon. On Monday evening the same gentleman gave a most instructive account of his life and work in the dark Continent, his description of the country, and of the people amongst whom he laboured, being supplemented by acme really good lantern slides. Mr U L Welford presided and Mr W T Moss operated the lantern. The collections were in advance of previous years. WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSECIATION.—Th3 monthly public meeting of the above association was held in the I Primitive Methodist schoolroom on Thursday evening. Miss Harper, of Altrincham, who presided, opened the meeting with a short and pithy address. The principal speaker of the evening was Mr W Abbott, also of Altrincharn, who warmly eulogised the energy of the Government in their endeavour to ameliorate the conditions of employment.

PRIMROSE LEA.GIMI.—The annual tea party and soiree in connection with the L3rum Habitation of the Primrose League was held in the Drill Hall on Wednes- day evening, when a large number sat down to an excellent tea; the caterer being Mrs. Kenzie. The tea was followed by a meeting at which Mr. H. P. Harris was the principal speaker. Afterwards Mr Taylor's band played for dancing until 12.30.

SKATING ON THE LAKE.—Lymm, like other portions of the country, passed the earlier part of the week in the lap of winter, and interest centred on the lake as a possible compensation for the severe weather. On Sunday the word went round that the lake was frozen over, on Monday we were informed that it would "bear to-morrow," and for once the local prophets touched the truth, but it was only a touch and nothing more as the skaters were few in number and lacking in confidence. By noon the thaw could no longer be disputed as water was rapidly forming on the surface of one of the finest sheets of ice ever seen on the lake and a settled gloom pervaded the faces of the dis- appointed votaries of " Jack Frost." But in spite of these changed conditions a few venturesome spirits were to be seen indulging in their favourite pastime even on Wednesday. Since then the frost has resumed sway, and there may be some sport this week-end. ITEATLEY BOY SCOUTS.—One of the laws of the Boy Scout movement requires each membor to do at least one kind act each ay, but on Saturday last the Heatley Troup improved upon this as through their instru- mentality over 200 children of Lymm and district were provided with a substantial tea, and an evening of unmixed pleasure. A peep into the Lymm Parochial Schoolroom shortly alter 5.30 p.m. revealed rows of childrens' faces lit, up with joy, and displaying a keen appreciation of the delectable and ample fare provided. The motto of the movement "Be prepared " had evidently been carefully considered both by the caterers, and the guests. Amongst the visitors were the Revs 0 B Thurston and 11 W B Wbiteway as representatives of the Established and Free Churches of Lymm. Tea over, the children formed in procession outside, and headed by the Scouts' Bugle Band,marched to the Congregational Schoolroom to witness the enter- tainment which was to be provided for them. Messrs T Nash and J Webb, jun. (Scoutmasters), Tom Kay and Fred Cousin (Assistant Scoutmasters) soon got their guests into position. After Mr A G Smith, J.P. had taken the chair the entertainment began and what a feast of enjoyment those children had ! Pianoforte solo by Stanley Jonee, violin duet by Fred Butter and Rowland Tilzey. Vocal solos by Allan Brown, Fred Rutter, I d Cowsill, W Jones, jun., Miss Ada Wrage, Reggie and Sydney Hillier, the last three mentioned especially tickling the palates of the audience. A handbell ringing exhibition and glees by the scouts' choir also added to the evening's enjoyment.— The Rev. 11, W. B. Whiteway addressing the meeting on the training of till Spartan children gave the derivation of tho phrase " lie's a bric!r," which originated wills one of the Spartan kings, and drew object lessons from this, complimentary to the Scout's laws. Mr IVbiteway said that be bad taken an interest in the movement from its innovation by Major- General Sir It Baden-Powell, and that the organisation had his entire approval.—A tour through Canada by means of lantern views manipulated by Mr J Webb, jour., with descriptive readings by Mr W G Jones (hon. secretary) followed by humorous and other slides, added variety to tbe programme.—The gathering was a pronounced success, reflecting the greatest credit on all concerned, and will undoubtedly give an impetus to the worthy cause under which auspices it was held. THE STORY OF THE 130TTZ E.

Ares Erg. liorlick's Malted Milk Co., have published the above interesting little booklet, which may be obtained from their manufactory at Slough, Bucks. It is a story of old nursery relics, mostly torn and battered, but which the mother had preserved until after 'sears, and among them was an empty bottle— accorded pride of place. It had contained the malted milk which the mother was convinced had saved the life of her child, after a wasting illness The story is prettily told in thedorrn of a dialogue. It is also illus- trated and affords delightful reading for little folks.

Padgate.

MOTHERS' MEETING.—For the first time this year the members of the above, asiembled in the Guild Room of the Wesleyan Church on Tuesday evening. To mark the occasion about 40 ladies partook of an excellent tea. Mrs Houghton presided during the very enjoyable social which followed. Songs end recitations were given by several of the members, and the games played evoked much amuseu.ent. VISIT OF THE REV. J. E. RATTENBURY.

Members of the Wesley Guild at Padgate look for- ward each year to the annual visit of the Rev. J. E. ! Rattenbury and their anticipations of a rich treat were • again fully realised on Thursday. In the afternoon the ! rev, gentleman preached a forceful and eloquent sermon ' from the words " Launch out into the deep, and let down sour nets for a draught." In the evening an audience numbering about 450 gave an enthusiastic welcome to Mr. Rattenbury and Mr. J. Upson who presided. The Chairman in the course of a short, but excellent address, said he was glad to be present for two reasons. First, as a local preacher, to thank the Padgate friends for the kind appreciation they manifested of the efforts made in the pulpit by himself and brethren,and,secondly because he felt honoured in being associated as Chair- man with so liners man as the lecturer. Proceeding Mr Upson pointed out the danger of under-estimating the humble efforts of ordinary individuals. Roughly speak- ing the world had come up to the standard of the churches. There could be very little more advanced legislative action without a move forward of the Churches. The first action of the modern church should be to create a nu( tive to set the pace for the world. The conscience of the public is satisfied if only the natural needs of the nation are met, and because of this Mr. Upson urged the necessity of personal consecration. In opening his lecture" The Church and the modern situation " the Rev. J. E, Itattenbury spoke of the pleasure it gave him to visit Padgate once more and incidentally congratulated them on the possession of the fine new school premises. He said there was no church of its size that he admired more than Padgate. In the modern situation, from the Churche's point of view, there was more widespread pessimism end solicitude for the Church's welfare than he had ever known. Although he did not intend to predch a sermon he based his remarks upon a passage in St. Luke, "And there shall be signs in the EUD, and In the moon, and in the stars, and upon the earth distress of nations, with perplexity ; the sea and the waves roaring ; men's hearts failing them for fear and for looking after those things which are coming on the earth ; for the powers of Heaven shall be shaken. And when these things become to come to pass, then look up and lift up your heads, for your redemption draweth nigh." Proceeding the lecturer said that in the political world " men's hearts are failing them for fear "—of Lloyd George. (Laughter.) He remarked that the Apostles rejoiced in adversity, not because they called it happiness, but because they knew that it was the cause of the success of religion. Referring to St. Paul he quoted the passage, " a great and effectual war is open to us, and there are many adversaries." If ever a man had a right to doubt the final triumph of Christ it was St. John the Divine. Banished to the Isle of Patmos, he felt all the isolation and lonliness of the exile inflicted by the power of the long arm of Imperial Rome, but his writings clearly showed the victory of the slaughtered lamb, over the multitude of savage beasts he desicribes. Everybody knows when the stakes of Smithfield and Oxford were lighted, and the great leaders of the church were burned, that it was a time of great pessimism and despair, but the well- known noble words of Latimer proved that where others caw only a destructive and consuming fire, he beheld a candle to enlighten. Of Sir Thomas Moore the lecturer said that he was the noblest man In England as a martyr ' for the truth, and he laid it down not for Pretestantism but for Catholicism. "Honour, where honour is due." Speaking of the Renaissance period, Mr. Rattenbury said that then' Europe woke cut of slumber, Court ladies instead of spending their time painting their faces learnt Greek and Latin, and it became fashionable to know classical literature. That was a long ti e ago. (Laughter.) At this time there was great doubt I and much scepticism, and devout people shook their , heads. There was serious doubt among those who stood for religion. This might clearly be seen in some of the church architecture of the period. In the choir of a church near Bourne- mouth might still be seen a carving representing / a monk preaching to a congregation. But the monk I , was represented as a fox wearing a cowl and the people as geese. (Laughter) ID the midst of all this what happened ? Martin Luther happened, and the Christ of , Luther was the biggest Christ Europe had ever known. Proceeding, the lecturer said he wanted to appeal to the ,1 credulity of no man but to faith and confidence. The unrest Gf the present time is the black bud that heralds the foliage of summer. He had hope of the future for two reasons, let, criticism of tbe Bible, or what is I known as the higher cr,ticism, and 2nd, social unrest. He did not shut his eyes to the danger of these things, but he believed in Truth at all cost. If the Bible will not stand criticism it is not worth cur acceptance Modern criticism is rot as convenient for the pulpit but it is much more Christian than the old ideas. He I thought the Christ of to-day not big enough, and that the Christ of the Bible and the future was infinitely bigger. Of social nnrest the lecturer raid there were many natural fears and misgivings. The demo- ' cratic spirit will probably continue to make mistakes, but without mistakes there could be no true progress. It was much better to be discontented than asleep. Discontent in men's minds is not an evil thing, it is the monument of God's Spirit. We should, as St Peter exhorts, grow not only in the grace but in the know- ledge of Jesus Christ. Hitherto the Churches had regarded Christ as their own exclusive possession and had forgotten the bone and blood and muscle of his humanity. Ills Kingdom had in the past been regarded es a limited monarchy—limited in politics and commerce The various forces o f unrest may mean a wider concep- tion of Christ ; the Christ of the future will be greater than at any time in the past. The future was not dark, but bright. " There's a good time coming," God kelp us all to discern the summer that is coming. A vote of thanks to the Chairman nod Lecturer was proposed by Dr Bennett, who spoke with enthusiasm of the work being done by Mr Rattenbury in the West , London Mission. Ile invited anyone with surplus cash to assist in reducing the debt on the magnificent Institutional buildings in which the work is carried on. Mr. Duckworth seconded and spoke of the benefit congregations derived from the eloquent sermons of Mr. Upson. The singing of the Doxology brought the meeting to a close.

ARSON CONFESSION AT sr HELENS. SERIOUS FARM FIRE.

The St. Helens Fire Brigade wataccupied for some hours on Sunday night dealing with a largo farm fire at Hilltop Farm, near the Bottle and Glass, on the Rainford road. The farm, which is in the occupation of Mr. Thomas Houghton, stands near the side of the main road, and on Monday morning it transpired that a man had given himself up to the police and con- fessed that he had wilfully caused the fire. The man's name is George Barrett, of no flied abode, aged thirty- two, who hails from Sheffield. The police state that Bat rett was seen going through Rainford during the course of the evening, and he called at a shop and bought some matches. He pro- ceeded about two miles on the road, and on getting to Hilltop farm he decided to go up to a stack by the side of the road and set fire to it. After doing this be walked straight on to the St. Helens Town Hall and gave himself up, stating that he had wilfully set fire to the stacks, and he had done it for the purpose of getting into vol. The fire brigade, under Superin- tendent Shaw, found that the fire had spread from the stack to a large Dutch barn, which was full of oats and farm in3plements. The produce was all practically consumed, and damage to the extent of £5OO was ch-ne•

Barrett was brought before Mr. T. Glover at St. Helens Town Hall at noon on Mcnday when he was formally charged with arson. Constable Stevenson proved the arrest, and swore to tie statement that Barrett made. The man said that be wanted to be in gaol again. Barrett offered no opposition to being remanded until to-day.

"LOYALISNI " DEVELOPMEN I' IN ULSTER.

Sit John Simon, the Solicitor General, made a speech cn Home Rule et a Liberal demonstration in Widnes OD Wednesdiy night, He referred to the attempt "to prevent " the Ulster Hall meeting as a classic example of bigotry and intolerance. The Ulster Unionists pretended that they were attached to British rule, but the attachment was only so long es British rule left them free to get everything they liked in their own way. They denounced boycotting, but nobody had ever heard of such an immense boycott as this they had planned at Belfast. They opposed coercion, but was there any crime more dastardly than that of people who in attempting to discredit a Govern- ment in England were prepared to ask their countrymen to risk their heads and their jives? It was the fixed determination of the Government to carry Home Rule, and they would not be thwarted by a party who desired to keep their ascendancy at all costs and against all comers. Tne meeting was held in the Skating Rink. and more than three thowaud people were present. Mr. Roscoe Brunner presided.

The Clerk said they had born written to and accom- dation had been found for some of the bones. Mr. Woodward said it seemed to him to be a serious matter for Frodsham. If there as to general nuisance there was a smell nuisance. On the street and at the station itself the smell was unbearable. It did not seem to him that the Railway Company attempted to get it away as they ought to do. That Council ought to influ(nce them to abate the nuisance.

The Clerk painted out that before they could take action they would have to prove an actual nuisance. If they stopped it altogether it might take that particalar industry away from the district. The Chairman suggested that more pressure should be brought to bear on the Railway Company, and see if they could not transfer the bones to some more suit- able place. He proposed that steps be taken in the matter.

A Voice : It is a matter we cannot lose sight of. It was decided to write the parties concerned further on the matter.

"STRANGERS EXCLUDED.

A resolution was passed to the effect that strangers should not be allowed to attend parochial committee meetings.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held on Tuesday. Mr. C. E. Linaker, Jun., presided.

LESS OUT RELIEF.

The Clerk said that on looking through Mr. Duff's report he found that in that district the amount of relief per head of the population was 3s. *5B. The average for semi-urban areas such as theirs was 48. 2d. '57. There was only one area which cost less than theirs and that was Tarvin which was 2s. lid. '57. Taking the whole areas, urban and semi-urban areJs and rural areas, Bucklow, Tarvin and Ellesmere were less and the others were more.

Mr. Woodward in presenting his financial report said he had been impressed by the reduction which was taking place in out-relief. Dur;ng the last few months the amounts had been from £l5 to £l2 per fortnight. They were paying Mr. Pearson a considerable sum, out of all proportion to what he was distributing for out- relief. It had occurred to him that that was a thing which might be remedied. At the same time he would not have been satisfied if he had not drawn their attention to the matter.

Mr. Morris.—lt is a matter for congratulation not censure.

_ Mr. Woodward said be would be pleased to see the amount disappear altogether. His only idea was :—Was it not possible to allocate any other duties to Mr. Pearson ? Was he sufficiently employed ? The Clerk said that at :he time when the Old Age Pensions Act was made to apply to paupers it relieved the Board of some expense and on the recommendation of the Local Government Board, tLey decided to do away with a relief station. Mr. Pearson was distributing less than before the Old Age Pension Act applied to paupers. But now he bad to go toths individuals at their houses. (Hear, hear.) That IN as more work than when they had a relief station. . .

Mr. Woodward Enid he was cnly carrying out his duty in drawing their attention to the matter. (Hear, hear) APPOINTMENT OF CHAPLAIN.

Apropos of the resignation of the Rev A L Temple, chaplain at the house, some discussion took place as to the arrangements for his successor. " •

Alderman A. L. Norman asked if the Board was bound to appoint an Anglican clergy man and get the sanction of the bishop. The Clerk : That is so. Alderman Norman said he had nothing against the appointment of an Anglican clergyman, but there were Nonconformists and lion afr Catholics ID the house. lie gave notice that he would move a resolution at the next meeting appealing to the Local Government Board to give them a free Land 3ver the appointment of a chaplain. Mr. Morris said the Housing Committee had spent some considerable time in discussing the matter and they thought that the late chaplain had taken a very high hand in the matter. In regard to the salaary, originally it was £4O hut it was reduced to £BO. Of the remaining £lO, £3 went to the Nonconformist instructor and .£5 to the Roman Catholic instructor. The two latter gentlemen gave quite as much time to the duties in the house as the chaplain. He suggested that they should reduce the salary to £3O and give the other two more.

Mr. Whitlow did not think they ought to blame the chaplain. He simply asked for a rise of sllary. They ought to have written him straight away. Mr. Linaker said he did not think Mr. Whitlow under- stood the circumstances. The chaplain had written to the Board asking for an increase in salary. The Board replied that in view of the alterations of the distribu- tion of children in the workhouse they would like him to wait until things were settled. Ho would not wait, and sent in his resignation. _

Mr. Linaker (senior) said they asked the chaplain to wait but he would rot. He should have waited, and be was of opinion that the Board had taken quite the I right lines. Mr. W. L. Morris expressed the cpinion that the chaplaincy ought to be a labour of love instead of a business matter. Mr. J. Stokes pointed out that a good deal had been said about the salary. The impression created seemed to he that the &Airs of the supplementary clergy were less and that the duties of the chaplain were the same as when he received a salary of £lO. Mr. Woodward said the question appeared to him to be alpurely business one. The real question was what were the duties of the chaplain and how were they performed ? He would like to understand whether the religious side of the life of the inmates of that house was attended to properly by the chaplain. Apart from any other question be did not think the chaplain ought to be brought into conflict with the Nonconformists. They onght to take the instructors of each section on their merits.

After further discussionl it was resolved to write to the clergymen in the neighbourhood, alvising them of the vacancy and inviting their applications. The committee reported en increase in the number of vagrants and inmates. The minutes were confirmed.

The several parts, after being well sand- papered, may now be carefully fitted together by small nails, and then the clock fitted in. Fig. 3, which is a view of the back, shows how this is done. A block of wood is hollowed out to the curve of the clock barrel, and made of a Sufficient height for the clock face to fit into the hole in the front. _ _ _

Two thin pieces of wood, about kin. thick and liin. deep, are now screwed to the back

at suitable distances from the top ard bottom, and serve to hold the clock firmly in the case. A small brass handle (a drawer handle costing a few pence will do) should next be neatly secured to the centre of the top piece, to carry the case about with. When fitted up a coat of walnut stain, a bottle of which can be purchased cheaply, w.ill finish it off.

The C.L.B. and the Exhibition.

Sir,— I noticed during the time that the Dumping Exhibition was being held in the Empire Skating Rink last reek, a body of lads parading the town each night to the strains of a bugle band. I understand that the lads belong to the Juvenile Conservative Association. Owing to the Equipment of the iada in some respects, such as the cap. and white haversack, being somewhat similar to that of the Church Lads Brigade and most of their parades being held after dark, 1 believ• that quite a large number of people thought that they were the Church Lads Brigade. This Brigade is an organization of Church of England lads, the officers and members of which as such know no politics. I wish to say on behalf of nay drams and myself that the Brigade has no conneetion whatever with the Juvenile Conservative Association. I shall be most grateful if you can find space to insert this letter in justice to the Brigade and its many supporters.-1 am, Sir, yours faithfully, F. M. APPLETON, (Officer commanding the Warrington Battalion Church Lads Brigade). Warrington. Feb. 2nd, 1012.

Peoples' College "Old Boys" Sir,—Tha suggestion has been made that the Old Boys of Peoples College have a re-union dinner, and it will, I feel sure, meet with approval from a large number of Old Boys. But why stop at dinner?. Could we not have cur Old Boys association with president, secretary, dinner etc all complete?. Such a suggestion is, however, a very old one, in fact I be- lieve I am correct in saying that the same one was put forward 20 years ago and has been repeatedly re-introdaced since. School re unions, dinners and associations have always a charm of their own and each time the matter has been brought forward it has been received favourably by both young and old Old Boys. All seem to have wished that something of the kind could be brought into being, but no one seems to have made any effort to make a start. That such an ass-ciation would be of immense value in many ways I am quite convinced and on its merits ought to eecure the active co-operation of all Old Boys of the school now resident in Warrington. A cordial invitation is given to all interested in the project to join me in a practical effort to see the matter through and I ask all Old Boys to kindly send their names and addresses to me as early' as possible If I recei /et sufficient response to justify the proposal, I shall be pleased to arrange for an inaugural meeting to be held, the particulars of which would be advertised later. This would give an opportunity for an exchange of views and would be a valuable guide as to whether there is a reason- able chance of successfully forming an association or not. —Tours,

T. N., ASHLEY.

Eorsemarket-street, Warrington. February Ist. 1012.

The Dumping Exhibition—A Few Facts.

Sir,—M one who firmly believes in our present Free Trade system may I venture to offer one or two remarks concerning the Dumping Exhibition recently held in Warrington? A. great deal of noise has been made and the good old flag has been prominently displayed, but what are the results. A. 4 a spectacular display, the Exhibition was a success, but as a means of education it was a dead failure. These are strong words but I will prove my assertion. The materials exhibited on most of the stalls were very shoddy and trashy and to tell British workingmen, that that was what they had to compete against, was a s'ur on their intelligence. Take the clothing stall. A. German suit was exhibited which coat 255. N. Why, sir, suits can be purchased in England for considerably less, but why ? Because the poor persons making them are sweated to enable big profits to accrue. 'Tis not the foreigner we have to fear, Sir ; it is the sweaters who grind the best out of their employees at a starvation wage. Fancy men in Warrington working besides roaring furnases for the splendid salary of 19s. 101 a week I 'On another stall our exports and imports in iron and steel were shown, but how much ? Only American. Now, Sir, 'tis well known America is a self-producing country, especially 93 far as iprn and steel are con- , cerned. But let us see what really is true. Since r:utr sahrs of iron and steel have risen from £29,000,000 ta £,14,013;.;,l)00 worth. Another stall told the dismal tale of bow we were 'Demi; Twited by the wicked foreigner in our glass and earthwar Q trad",:. litit cur true rotition is this 13Q2 ra .purchase,i 'tall as much again we eole,but in 1910 we sold 1150,000 worth more than we bought. Dying industry' Mr. Chamberlain the great Tariff apostle, said at Greenock in October, 1903, " Cotton will go." So it has, where? To every quarter of the world. In 1902 we sold to the outside world £72,500,000 worth and bought £6,000,000 worth. In 1911 we sold £120,000,000 worth and bought £13,300,000 worth. Who are the Dumpers? Ask Lancashire and Yorkshire. Our Tariff Reform friends are always telling work- ing men and women that Tariff Reform cures unem- ployment. What a fallacy I—happily long since exploded. What has been the recent experience of America and Getmany ? The latest unemployed returns are :—America, 11 per cent ; Germany, no official figures are given, but the " Vorwaerts ' ' a powerful organ makes the following statement "During the months of December and January more than 200,000 homeless and penniless men were received in the municipal night refuge, whilst the privare shelter society has provided for another 31,000, during the same period," Official returns for England, :3 per cent the lowest cn record. Cold com-fort to our Tory friends. Mr. Chamterlain said at Liverpool in October, 1903 : -" I hold the success of a country must he measured by the Imports and Exports. Whiht I do not agree entirely with that argument yet assuming it is correct what do we find? The figures for 1911 have just been published and all previous records have been beaten, Mr. Bonar Law must still pine for his two bad winters I No Dump- ing Exhibition can destroy the facts, hence my assertion, that the show was an utter failure. Our supremacy in the commercial world stands undisputed; Our flag is safe under Free Trade and all this show is only another attempt to side track people's attention 'tom the more important issues. I will deal gently with the clocks that were repre- sented as English and presented as prizes. Perhaps the promoters will tell us where they were made. (perhaps uot).—Yours HARRY NEW.

GAY SCENES IN PARR HALL.

No prettier spectacle has ever been seen at the Parr Hall than that which was presented on Thursday evening when the Warrington Women's Liberal Association held a fancy dress ball and carnival. The brilliance and animation of the picture called forth admir- ing comments from the spectators who crowded the galleries, for in the number and variety of the characters portrayed the function has probably never been equalled in Warrington. In every respect the success of the ball more than realized the highest expectations, and too much praise cannot be given to Mrs. Osborne, the energetic hon. secretary, and the ladies' committee who were responsible for the arrangements. The beautiful decorations of the hall gave the scene a charming setting. Red, white and blue were the predominant colours and large Union Jacks were suspended at the back of the platform. The attendance of dancers reached the splendid number of 370, over 200 of whom were in fancy costumes The representation of an almost bewildering array of characters gave ample scope for the exercise of taste and ingenuity and friendly rivalry was stimulated by the offer of prizes in the various classes. Before the ball began there was a grand parade of competitors and as the long line of colour unfolded itself, the effect was exceedingly picturesque. Repre- sentatives of different nationalities, comical personages, foresters, toreadors, pierettes, red Indians, flower girls, jesters—and a host of others were to be seen in the procession. Of the original touches, one of the most striking was a tram conductor's uniform which appeared to be one mass of tram tickets. The company gave a cordial welcome to Mr. A. H. CrosfielJ, who appeared in the

KNUTSFORD DIVISION LIBERALS.

Prospective Candidate at Appleton.

Mr. P. M, Oliver, Trospective Liberal Candidate for the Knutsford Division visited Appleton on Thursday evening and delivered an ihterr sting addre:es in the schoolroom. .

Mr. H. G. Rob.rts who presided, in opening the proceedings, said be was sure they were all glad to see Mr. Oliver. (Hear, hear.) Ile was a young man and enthusiastic in the cause of Liherelism. He (the chairman) thought that when they bad heard him speak they would be pleased with him and he hoped that at some Lot very distant date they would have him as their member. St. John's Presbyterian Church.

Prior to the captivity it had played relatively a small part in religious life, the vital pro- gressive spirit expressing itself in prophecy, which had laid the foundations for the future wisdom teaching. The sage was the true descendent of the prophet, and it was his duty to adjust the truths passed on to the new problems of his day. The leaching of wisdom had dove& per!. St. Peter's Church.

St. Peter's was a !ergs parish, and its needs were many. They had the reputation of being a congregation hich, nlthoJgh poor, always contributed well to the many organizations which were carried out in their midst. As they were aware, St. Peter's was the only church in the parish, and it had become necessary to extend the work. The schoolroom was becoming too small to nccorarnodate the children in the Sunday school, and some of the classes of the larger children had to be tram ferred each Sunday to the Mission building. In %Mil ion, Mrs. Percival each Sunday after- noon held a very large Young Women's Bible Class in in the bottom room at the Mission, and on Sunday evenings a servio3 was held in the Mission by the Church Army, services which be (the chairman) was sure acre greatly appreciated by a large number of people. They would, therefore, see that the Mission Hall was being tested to its full capacity. ===== 0002601_19120203_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
TALKS ON HEALTH.

[l3x A FAMILY DOCTOR.]

I TATA ABOUT BACTERIA.

Our ideas about disease lave undergone a reyolution since it was discoyered that a great many of the familiar diseases wore due to microbes. A new science was initiated called bacteriology, and the men who pursued the science wero known as bacteriologists. A bacteriologist is a clever man, who generally has a protuberant fore- head, a bald head, aid eyes weak from con- stantly straining his vision by looking clown a microscope studying minute objects. Now, the use of a powerful microscope re- veals another world to us; there are micro- scopic forests with microscopic denizens; there are fierce little creatures witiolt prey upon harmless little beings, just as lions prey upon antelopes.

:4): -----

MICROBES OF MANY KINDS.

There are all sorts of minute organisms, and their shapes, habits, diet, manners, and customs are studied by the bacteriologist just as the botanist studies plants and the zoologieb animals. We find, for instance, that polar bears live in cold climates, and would die if put in the clesert, and that camels enjoy living in the desert, and would die in Arctic regions. We know that ferns prefer a moist, shady place, and could not live in the sandy plains, but that palms and the cactus flourish in dry, sandy soils. There are just as many kinds of microbes as there are kinds of animals. There are lions and elephants and guinea-pigs and jelly-fish, and, in the microscopic world, there are the specific organisms known as the bacilli of tuberculosis, ef boils and car- buncles, and of typhoid fever, etc. They all have to be studied, because we can then dis- cover what they like and what they do net like, and I need not tell you that we are only too ready to apply to tk• microbes of fell diseasee everything they strongly ob- ject to.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BACILLUS OF CONSUMPTION.

Suppose we were to look down a powerful microscope at a collection of germs, what would they really be like? Of course, when you 6E6 pictures in comic papers of microbes with snapping jaws, oreepy-orawly legs, and flapping wings, that is only fun. They have no such attributes; no, they are the simplest kind of creature you can imagine. The word "bacterium" means a "rod," and the word "bacillus" means a "little rod." These microbes or germs look like short rods; the bacillus of consumption is just like a saimage in shape—no legs or arms, nor eyes and ears—just a sausage. This sausage can grow by lengthening out and then dividing into two, as though someone had tied a string round its middle and pulled it tight. Then those two grow long and divide again, and so on indefinitely.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
—: o: LOCKJAW.

Or take another example, the bacillus of Lockjaw. It does not matter where you cut yourself or how deep you go; if there are no bacilli in the wound you will not get lockjaw; if there are you will. A clean cut between the thumb and first linger will not. give you lockjaw, but a cut on the top of your teed or the sole of your foot contain- ing dirt with the special bacillus in it will certainly give you lockjaw. We can spot this bacillus because it is shaped like a drum-stick; it has a blob at one end, and we know that it likes to grow in mould, especially in the neighbourhood of a farm- yard or stables where horses are kept. This teaches us that a cut into which dirt from the floor of a stable has got should be moat carefully cleansed.

0:- DIRT AND DISEASE.

Now, it 19 all very well for bacteriologists to pore over microscopes, but can you and I learn anything of practical use to us in our everyday life from their studies? Well, I think we can, or I would not have written all this. First of all let us remember that disease microbes are very, very small. An ant would appear an enormous mammoth to a microbe. , If a microbe met an ant walk- ing along, it would be terrified lest one step from the ponderous limb of the ant would crush the microbe to a jelly. Microbes are very small and very light—• light as dust and can float in the air. That is number one fact. The next fact that you must remember is that microbes love dirt ; wherever rubbish heaps, dirty dust-bins, dusty rooms, filthy back-alleys are, there will microbes flourish. Yea, and not only in dirty places but in dirty persons. An un- washed tramp who has never been really clean in his life is a menace to all of us. Has your little boy been ill from some microbic disease? "Well, the microbe; that laid him low came from some dirty person or from some dirty place. We always knew that dirt was bad; but now that we know a little more, now that we can take some of that dirt and look at it under a microscope and find disease germs in it, surely we may make renewed efforts to keep ourselves, our houses and our towns, as clean as possible.

:o A LESSON FROM FRUIT FARMING.

What a glorious vista it. opens up—a united effort of the whole forty millions of wretched eto makethessteativslead out forndose/eau, wanandt of the microbes a place to lodge. When microbes advertise in their papers for dwellings they ask for a suitable dirty residence, with bad drains, very little sunlight, darkness preferred, if possible near an open cesspool, no baths (h. and c.), and no fresh air. So remember, number one fact, they are as light as air; number two fact, they love dirt and dark- ness, for their deeds are evil; and now for the most important fact—number three— they cannot live in healthy bodies. I was reading a book on fruit-terraing the other day and came across the statement that, although there are several ways of fighting the blight that settles on trees, as a matter of fact the only proper method of resisting blight is to keep the trees healthy, and then they will have enough vitality of their own to destroy the blight of themselves. It struck me at once that it would be a grand day for the people of England if only they would learn what the fruit-farmers have discovered—that microbes will not grow on healthy bodies.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
HELP YOURSELVES.

So you see, friends, you must help your. selves; you must not stand idle and say you expect the doctors will invent something some day to rid us of disease. God helps those vrho help themselves; we shall never discover anything that will do better work than soap and water, good food well cooked and properly masticated, fresh air, exercise, and sleep. What we can, and must, do is to help the less fortunate to reach these desir- able ends; and especially must we help the little children. I am only one, but I try to do my microscopic share. Please help me; and I invite your sympathy now as I start out to my school, where I shall find half the children begrimed with dirt and with vermin in their hair. When I die I want this written on my tombstone: "He loved little children and he tried to do them good."

===== 0002601_19120203_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW WE GET CHILLS.

In the autumn, when colds are so common, the clothing that is best suited to avoid chills may well be discussed with advantage. It Must be pointed out that it is not so much the cold weather in itself that is dangerous as the sudden changes from a warm house to a cold street. We could easily stand a low temperature indefinitely if we were in the same temperature all the time, but, no organism eau resist, the heavy strain put upon' it: by this- iapi:4alternation a heat and cold. First the-,eolei hedroom, then the warm breakfast-room then the Geld

walk to the station ano tire minute,* LZ tral • ing about in the draught waiting for the train; then the stuffy journey with ten people breathing the same air over and over again, all the windows being tightly closed; then a cold shivering ride on top of a 'bus and the incarceration in a badly-ventilated, over-heated office—no wonder wia get chills.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
A SONG IN THE MORNING

Everyone hail some plan or fad whereby We may never have colds again as long as we live. My own fad is that everyone who can stand it, and most of us who are in ordinary' health can, should have a cold bath in the . morning. Do not grumble and shiver, just : go and do it. Have a good rub down and i i make your skin glow, and sing at the toe of your voice, because it is good for you.. Stop singing when the neighbours complain. My other recommendation is to have a war= overcoat. When you are sitting in a roons • beautifully warmed by a fire, you do not.. really need those enormously thick under— clothes that you wear. As a matter of fact, , the temperature of the room with a fire 19 )higher than the summer temperature. No, i it s not in the house that you need SO much extra clothing, it is when you go out; it ig wiser to spend money on a warm overcoat ,i than on warm underclothing. The men who' go seeking for the l'oles in frigid tempera- tures do not suffer from colds even when ' they sleep out in the snow.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
ALWAYS THE SAME.

It is -astonishing that so long as we are in health our temperature is always the same, , 1 viz., between 98 and 99 degrees Fahrenheit. That is why the doctor takes your tempera- ture with a clinical thermometer. Unless you are ill, the thermometer ought to. register 98 degrees. It does not matter. 4", whether it is midsummer or midwinter; it 't matters not whether you are in the crickef.? field making: a "century," with the perspiretion streaming down your face, or whether: you are shivering in an east wind watching a football match, your temperature 10 always the same. The nigger sweltering es the line and the Eskimo sitting-, on the North) Pole are both of exactly the same tempera.' ture as registered by the thermometer. ) One's own feelings are deceptive. It is well' known that in a paroxysm of malaria, when - the temperature 13 mounting up and the patient is quite hot, he will shiver till hie.,,: teeth chatter again, and he will draw the bed clothes around him closer and closer to try and keep warm.

===== 0002601_19120203_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
EATING TOO FAST.

Now listen to the words of wisdom; it 15 my turn to talk. You say that Ernest eats a tremendottel- lot, and Yet he remains a° thi° as a rake, and the others are all going the same way. The truth is that they do not ' really get the goodness out of the food you' supply because they eat too fast. It is nO good going to the market yourself, no goo 4 staggering home with the basket, useless t‘!, be the best cook for miles around, of aw: avail to put the food on the table in a* appetising, manner. You do your dutyt mother mine, but no sooner is the food 4:o‘ the plates of your offspring than it has goueij disappeared, bolted, wolfed. They might A well have no teeth at all, fc,r they are never usod.

The sudden death of the Duke of Fife has again plunged the whole of the Royal Family into mourning. The first intimation of the Duke's illness was rpceived from Wady Haifa only a week ago, but later bulletins showed that . the gravity of his condition was causing much anxiety. _.To. the wreck of the Delhi near Tangier must be ascribed the primary cause 0h is illness. It will be remembered that the Royal party were landed under great difficulties, their boat being swamped. The Duke who was a brother-in-law of the King,was sixty-two years of age, and had been married the Princess Royal close upon twenty-three years.
